Chern-Simons invariants of hyperbolic three-manifolds, mixed Tate motives, and motivic path torsor of augmented character varieties
Dong Uk Lee

Abstract
For any complete hyperbolic three-manifold of finite volume, we construct a mixed Tate motive defined over the invariant trace field whose image under Beilinson regulator equals the PSL 2 ​ ( ℂ ) \mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}) -Chern-Simons invariant, thus equals − − 1 -\sqrt{-1} times the complex volume of the manifold. Further, we show that when M M has single torus boundary, under some assumption on asymptotic behaviour of the Chern-Simons invariant near an ideal point, its Hodge realization is a quotient of the mixed Hodge structure on the path torsor of the smooth locus of the canonical curve component of the augmented character variety of the three-manifold between a geometric point (giving the complete hyperbolic structure) and some tangential base point at an ideal point whose existence is asserted by the assumption. We explain its motivic implication. In the appendix, we verify some cases of the assumption. The theory developed here is parallel to the motivic theory of polylogarithms.



1
Introduction

One way of finding the volume of an oriented Riemannian manifold of finite volume is to use, if it exists, a geodesic triangulation, i.e. a triangulation whose constituent simplexes have geodesic faces. In hyperbolic geometry, the volume of any geodesic simplex has algebro-geometric expression as an integral of a rational differential form over an algebraically defined domain. Indeed, in the Klein model
K^{n}
of the
m
-dimensional hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}^{m}
:

(1.0.0.1)

K_{m}=\{(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m},1)\in\mathbb{R}^{m+1}\ :\ Q_{m}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m}):=x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{m}^{2}-1<0\},

geodesic simplexes are the same as “linear” simplexes inside the ball
Q_{m}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{m})\leq 0
, where linearity is taken with respect to the affine structure of
\mathbb{A}^{m+1}_{\mathbb{R}}
, so that every geodesic simplex is determined by a collection of hyperplanes
M=(M_{1},\cdots,M_{m+1})
. It is known that the volume of such simplex is given by the integral
\int_{\Delta_{M}}\omega_{Q}
, where
\Delta_{M}
is a (real)
m
-dimensional simplex in
\mathbb{CP}^{m}
determined by
M
and
\omega_{Q}
is a certain rational differential
m
-form on
\mathbb{CP}^{m}
defined over
\mathbb{Q}
(up to a constant multiple in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
) with poles on the projecctivization of the quadratic
Q_{m}=0
(cf.
[
Gon99
]
). Therefore, when each
M_{i}
is defined over the algebraic numbers
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\subset\mathbb{C}
, this integral is a period, in the sense of Kontsevich-Zagier
[
KZ01
]
(i.e. integral of a rational differential form over a domain defined by algebraic equations or inequalities, all having algebraic numbers as coefficients), and so we see that the volume of any complete hyperbolic manifold of finite volume, as it is known to have a geodesic triangulation defined over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
, is a sum of periods, or, more suggestively, is an element of the ring of periods.

Now, assume that the dimension
m=2n-1
is odd. Then, Goncharov
[
Gon99
]
showed that the volumes of complete hyperbolic manifolds of finite volume as well as hyperbolic geodesic simplexes are all certain
canonically defined
periods of some special kind of mixed Hodge structures (which are in fact motivic). First, he showed that the volume of a hyperbolic geodesic simplex is the
real period
, a novel notion which he introduced in
ibid.
, of the mixed
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge structure

(1.0.0.2)

H^{m}(\mathbb{CP}^{m}\backslash Q_{m},\cup M_{i}\backslash Q_{m},\mathbb{Q})

(the relative cohomology group of a pair of algebraic variety and closed subvariety), provided with
\Delta_{M}
(a relative cycle in
H_{m}(\mathbb{CP}^{m},M_{1}\cup\cdots\cup M_{m})
) and
\omega_{Q}
(an element of
H^{m}(\mathbb{CP}^{m}\backslash Q_{m},\mathbb{Q})
) (cf.
[
Gon99
,
\lx@sectionsign
1.5]
).
The key point is that this mixed
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge structure
H
is a
Hodge-Tate
structure, i.e. its associated weight graded’s
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{l}H
satisfy that for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2k+1}H=0
and
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2k}H\simeq\mathbb{Q}(-k)^{\oplus d_{k}}
for some
d_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, and that
\Delta_{M}
and
\omega_{Q}
form a
framing
of
H
, i.e. are elements of
(\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{0}H)^{\vee}
(dual space) and
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2n}H
in a natural manner; such triple
(H,\Delta_{M},\omega_{Q})
is called (
n
-)
framed Hodge-Tate structure
.

Let
\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}
denote the abelian category of
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge-Tate structures and for a number field
F\subset\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
,
\mathrm{MT}(F)
the abelian category of mixed Tate motives over
F

[
Lev93
]
, and
R^{\mathrm{Bo}}:K_{2n-1}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow K_{2n-1}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the Borel regulator map from higher algebraic K-theory to
\mathbb{R}
(cf.
[
Bur02
]
). Via the canonical isomorphism

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))=K_{2n-1}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})\otimes\mathbb{Q}

(cf.
[
Lev98
]
), where for an abelian category
\mathcal{C}
,
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(-,-)
is the Yoneda extension group,

R^{\mathrm{Bo}}
is identified with
twice
the “real/imaginary part” of the canonical period map:

(1.0.0.3)

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{real}^{H}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))\cong\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(n)\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{n}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ \mathbb{R}(n-1)\cong\mathbb{R}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\times 2}}{{\rightarrow}}\ \mathbb{R},

where
\mathrm{real}^{H}
is the Hodge realization functor,
p_{n}
is the projection onto the real/imaginary part (for a subring
R\subset\mathbb{C}
,
R(n):=(2\pi i)^{n}R\subset\mathbb{C}
;
\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{R}(n)\oplus\mathbb{R}(n-1)
), and
\mathbb{R}(n-1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathbb{R}
is the map
i^{n-1}r\mapsto r
. Using the above interpretation of the volume of geodesic simplex as a real period, Goncharov showed the following remarkable fact:

Theorem 1
.

[
Gon99
, Thm.1.1]

With every hyperbolic manifold of
odd
dimension
m=2n-1
, there exists a mixed Tate motive defined over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\subset\mathbb{C}
which is a simple extension of
\mathbb{Q}(0)
by
\mathbb{Q}(n)
in the abelian category
\mathrm{MT}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
of mixed Tate motives over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
and whose image under the Borel regulator equals the volume.

This highly nontrivial result is a much more precise statement than that the volume is a sum of some periods, and suggests the possibility of understanding invariants of odd-dimensional hyperbolic manifolds in terms of mixed Tate motives over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
(equiv. of elements of algebraic K-groups of
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
) and their periods.

Now, we specialize to the dimension
m=3
. In this case, there is another important differential-geometric invariant, that is, the (metric) Chern-Simons invariant. For a
closed
(i.e. compact without boundary) oriented Riemannian
3
-manifold
M
, the metric
Chern-Simons invariant

cs(M)
was first introduced by Chern and Simons
[
CS74
]
as the integral of a certain real
C^{\infty}

3
-form
Q_{M}
(
Chern-Simons
3
-form
) on the oriented orthonormal frame bundle
F(M)
along a frame field
s
(i.e. a section
s:M\rightarrow F(M)
), more explicitly:

(1.0.0.4)

cs(M):=\int_{s(M)}Q_{M}:=-\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{s(M)}\mathrm{Tr}(\omega\wedge d\omega+\frac{2}{3}\omega\wedge\omega\wedge\wedge\omega),

where
\omega\in\mathcal{A}^{1}(F(M),\mathfrak{so}(3))
is the Levi-Civita connection on
F(M)
.
For
3
-manifolds with non-empty boundary, Meyerhoff in his thesis
[
Mey86
]
extended the definition of metric Chern-Simons invariant to complete hyperbolic three-manifolds
M
having cusps:

(1.0.0.5)

cs(M):=\int_{s(M-L)}Q_{M}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{K\in L}\tau(K).

Here,
L
is some link in
M
such that there exists a frame field
s
defined on the complement
M-L
which has certain special singularity along
L
and also linearity near cusps. For each component
K
of
L
,
\tau(K)
is the
complex torsion
of that curve.
Both integrals, as being valued in
\mathbb{R}/2\mathbb{Z}
in the closed case or in
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
in the cusped case, are known to be well-defined independent of the choices made (of
s
,
L
), that is
cs(M)
is an invariant of
M
.

Now, one can ask the same question whether this invariant is also motivic, especially is another canonically defined period of a mixed Tate motive, preferably the motive constructed by Goncharov. One may attempt to adapt Goncharov’s original argument for volume computation which is to add local contributions over constituent geodesic simplexes. For this to work, it is necessary that the local contribution is some canonically defined period of some (mixed Tate) Hodge structure which is defined by the geodesic simplex only. But one faces difficulties. First, in the closed case,
cs(M)
is the integral over
M
of a
3
-form
s^{\ast}Q_{M}
on
M
which itself depends on the choice of the section
s
, and it is not even clear in the first place whether there exists
s
such that the restriction of
s^{\ast}Q_{M}
to every constituent geodesic simplex becomes a
rational
differential form. In the cusped case, the local contribution of each geodesic simplex to (
2.1.5.1
) is not even an integral of some
3
-form over the simplex, much less depends on
L
as well as
s
. So a naive guess at answer is “unlikely”.

On the other hand, according to Thurston, the Riemannian volume
\mathrm{Vol}(M)
and the metric Chern-Simons invariant
cs(M)
should be considered together, as a single
\mathbb{C}/\sqrt{-1}\pi^{2}\mathbb{Z}
-valued invariant:

\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)=\mathrm{Vol}(M)+\sqrt{-1}\pi^{2}cs(M),

called
complex volume
. It has better properties, such as analyticity on deformation space, and arises naturally in the theory of hyperbolic three-manifolds and especially in quantum topology.
Neumann and Yang
[
Neu92
]
constructed an invariant
\beta(M)
living in the Bloch group
\mathcal{B}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
and Neumann
[
NY99
]
proved that the complex volume of a hyperbolic three-manifold
M
equals, up to a constant, the value at
\beta(M)
of the Bloch regulator
\tilde{\rho}(z)
introduced by Dupont and Sah.
Also, Kirk and Klassen
[
KK93
]
defined the
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Chern-Simons invariant for any (closed or not) hyperbolic three-manifold and showed that it equals the complex volume, up to a constant (
2.2.8.1
).

We recall the Beilinson regulator
K_{n}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow K_{n}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(n)
(
4.1.0.8
) which is identified with the Hodge realization

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))\cong\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(n)
via the canonical isomorphism
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))=K_{2n-1}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
.

Our first main result is the following:

Theorem 2
.

(Theorem
4.2.4
)
For any hyperbolic three-manifold
X
, there exists a mixed Tate motive
M(X)
in
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(k(M))}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(2))
defined over the invariant trace field
k(M)\subset\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
whose image under the Beilinson regulator equals the (normalized)
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Chern-Simons invariant
\mathrm{CS}_{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}}(X)
.

Since the Borel regulator
K_{3}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is twice the imaginary part of the Beilinson regulator (
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(2)=\mathbb{R}/\pi^{2}\mathbb{Q}\oplus\sqrt{-1}\mathbb{R}
), this result improves upon the Goncharov’ result (Theorem
1
).

Our definition of the mixed Tate motive in Theorem A depends on the choice of a representative of the Bloch invariant
\beta(M)
, and at the moment we do not have a proof that this mixed Tate motive is independent of such choice, although it should be so according to some general motivic conjectures (cf. Remark
4.2.5
). Nevertheless, in this work, since there is no need for clarifying which one is being used, we call any mixed Tate motive as in Theorem 2,
Chern-Simons mixed Tate motive
.

Now, there arises the natural question whether our Chern-Simons mixed Tate motive is the same, up to a constant multiple, as the Goncharov’s mixed Tate motive (if we know that our motive is well-defined). The answer is “presumably yes” and “probably no”. In fact, Goncharov constructed one element for each of the two groups
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(2))=K_{3}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
and raised the question of equality of these two elements. His first homological method constructs an element of
H_{3}(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}),\mathbb{Q})\cong K_{3}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})_{\mathbb{Q}}
. We believe that this element should be the same as our mixed Tate motive. On the other hand, his second method produces a certain sum of mixed Tate motives which turns out to be an extension of
\mathbb{Q}(0)
by
\mathbb{Q}(2)
(like our mixed Tate motive). But, we observe that because the input for his construction of mixed Tate motive is an element in the scissors congruence group
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
, there is no guarantee that his mixed Tate motive detects the Chern-Simons invariant; in contrast, the Bloch group
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C})
which was used for our construction is finer than
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
. So there is no guarantee that his (second) mixed Tate motive is the same as our mixed Tate motive or with his (first) K-theory element. We will give more detailed explanation in Subsection
4.3
.

Our second main result relates this “Chern-Simons mixed Tate motive” to the mixed Hodge structure defined on the path torsor of the agumented character variety of the hyperbolic manifold between two some specific base points.

Theorem 3
.

(Theorem
7.5.3
)
Suppose
M
has a single cusp. Let
X
be the smooth open part of the geometric (curve) component of the augemented character variety
\tilde{X}(M)
; choose a point
z^{0}\in X
giving the complete hyperbolic structure. Assume Conjecture
7.4.2
; let
\overrightarrow{v}
be any tangent vector satisfying the condition of this conjecture.

Then, the Chern-Simons Hodge-Tate structure constructed in Theorem 2 is a quotient of the completed path torsor
\mathbb{Q}[P_{\overrightarrow{v},z^{0}}X]^{\wedge}
.

This theorem is an analogue of a well-known result about the polylogarithm variation of mixed Hodge structure on the projective line minus three points
\mathbb{P}^{1}\backslash\{0,1,\infty\}

[
Hai94
, 11.3]
. In fact, our proof follows the Deligne’s original argument faithfully, and our Conjecture
7.4.2
is one of the ingredients needed to mimic his argument. The most essential ingredient in the original argument is computation of local monodromies of the local system underlying the polylogarithm VMHS around the two ideal points
0
,
1
(which is reduced to (known) monodromy computations of polylogarithm functions
[
Ram82
]
); the statements corresponding to our Conjecture
7.4.2
and our Theorem 1 (i.e.
[
Hai94
]
, Thm.7.2, Thm.11.3) then follow easily from the local and global monodromy informations, respectively. But, in our case, to obtain necessary informations on these monodromies, we need some non-trivial results in three-dimensional topology (about Culler-Shalen theory and A-polynomials, cf.
[
CS83
]
,
[
CS84
]
,
[
CCGLS94
]
).

This result strongly suggests (an observation due to Deligne in the polylogarithm case) that the Chern-Simons mixed Tate motive constructed in Theorem 2 is also a quotient of the mixed motive
A_{\overrightarrow{v},z^{0}}(X)
whose dual is the affine algebra of the motivic path torsor with (tangential) base points
\overrightarrow{v}
,
z^{0}
; the motivic path torsor
P^{\mathrm{mot}}_{\overrightarrow{v},z^{0}}(X)
is a scheme
\mathrm{Spec}(A_{\overrightarrow{v},z^{0}}(X)^{\vee})
in the (hypothetical) Tannakian category of mixed motives, in the sense of Deligne
[
Del89
]
, having the Betti realization
\mathrm{real}^{Betti}(A_{\overrightarrow{v},z^{0}}(X))=(\mathbb{Q}[P_{\overrightarrow{v},z^{0}}X]^{\wedge})^{\vee}
. The main obstacle for turning this expectation into a rigorous statement is that at the moment, for non-rational curves, the motivic path torsor is a hypothetical object (due to lack of motivic
t
-structure and difficulty with tangential base points), although its existence is a standard belief. As a matter of fact, we obtained Theorem 3 before Theorem 2. The motivic implication of Theorem 3 led us to look for a mixed Tate motive with the property in Theorem 2.

We also bring the readers’ attention to that in view of that the (augmented) character variety is defined only by the topological fundamental group of the three-manifold, this theorem is a vivid example of Mostow rigidity theorem, which says that the differential-geometric invariants of complete hyperbolic three-manifolds are also homotopy invariants.

This article is organized as follows.

Section 2 is devoted to a review of three invariants attached to arbitrary (i.e. closed or not) complete hyperbolic three-manifolds
M
of finite volume and their relations:
(i) the complex volume
\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
, (ii) the Bloch invariant
\beta(M)
and its Bloch regulator value, and (iii) the
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
Chern-Simons invariant
\mathrm{CS}_{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}}(M)
.
The main result is that the Bloch regulator value at the Bloch invariant
\beta(M)
of
M
equals half the
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
Chern-Simons invariant of
M
.

An essential tool in the Goncharov’s work
[
Gon99
]
is the novel notion of
big period
which is defined for framed
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge-Tate structures and is valued in the “big” group
\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}
. In fact, for his purpose of constructing a mixed Tate motive giving the Riemannian volume of hyperbolic manifold as Borel regulator value, Goncharov uses only the image
P_{2}
of the big period under the map
x\otimes y\mapsto 2\pi i\cdot x\otimes\exp(2\pi i\cdot y)
, which thus loses some information. The aforementioned real period of a framed Hodge-Tate structure is derived from this simplified big period
P_{2}
. In Section 3, we give a review of the theory of big period and also introduce a new notion of “skew-symmetric period”. Our key observation leading to Theorem
4.2.4
is that if for
z\in\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\backslash\{0,1,\infty\}
, we let
\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(z)
denote the polylogarithm framed Hodge-Tate structure of rank
3
(
3.3.0.1
), the skew-symmetrization of the bid period (valued in
\mathbb{C}\wedge_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}
) of
\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(z)
equals the Bloch regulator value
\tilde{\rho}(z)
, cf. (
3.3.0.5
).
In the last subsection, we recall the description, due to
[
BMS87
]
,
[
BGSV90
]
, of the Tannakian category of
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge-Tate structures in terms of framed
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge-Tate structures and explain the formula
\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))=\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\nu}|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}})
, the right side being the kernel of the reduced comultiplication
\bar{\nu}
on the associated fundamental Hopf algebra
\mathcal{H}=\oplus\mathcal{H}_{n}
, called “Hodge-Tate Hopf algebra” (Proposition
3.4.7
).

In Section 4, we start with a brief survey of the theory of mixed Tate motives over number fields, and
prove our first main theorem: Theorem
4.2.4
, constructing (the) Chern-Simons mixed Tate motive as a certain sum of various polylogarithm mixed Tate motives. Its ingredient is a representative of the Bloch invariant
\beta(M)
. We give a comparison of this mixed Tate motive with Goncharov’s mixed Tate motive (his second construction). The three sections 2, 3, 4 constitute the first part of this article.

Our second main theorem, in its statement and proof as well, has a model in the theory of polylogarithm sheaves on the projective line minus three points
\mathbb{P}^{1}\backslash\{0,1,\infty\}
. For motivation, we recall this theory in Section 5.

In Section 6, we review the theory of character varieties of three-manifolds and Thurston deformation curves in the single torus boundary case. For our purpose, the more useful objects is a certain double covering of the canonical (curve) component of the character variety of
M
, called
augmented character variety
.
For each hyperbolic three-manifold
M
with single torus boundary, following a previous construction of Morishita-Terashima
[
MT09
]
, we construct a variation of mixed Hodge structure (which we call Chern-Simons VMHS) over the smooth locus of the canonical component of the augmented character variety. The primary ingredient for this construction is the Chern-Simons invariants of flat connections on the trivial principal
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-bundle on
M
; here the Chern-Simons invariant is regarded as a section of certain line bundle over the character variety of the boundary torus
\partial M
, the idea going back to Kirk and Klassen
[
KK93
]
. We related their construction with that of Morishita-Terashima.

In Section 7, we prove our second main theorem: Theorem
7.5.3
. As discussed briefly earlier, we follow the arguments in the polylogarithm theory. But, the main ingredients are provided by three-manifold topology theory.

In appendix, we verify Conjecture
7.4.2
for the figure eight knot complement.

2
Chern-Simons invariant of hyperbolic three-manifolds and Bloch regulator

2.1
\mathrm{SO}(3)
Chern-Simons invariant of hyperbolic
3
-manifolds

Here, we give a review of the theory of metric Chern-Simons invariant of hyperbolic three-manifolds, focusing on the cusped case. Our main references are
[
CS74
]
,
[
Mey86
]
, and
[
Yos85
]
.

For our work, we also need constructions of general Chern-Simons invariants of connections on principal bundles for various Lie groups (the only Lie groups which we will deal with in this work are
\mathrm{SO}(3)
,
\mathrm{SU}(2)
,
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
,
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
).

Definition 2.1.1
.

Let
G
be a (real or complex) Lie group with finitely many components. Suppose fixed an
\mathrm{Ad}
-invariant symmetric bilinear form on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}

\langle-,-\rangle\,:\,\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}.

(1) Let
P
be a principal
G
-bundle on a three-manifold
M
.
The
Chern-Simons
3
-form
of a connection
\omega\in\mathcal{A}^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})
on
P
is the closed
3
-form

Q(\omega):=\langle\omega\wedge d\omega+\frac{1}{3}\omega\wedge[\omega\wedge\omega]\rangle.

(recall that
\langle-,-\rangle:\mathfrak{g}\times\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
(resp. the Lie bracket
[-,-]
) extends to
\mathfrak{g}
-valued differential forms on any smooth manifold
X
:
\langle-,-\rangle:\mathcal{A}^{k}(X,\mathfrak{g})\times\mathcal{A}^{l}(X,\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{k+l}(X,\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{k+l}(X,\mathbb{C})
(resp.
[-,-]:\mathcal{A}^{k}(X,\mathfrak{g})\times\mathcal{A}^{l}(X,\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{k+l}(X,\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{g})\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{k+l}(X,\mathfrak{g})
);
[-\wedge-]
is another notation for
[-,-]
.)

(2) For an oriented Riemannian three-manifold
M
, the
metric Chern-Simons
3
-form

Q_{M}
is the Chern-Simons
3
-form of the Levi-Civita connection
A_{\mathrm{L.C.}}\in\mathcal{A}^{1}(F(M),\mathfrak{so}_{3})
on the oriented orthonormal frame bundle
F(M)

Q_{M}:=Q(\omega_{\mathrm{L.C.}})=-\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}(\omega_{\mathrm{L.C.}}\wedge d\omega_{\mathrm{L.C.}}+\frac{2}{3}\omega_{\mathrm{L.C.}}\wedge\omega_{\mathrm{L.C.}}\wedge\wedge\omega_{\mathrm{L.C.}})

for the choice
\langle-,-\rangle\>=-\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}
, where
\mathrm{Tr}:\mathfrak{so}(3)\times\mathfrak{so}(3)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the restriction to
\mathfrak{so}(3)
of the trace function on
M_{3}(\mathbb{R})
(we used the identity:
\omega\wedge\omega=2[\omega\wedge\omega]
).

The choice of
\langle-,-\rangle\>=-\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}
in (2) will be explained in Remark
2.1.7
.

Now, we specialize to metric Chern-Simons invariant of cusped hyperbolic three-manifolds.
Let
M
be an oriented, hyperbolic three-manifold of finite volume with cusps and
F(M)\rightarrow M
its oriented orthonormal frame bundle (so, a principal
\mathrm{SO}(3)
-bundle).

We have to choose the frame field on
M
over which the Chern-Simons
3
-form
Q_{M}
is integrated, cf. (
1.0.0.4
). One wishes to use a frame field which is “linear on the cusps”.

Definition 2.1.2
.

[
Mey86
,
\lx@sectionsign
4.1]

Suppose that a cusp of
M
correspond to the point at infinity of the Poincare upper-half space model
\mathbb{H}^{3}
with the coordinate
(x,y,t)\ (t>0)
. A
linear frame field on a cusp
is given by

e_{1}=t\partial/\partial t,\ e_{2}=t[\cos(r)\partial/\partial x+\sin(r)\partial/\partial y],\ e_{e}=t[-\sin(r)\partial/\partial x+\cos(r)\partial/\partial y]

where
r
is a constant.

(See also
[
Yos85
, p.486]
.)

But, there does not always exist such a frame field: one might want to start with a frame field defined linearly in a neighborhood of cusps and attempt to extend it to the entire manifold. But this is not always possible: there exists an obstruction to such extension, a cohomology class in
H^{1}(M,\partial M;\pi_{1}(G))
, cf.
[
Ste99
,
\lx@sectionsign
34.2]
. In trying to extend a given local frame field, one typically ends up meeting singularities along some link
L
. Meyerhoff’s key idea for defining the metric Chern-Simons invariant in the cusped case is to require for the section
s:M-L\rightarrow F(M)
thus obtained to have only special kind of singularities at
L
, and to compensate the integral
\int_{s(M-L)}Q_{M}
by the torsion of
L
.

Definition 2.1.3
.

[
Mey86
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.1]
,
[
Yos85
, Def.1.3]

A
special singular frame field
on
M
is an orthonormal frame field on
M-L
for some link
L
which has the following behaviour at each component
K
in
L
:

i) (in the limit)
e_{1}
is tangent to
K
.

ii)
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
determine a singularity transverse to
K
of index
1
or
-1
.

Definition 2.1.4
.

Let
M
be an oriented Riemannian three-manifold. Let
(\theta_{ij})\in\mathcal{A}^{1}(F(M),\mathfrak{so}_{3})
be the Levi-Civita connection
1
-form defined by an orthonormal frame field (i.e.
(\theta_{ij})
is a matrix of
1
-forms on
F(M)
such that
\theta_{ji}=-\theta_{ij}
and
d\theta^{i}=-\theta_{ij}\wedge\theta^{j}
for the dual coframe field
(\theta^{i})
). Then, the
torsion

\tau(L)
of a link
L
in
M
is defined by

\tau(L):=-\int_{s(L)}\theta_{23}\mod 2\pi\mathbb{Z}

where
s:L\rightarrow F(M)
is the section given by some orthonormal framing
\alpha:=(e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})
defined on a subset of
M
containing
L
such that
e_{1}(y)
is tangent to
L
at each
y\in L
and the orientation of
L
is given by
e_{1}
.

It is known
[
Yos85
, Lem.1.1]
that this integral is well-defined modulo
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
, independent of the choice of the orthonormal framing
\alpha
.

Now, we present the Meyerhoff’s definition of metric Chern-Simons invariant for complete hyperbolic three-manifolds of finite volume, either closed or not.

Theorem 2.1.5
.

(1) For every hyperbolic manifold
M
, closed or not, there exist a (possibly empty) link
L
in
M
and an oriented orthonormal framing
s
on
M-L
that has special singularity at
L
and is linear on cusps.
When
M
is closed, one can take
L=\emptyset
, i.e. there exists a framing
s
defined on the entire
M
.

(2) For any link
L
and a framing
s
on
M-L
as in (1), the number (mod
\mathbb{Z}
)

(2.1.5.1)

cs(M):=\int_{s(M-L)}Q_{M}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\sum_{K\in L}\tau(K)\quad(\mathrm{mod}\mathbb{Z})

is independent of the choice of the datum
(L,s)
, and if
M
is closed and
L=\emptyset
, this is even well-defined mod
2\mathbb{Z}
;
by definition, this is the metric (or
\mathrm{SO}(3)
) Chern-Simons invariant of the hyperbolic three manifold
M
.

Remark 2.1.6
.

At this point, it seems worthwhile to clarify different conventions, normalizations, and notations appearing in literatures, centering around Chern and Simons
[
CS74
]
, Meyerhoff
[
Mey86
]
, Yoshida
[
Yos85
]
, Neumann
[
Neu92
]
, and Kirk and Klassen
[
KK93
]
, and to compare them with our own choice.
1
1

1

Often, such differences among different people, especially in the definition/normalization of various CS-invariants, cause confusions and incorrect details in statements.

(i) Yoshida
[
Yos85
, Def.1.1]
and Meyerhoff
[
Mey86
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.2]
use opposite signs for the definition of torsion of curves. We chose Yoshida’s sign, thus have minus sign in Definition
2.1.4
) and accordingly in front of the torsion term in (
2.1.5.1
).

(ii) For the metric Chern-Simons
3
-form, which we denoted by
Q_{M}
, Yoshida uses the letter
Q

[
Yos85
, l.-6 on p.480]
, while Meyerhoff uses the same letter
Q
to mean
4\pi^{2}Q_{M}

[
Mey86
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.2]
.

(iii) The metric Chern-Simons invariants defined by Yoshida
[
Yos85
, l.13 on p.491]
and Meyerhoff
[
Mey86
, Theorem in
\lx@sectionsign
1]
, with the same notation
CS(M)
(capital letters, oblique style), are the same, and is equal to
half
our metric CS-invariant
cs(M)
(
2.1.5.1
); so, for cusped hyperbolic three-manifolds, their CS-invariants are well-defined modulo
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
.
In the case of closed three-manifolds, their definitions are also the same as the original definition of Chern and Simons
[
CS74
, (6.2)]
, and thus are well-defined even modulo
\mathbb{Z}
(see Remark
2.1.7
, (2)).

(iv) Neumann (
[
Neu92
]
,
[
Neu98
]
,
[
NY99
]
) also adopts the original definition of metric Chern-Simon invariant. But, in these works he almost exclusively works with a normalized metric Chern-Simon invariant which he denotes by
\mathrm{CS}(M)
(capital letters, upright style). We will also work with a normalized metric Chern-Simon invariant with the same notation
\mathrm{CS}(M)
: of course, they will have the same meanings (see Footnote
2
).

(v) Kirk and Klassen’s definition of metric Chern-Simons invariant is the same as ours. But, for
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Chern-Simons invariant, they use the first Pontryagin polynomial
P_{1}
, while our definition (to be given later) uses the second Chern polynomial
C_{2}
(cf.
[
KK93
, p.555, line17]
, Remark
2.1.7
, (3)).

Proof.

(1) First, the case when
M
is closed follows from the well-known fact (
[
Kir89
, VII.Thm.1]
) that every orientable three-manifold is parallellizable and the Gram-Schmidt orthonormalization. In the general case, this is proved in
[
Mey86
,
\lx@sectionsign
4.2]
. A stronger statement is established in
[
Yos85
, Prop.3.1]
.

(2) This is proved in Section 3.2 to 3.4 of
[
Mey86
]
, and a different proof is given in
[
Yos85
, Cor.1.1]
.
The mod-
\mathbb{Z}
(rather than mod-
2\mathbb{Z}
) independence in the presence of the link
L
comes from the fact that for two different links
L_{0}
,
L_{1}
which are moved by a homotopy, the change in their Chern-Simons integrals
\int_{s(M-L_{i})}Q_{M}
is given by
\frac{1}{2\pi}(\tau(L_{0})-\tau(L_{1}))
, while
\tau(L_{i})
is well-defined modulo
2\pi\mathbb{Z}
(rather than
4\pi\mathbb{Z}
), see the discussion of
\lx@sectionsign
3.2 of
[
Mey86
]
. When
M
is closed and
L=\emptyset
, the Chern-Simons integral is well-defined even modulo
2\mathbb{Z}
, independent of the choice of
s
(see Remark
2.1.7
, (2)).
∎

Remark 2.1.7
.

(1) The choice of a specific bilinear form
\langle-,-\rangle
on
\mathfrak{g}
in Definition
2.1.1
, (1) is determined by the requirement that for
closed
three-manifolds
M
, the Chern-Simons integral
\int_{s(M)}Q(\omega)
is a
mod
\mathbb{Z}
-well-defined invariant
cs(P,A)
of a principal
G
-bundle
P
over
M
endowed with a connection
\omega
(i.e. the integral is independent of the choice of a section
s:M\rightarrow P
). This condition is reduced to the one that

(
\dagger
)
for the Maurer-Cartan form
\omega_{\mathrm{M}.\mathrm{C}.}
on
G
, the closed
3
-form
-\frac{1}{6}\langle\omega_{\mathrm{M}.\mathrm{C}.}\wedge[\omega_{\mathrm{M}.\mathrm{C}.}\wedge\omega_{\mathrm{M}.\mathrm{C}.}]\rangle
represents an integral class in
H^{3}(G,\mathbb{R})
(“Hypothesis 2.5.” in
[
Fre95
]
).

This can be seen, for instance, from the transformation formula of
Q(\omega)
under gauge transformation (
[
Fre95
, Prop.2.3]
). We follow the original discussion of Chern and Simons
[
CS74
]
(See also
[
DW90
,
\lx@sectionsign
4.2-4.3]
), to find the bilinear forms
\langle-,-\rangle
satisfying (
\dagger
).

For a real Lie group
G
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, let
I^{k}(G)=S^{k}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})^{G}
, the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space of
\mathbb{R}
-valued degree-
k
polynomial functions on
\mathfrak{g}
which are invariant under the adjoint action of
G
, and set
I^{\ast}(G):=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}I^{k}(G)
; for a complex Lie group
G
, we use the same notation
I^{k}(G)
for the
\mathbb{C}
-space of similarly defined
\mathbb{C}
-valued functions on
\mathfrak{g}
. We recall the (universal) Chern-Weil homomorphism
W:I^{k}(G)\rightarrow H^{2k}(BG,\mathbb{F})
which sends
P\in I^{k}(G)
to the cohomology class
[P(F)]
of the closed
2k
-form
P(F)
, where
F
is the curvature of an
arbitrary
connection on the universal principal
G
-bundle
EG
over the classifying space
BG
, and
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
according as
G
is real or complex; it is a fundamental fact that this map is well-defined, i.e. the cohomology class
[P(F)]
is independent of the choice of the connection, and further is an algebra isomorphism either if
G
is a connected compact real Lie group or if
G
is a connected reductive complex Lie group
[
Bur02
, 5.23,5.30]
.
A key ingredient in the work of Chern and Simons is the
universal suspension map

[
CS74
, p.55]
(cf.
[
Bor55
,
\lx@sectionsign
9]
):

\displaystyle I^{k}(G)\cong H^{2k}(BG,\mathbb{F})\rightarrow

\displaystyle\ H^{2k-1}(G,\mathbb{F})\ ;

\displaystyle[P]\qquad\mapsto

\displaystyle\ [TP(\omega_{\mathrm{M}.\mathrm{C}.}):=\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{2^{k-1}{2k-1\choose k}}P(\omega_{\mathrm{M}.\mathrm{C}.}\wedge[\omega_{\mathrm{M}.\mathrm{C}.},\omega_{\mathrm{M}.\mathrm{C}.}]^{k-1})]

(
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
); the cohomology classes in the image are called
universally transgressive
. This suspension map preserves the integral subspaces:

I^{k}_{0}(G)\cong H^{2k}(BG,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{2k-1}(G,\mathbb{Z}),

where
I^{k}_{0}(G):=\{P\in I^{k}(G)\ |\ W(P)\in H^{2k}(BG,\mathbb{Z})\}
(
[
CS74
, Prop.3.15]
).

Now, the
3
-form in the condition (
\dagger
) is
TP(\omega_{\mathrm{M}.\mathrm{C}.})
for
P=\langle-,-\rangle
(
k=2
): for any
1
-form
\theta
,

-\frac{1}{6}\langle\theta\wedge[\theta\wedge\theta]\rangle=TP(\theta);

also, when the curvature
F=d\theta+\frac{1}{2}[\theta\wedge\theta]
is zero, we have
TP(\theta)=\langle\theta\wedge d\theta+\frac{1}{3}\theta\wedge[\theta\wedge\theta]\rangle
, i.e.
TP(\theta)
is the Chern-Simons form
Q(\theta)
.

Therefore, in order for (
\dagger
) to hold, it suffices that
\langle-,-\rangle\in I^{2}(G)
lies in
I^{2}_{0}(G)
.
For example, for
G=\mathrm{SO}(3)
, it is known that
I^{2}_{0}(\mathrm{SO}(3))=H^{3}(B\mathrm{SO}(3),\mathbb{Z})
is generated by the first Pontryagin polynomial
P_{1}(X)=-\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\mathrm{tr}(X^{2})
(here, the product
X^{2}
and
\mathrm{tr}(-)
is defined via the embedding
X\in\mathfrak{so}(3)\subset M_{3\times 3}(\mathbb{R})
) and the Stiefel-Whitney class (which is a
2
-torsion)
[
Bro82
, 1.5]
.
For
G=\mathrm{SU}(2)
,
I^{2}_{0}(\mathrm{SU}(2))=H^{3}(B\mathrm{SU}(2),\mathbb{Z})
is generated by the second Chern polynomial
C_{2}(X)=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\mathrm{tr}(X^{2})
(here, the product
X^{2}
and
\mathrm{tr}(-)
is defined via
\mathfrak{su}(2)\hookrightarrow M_{2\times 2}(\mathbb{C})
)
[
MT91
, Cor.5.7]
.

(2) The integral universal suspension map
H^{2k}(BG,\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow H^{2k-1}(G,\mathbb{Z})
is not surjective for general
G
, especially for
\mathrm{SO}(3)
. In this case, for
\langle-,-\rangle
, Chern and Simons take
\frac{1}{2}P_{1}
which lies in
H^{3}(G,\mathbb{Z})
,
[
CS74
, 5.13,
\lx@sectionsign
6]
. So, their original definition
[
CS74
, (6.2)]
of CS-invariant is half our metric CS-invariant (
1.0.0.4
), which is still well-defined mod
\mathbb{Z}
for closed manifolds.

(3) There exists a double covering map
\sigma:\mathrm{SU}(2)\rightarrow\mathrm{SO}(3)
with kernel the center
Z(\mathrm{SU}(2))(\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
. Since
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})=\mathrm{SU}(2)_{\mathbb{C}}
(complexification of
\mathrm{SU}(2)
) and
Z(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))=Z(\mathrm{SU}(2))
, we also have
\mathrm{SO}(3)_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, and there are commutative diagrams

(2.1.7.1)

\textstyle{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{su}(2)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{so}(3)\ ,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{I^{\ast}(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I^{\ast}(\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))}
\textstyle{I^{\ast}(\mathrm{SU}(2))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I^{\ast}(\mathrm{SO}(3))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{\ast}}

It is known that
\sigma^{\ast}:I^{2}_{0}(\mathrm{SO}(3))=H^{4}(B\mathrm{SO}(3),\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow I^{2}_{0}(\mathrm{SU}(2))=H^{4}(B\mathrm{SU}(2),\mathbb{Z})
sends the first Pontryagin polynomial
P_{1}=-\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\mathrm{tr}(X^{2})_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}
to the minus four times the second Chern polynomial
-4C_{2}=-\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\mathrm{tr}(X^{2})_{\mathfrak{su}(2)}
. Indeed, one has
4\mathrm{tr}(XY)_{\mathfrak{sl}_{2,\mathbb{C}}}|_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}=\mathrm{tr}(XY)_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}
(the trace bilinear form
\mathrm{tr}(XY)_{\mathfrak{su}(2)}
on
\mathfrak{su}(2)
is the restriction of the trace form
\mathrm{tr}(XY)_{\mathfrak{sl}_{2,\mathbb{C}}}
on
\mathfrak{sl}_{2,\mathbb{C}}
, which, when restricted to
\mathfrak{so}(3)
, equals one fourth of the trace form
\mathrm{tr}(XY)_{\mathfrak{so}(3)}
on
\mathfrak{so}(3)\subset\mathfrak{sl}_{3}
). This is also a statement equating the Killing forms on
\mathfrak{so}(3)
and
\mathfrak{su}(2)
via the isomorphism
\sigma^{\ast}
.

2.2  Complex volume of hyperbolic
3
-manifolds as
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
Chern-Simons invariant

It has been proved to be quite useful to treat two most important invariants of complete hyperbolic three-manifold, Riemannian volume and metric Chern-Simons invariant, together as the real part and the imaginary part of a single complex-number (modulo
\sqrt{-1}\mathbb{Z}
). In this section, we explain how the complex volume
\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
of
any
(closed or not) complete hyperbolic three-manifold
M
of finite volume can be viewed as the Chern-Simons invariant of some connection on a (triviallizable)
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-principal bundle over
M
. We discuss the case that
M
is closed first, following Yoshida
[
Yos85
]
, and then treat the cusped case, where the definition of
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
Chern-Simons invariant is not even clear and is due to Kirk and Klassen
[
KK93
]
.

Definition 2.2.1
.

The
complex volume
of a complete hyperbolic three-manifold is defined to be

\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)=\mathrm{Vol}(M)+i\mathrm{CS}(M)\in\ \mathbb{C}/2\pi^{2}i\mathbb{Z}\text{ or }\mathbb{C}/\pi^{2}i\mathbb{Z},

where
\mathrm{CS}(M):=\pi^{2}cs(M)
.
2
2

2

This normalized metric CS-invariant
\mathrm{CS}(M)
is the one that appears with same notation in
[
Neu92
, Introduction]
. Indeed, Neumann’s
\mathrm{CS}(M)
is
2\pi^{2}
times his metric CS-invariant which is half our metric CS-invariant
cs(M)
(Remark
2.1.6
, (iv)).

This is valued in
\mathbb{C}/2\pi^{2}i\mathbb{Z}
if
\partial M=\emptyset
, and in
\mathbb{C}/\pi^{2}i\mathbb{Z}
if
\partial M\neq\emptyset
.

We begin by recalling the general fact that for every smooth manifold
M
, with any representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow G
into a Lie group, there is associated a principal bundle
P(\rho):=\widetilde{M}\times_{\pi_{1}(M)}G
(in this work, principal bundles are endowed with right action by structure group). This principal bundle is endowed with a flat connection
\omega_{\rho}
which is induced from the trivial connection on
\widetilde{M}\times G
.

For a complete hyperbolic three-manifold
M
, let
\rho_{0}:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
be the
geometric representation
, the holonomy representation corresponding to the hyperbolic metric of
M
and
P(\rho_{0})
be the associated principal bundle with flat connection
\omega_{\rho_{0}}
. Then, there exists a map
F(M)\rightarrow P(\rho_{0})
equivariant with respect to
\mathrm{SO}(3)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, constructed as follows. Fixing a frame over a point of the hyperbolic space
\mathbb{H}
, we identify the frame bundle
F(\mathbb{H})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}
with
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{SO}(3)\backslash\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
This gives a
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-equivariant map

(2.2.1.1)

p\times\mathrm{id}:F(\mathbb{H})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}\times\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),

where
p:F(\mathbb{H})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}
is the bundle projection map.
Using the existence of an isomorphism
\widetilde{M}\cong\mathbb{H}
that is equivariant with respect to
\rho_{0}:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
(by completeness of the hyperbolic metric), by taking quotients of (
2.2.1.1
) by
\pi_{1}(M)
we obtain a map

q:F(M)=F(\widetilde{M})/\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow P(\rho_{0})=\widetilde{M}\times_{\pi_{1}(M)}\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})

covering the identity map of
M
. So, any section
s:M\rightarrow F(M)
gives rise to a section
\hat{s}=q\circ s:M\rightarrow P(\rho_{0})
. In particular,
P(\rho_{0})
is trivial since
F(M)
is so, and it follows that the (trivial) principal bundle
P(\rho_{0})
is the extension of the (trivial) bundle
F(M)
via the closed embedding of structure groups
\mathrm{SO}(3)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

Then, the
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Chern-Simons invariant of closed complete hyperbolic three-manifold
M
is defined to be

(2.2.1.2)

cs_{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}}(M):=cs(\rho_{0},\hat{s}):=\int_{\hat{s}(M)}Q(\omega_{\rho_{0}})\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}.

Here, we emphasize that for the Ad-invariant bilinear form
\langle-,-\rangle
in the definition of
Q(\omega_{\rho_{0}})
, we use the second Chern polynomial
C_{2}(X)=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\mathrm{tr}(X^{2})\in I^{2}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2,\mathbb{C}})
(while for the metric Chern-Simons invariant we used the first Pontryagin polynomial
P_{1}(X)=-\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\mathrm{tr}(X^{2})\in I^{2}(\mathfrak{so}(3))
); we caution readers that some people, e.g. Kirk and Klassen
[
KK90
, p.555, line17]
, use the first Pontryagin polynomial
P_{1}
to define
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Chern-Simons invariant.

Lemma 2.2.2
.

We have

-4\sqrt{-1}q^{\ast}Q(\omega_{\rho_{0}})=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}C_{\mathbf{M}\!\mathbf{T}}^{\ast}\mathrm{Vol}_{M}+\sqrt{-1}Q_{M}+d\gamma

for some
2
-form
\gamma
, where
C_{\mathbf{M}\!\mathbf{T}}:F(M)\rightarrow M
is the frame bundle map.

Proof.

(cf.
[
KK90
, p.554]
)
As the first claim is a local statement, it suffices to check it on the covering space (
2.2.1.1
).
Let
\mathrm{pr}_{2}:\mathbb{H}\times\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
be the projection and
\theta
the Maurer-Cartan form on
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
Since
\omega_{\rho_{0}}
is locally given by
\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{\ast}\theta
,
q^{\ast}Q(\omega_{\rho_{0}})
descends from the Chern-Simons
3
-form
Q(\theta)=-\frac{1}{6}\langle\theta\wedge[\theta\wedge\theta]\rangle
on
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
(the curvature of
\theta
is zero), where
\langle-,-\rangle
is the second Chern polynomial
C_{2}(X)=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\mathrm{tr}(X^{2})
on
\mathfrak{sl}_{2,\mathbb{C}}
. Hence, the claim reduces to the equality of two
\mathbb{C}
-valued
3
-forms on
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
:

(2.2.2.1)

-4\sqrt{-1}Q(\theta)=\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}f_{\mathbb{H}}^{\ast}\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathbb{H}}+\sqrt{-1}Q_{\mathbb{H}}+d\widetilde{\gamma}

for some
2
-form
\widetilde{\gamma}
on
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. This is Lemma 3.1 of
[
Yos85
]
. Indeed, choosing a basis

h=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right),\ e=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
0&0\end{array}\right),\ f=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
1&0\end{array}\right)

of
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{sl}_{2,\mathbb{C}}
with dual basis
\{h^{\vee},e^{\vee},f^{\vee}\}
, and using that

[h,e]=2e,\ [h,f]=-2f,\ [e,f]=h,

and
\theta=h\otimes h^{\vee}+e\otimes e^{\vee}+f\otimes f^{\vee}
(regarding elements of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2,\mathbb{C}}^{\vee}
as left-invariant differential forms, as usual), we see that
-4Q(\theta)=\frac{2}{3}\langle\theta\wedge[\theta\wedge\theta]\rangle
equals
\frac{1}{\pi^{2}}h^{\vee}\wedge e^{\vee}\wedge f^{\vee}
. Now, the Yoshida’s lemma in question says that the
\sqrt{-1}
-multiple of the latter
3
-form (which he denoted by
C
) equals the right-hand side of (
2.2.2.1
) (for some
\widetilde{\gamma}
).
∎

Consequently, the
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Chern-Simons invariant of any closed complete hyperbolic three-manifold
M
equals the complex volume of
M
, up to a constant:

Theorem 2.2.3
.

For any closed complete hyperbolic three-manifold
M
, we have

-4\pi^{2}\sqrt{-1}cs_{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}}(M)=\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(M).

Even when
M
is not closed, Kirk and Klassen
[
KK93
]
define the
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Chern-Simon invariant of
cusped
hyperbolic three-manifolds. Recall that
P(\rho_{0})
is trivial.
Their idea is based on the viewpoint that via a choice of a section
s
of
P(\rho_{0})
, the integral (
2.2.1.2
) is an integral over
M
of the
3
-form
Q(A_{0})
for an
1
-form
A_{0}=s^{\ast}(\omega_{\rho_{0}})\in\mathcal{A}^{1}(M,\mathfrak{sl}_{2,\mathbb{C}})
on
M
, and that choices of
s
are in bijection with the bundle automorphisms of the trivial bundle
M\times\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
(
gauge group
), also with gauge-transformations of
A_{0}
. Then the
1
-forms corresponding to the sections
s
that are linear on cusps will be of certain special shape near cusps, called
normal form
(cf.
[
Mey86
, p.228]
). A result of Kirk and Klassen says that the Chern-Simons integral
\int_{M}Q(A)
of a connection
A\in\mathcal{A}^{1}(M,\mathfrak{sl}_{2,\mathbb{C}})
is independent of the choice of
A
, as long as
A
lies in a single gauge-equivalence class and is in normal form near cusps. Although there might not exist a section
s:M\rightarrow P(\rho_{0})
that is linear on cusps, we can still show the existence of a flat connection
A\in\mathcal{A}^{1}(M,\mathfrak{sl}_{2,\mathbb{C}})
in normal form near cusps and having the geometric representation as its holonomy. Applying the result of Kirk and Klassen, we define the
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Chern-Simons invariant of
M
as the Chern-Simons integral
\int_{M}Q(A)
of such connection
A
. This turns out to have the expected relation to the complex volume.

In the next discussion, for simplicity, let us assume that
\partial M
has only one connected component.
Let
\partial M\times[0,1]\subset\overline{M}
be a collar with
\partial M
identified with
\partial M\times\{1\}
. The choice of an ordered basis
\mu,\nu
of
H_{1}(\partial M,\mathbb{Z})
allows to identify
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with the universal covering:
\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\partial M:(x,y)\rightarrow(e^{ix},e^{iy})
. We assume that the orientation of
\partial M
as the boundary of
M
agrees with that inherited from the cover
\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\partial M
, so that
\{dx,dy,dr\}
is an oriented basis of
1
-forms on
\partial M\times I
, where
r
is the coordinate of
I=[0,1]
.

Suppose given a trivialized principal bundle
P=\mathrm{SL}_{2}\times G
for
G=\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
or
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. With respect to the trivialization, any connection
A
is written as
A=\alpha dx+\beta dy+\gamma dr
in a neighborhood of
\partial M
, where
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
are functions
\partial M\times I\rightarrow\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
.
Note that since
\pi_{1}(\partial M)
is abelian, any representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(\partial M)\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is conjugate to one whose image lies in (the image in
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
of) the group of upper triangular representations, and further to a diagonal representation unless
\rho
is (non-central) parabolic, i.e.
\mathrm{Tr}(\rho(\pi_{1}(\partial M)))=\pm 2
.

Definition 2.2.4
.

A flat connection
A
on
M
is in
normal form near boundary
if one of the following two conditions holds:

Case 1
.
in a neighborhood of the boundary,

(2.2.4.1)

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}i\alpha&0\\
0&-i\alpha\end{array}\right)dx+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}i\beta&0\\
0&-i\beta\end{array}\right)dy

for some
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
: we write
A=A^{(1)}(\alpha,\beta)
.

Case 2
.
in a neighborhood of the boundary,

(2.2.4.2)

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\frac{iu}{2}&\frac{a}{2\pi}\exp(i(ux+vy))\\
0&\frac{iu}{2}\end{array}\right)dx+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\frac{iv}{2}&\frac{b}{2\pi}\exp(i(ux+vy))\\
0&\frac{iv}{2}\end{array}\right)dy.

for some
u,v\in\mathbb{Z}
,
a,b\in\mathbb{C}
: we write
A=A^{(2)}(u,v;a,b)
.

The point here is that the connection
A
is constant near boundary (i.e.
\alpha
,
\beta
,
u
,
v
,
a
,
b
are all constants).

The local holonomy
\pi_{1}(\partial M)\rightarrow(\mathrm{P})\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
of
A
at the boundary is described explicitly by the shape of
A
near the boundary as follows.

Lemma 2.2.5
.

Every connection in normal form near boundary is flat in a neighborhood of the boundary, and for a suitable base point near
\partial M
, the holonomy representation
\rho_{A}
is given by:

\displaystyle\text{ Case }1:\qquad

\displaystyle\mu\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{2\pi i\alpha}&0\\
0&e^{-2\pi i\alpha}\end{array}\right),\ \nu\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{2\pi i\beta}&0\\
0&e^{-2\pi i\beta}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\text{ Case }2:\qquad

\displaystyle\mu\mapsto(-1)^{u}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&a\\
0&1\end{array}\right),\quad\nu\mapsto(-1)^{v}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&b\\
0&1\end{array}\right)

(in the case
G=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, we consider their images in
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
).

From this lemma, we see that given a local holonomy representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(\partial M)\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, there are as many local connections in normal form having holonomy
\rho
as choices of branches of
\log\rho(\mu)_{11}
,
\log\rho(\nu)_{11}
, where
\rho
is conjugated to be of the form (
2.2.5
) or (
2.2.5
) (in Case 2, these branches are really parities of
u
and
v
). Note that any homomorphism
\rho:\pi_{1}(\partial M)\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
has a conjugate in one of these forms (since
\pi_{1}(\partial M)
is abelian). It turns out that for every global homomorphism
\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
and for any choice of constants
(\alpha,\beta)
,
(u,v,a,b)
in (
2.2.5
), (
2.2.5
) (for some conjugate of
\rho|_{\pi_{1}(\partial M)}
), there exists a global flat connection giving
\rho
as holonomy which is in normal form for these constants.

Proposition 2.2.6
.

[
KK93
, 2.3,3.2]

Let
N_{1},\cdots,N_{h}
be the connected components (tori) of
\partial M
.

(1) Any connection on
M
which is flat in a neighborhood of
\partial M
can be gauge-transformed to a connection in normal form near boundary.

(2) Suppose given a representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. For any choice of constants
(\alpha_{k},\beta_{k})
in Case 1 and
(u_{k},v_{k},a_{k},b_{k})
in Case 2 (
k=1,\cdots,h
) such that for each
k=1,\cdots,h
,
\rho|_{N_{k}}
is conjugate to either (
2.2.5
) or (
2.2.5
), there exists a flat connection on
P=M\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}
whose holonomy is conjugate to
\rho
, and which at each cusp is in normal form (
2.2.4.1
), (
2.2.4.2
) for these constants.

The same holds for a representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

In (2), there is an overlap among the two cases, i.e. Case 1 with
(\alpha,\beta)\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
equals Case 2 with
(a,b)=(0,0)
,
(\alpha,\beta)=(-\frac{u}{2},-\frac{v}{2})
, in which case we could choose any one among these two.

Proof.

(1) This is proved in
[
KK93
, 2.3]
in the case that the structure group of the principal bundle is
\mathrm{SU}(2)
, in which case the holonomy at
\pi_{1}(\partial M)
is always diagonalizable. This gives a proof in Case 1 of Definition
2.2.4
. The same argument carries over to
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
in Case 2, too.
In more detail, the obstructions to extending a given local
1
-form to entire
M
lie in
H^{i}(M,\partial M,\pi_{i}(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})))
, which are all zero.
So, the question is reduced to the problem of finding a local connection of the form (
2.2.4.2
) having the (local) holonomy (
2.2.5
). This problem is solved in the explanation (p.538) of Case 2 in the proof of ibid. Theorem 3.2.

(2) For
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, in case 1, this is ibid. Theorem 2.3 (3), while in case 2 it is shown in the proof (p. 538) of ibid. Theorem 3.2. One easily sees that the argument carries over to
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
∎

We define the Chern-Simons invariant of a flat connection on the trivial bundle
P=M\times G
(
G=\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
or
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
), for flat connections that are in normal form near boundary,
3
3

3

Later, we will also need to consider the Chern-Simons integral of an arbitrary flat connection.

as

(2.2.6.1)

cs_{M}(A):=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{M}\mathrm{Tr}(dA\wedge A+\frac{2}{3}A\wedge A\wedge A)

(note that here we use the second Chern polynomial
C_{2}=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\mathrm{tr}
).

Theorem 2.2.7
.

[
KK93
, 2.4]

If two connections
A
,
B
on
P
are gauge-equivalent and also are in the same normal form near
\partial M
, then
cs(A)=cs(B)
\mod\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

First, suppose that
G=\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. When the boundary holonomy is diagonalizable,
we can repeat the proof of the same statement in the case
G=\mathrm{SU}(2)
(Theorem 2.4 of
[
KK93
]
). When the boundary holonomy is parabolic but non-diagonalizable, this is proved in
ibid.
Lemma 3.3. When
G=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, it is not difficult to check that the same arguments continue to work.
∎

Corollary 2.2.8
.

There exists a flat connection
A
on
M\times\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
such that at each cusp
N_{k}
,
A
is in normal form of Case 2 for
(u_{k},v_{k})=(0,0)
and some
(a_{k},b_{k})
and that the holonomy representation of
A
is the geometric representation
\rho_{0}
, up to conjugacy.

The Chern-Simons invariant
cs_{M}(A)
of such a flat connection
A
is well-defined mod
\mathbb{Z}
, independent of the choice of
A
; by definition, this is the
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Chern-Simons invariant
cs_{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}}(M)
of the cusped complete hyperbolic three-manifold
M
.

Proof.

It is known (
[
NZ85
,
\lx@sectionsign
4]
) that the geometric representation
\rho_{0}
is
boundary parabolic
, meaning that at each cusp, after a conjugation of
\rho_{0}
, the images of the meridian and the longitude lie in the unipotent subgroup (i.e. the image in
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
of the subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
of unipotent matrices). Hence, for each
k=1,\cdots,h
, there exists a unique
(a_{k},b_{k})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
such that
\rho|_{N_{k}}
is conjugate to (
2.2.5
) for the constants
(0,0,a_{k},b_{k})
.
Then, for this family of constants
\{(0,0,a_{k},b_{k})\}_{k=1,\cdots,h}
and
\rho=\rho_{0}
,
Proposition
2.2.6
, (2) gives a flat connection
A
with the stated properties.

The second statement is an immediate consequence of Theorem
2.2.7
, in view of the well-known fact that the holonomy gives an injection

\{\text{flat connections on }M\times G\}/\mathcal{G}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{1}(M),G)/\text{conjugation},

where
\mathcal{G}=\mathrm{Hom}(M,G)
(gauge group), cf.
[
KK90
, Lem.2.2]
.
∎

We normalize the
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Chern-Simons invariant
cs_{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}}(M)
by

(2.2.8.1)

\mathrm{CS}_{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}}(M):=(2\pi i)^{2}cs_{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}}(M)\ \in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(2).

Theorem 2.2.9
.

The (normalized)
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Chern-Simons invariant
\mathrm{CS}_{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}}(M)
of any complete hyperbolic three-manifold
M
of finite volume is related to the complex volume
\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)
by

\sqrt{-1}\mathrm{CS}_{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}}(M)=\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(M).

Proof.

The case that
M
is closed is Theorem
2.2.3
. In the case that
M
has cusps, this is proved in p. 555-556 (especially, line 16-20 on p.556) of
[
KK93
]
: we remind the readers that to define the
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
Chern-Simons invariant
cs_{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}}(M)
, Kirk and Klassen use the first Pontryagin polynomial for the bilinear form
\langle-,-\rangle
in Definition
2.1.1
, so that (in terms of
cs_{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}}(M)
) their
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
Chern-Simon invariant equals
-4
times our
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
Chern-Simon invariant (
[
KK93
, p.555,line17]
).
∎

We can also define
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Chern-Simons invariant of hyperbolic three-manifolds
M
which however depends on a lift of the geometric representation
\rho_{0}:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
to
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
; such a lift is called a
spin structure
on
M
(recall that the bundle
P(\rho_{0})
is trivial and
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})=\mathrm{Spin}(3)_{\mathbb{C}}
, so that if
\tilde{\rho}_{0}:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is a lift of
\rho_{0}
, there exists a double covering
P(\tilde{\rho}_{0})\rightarrow P(\rho_{0})
of the associated principal bundles which is compatible with the actions of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
; in fact,
P(\rho_{0})=P(\tilde{\rho}_{0})\times_{\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, extension via change of structure group).
Here, we only consider the case
M
is closed; the cusped case will be considered in Subsection
6.1
.

Like
P(\rho_{0})
, the
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-bundle
P(\tilde{\rho}_{0})
affords a section
\tilde{s}
, since
\pi_{1}(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}))=\{1\}
, as one can see by the obstruction theory
[
Ste99
,
\lx@sectionsign
34.2]
.
When
M
is closed, using
\tilde{s}
one defines the
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Chern-Simons invariant of a lift
\tilde{\rho}_{0}:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
of
\rho_{0}
by

(2.2.9.1)

cs(\tilde{\rho}_{0},\tilde{s}):=\int_{\tilde{s}(M)}Q(\omega_{\tilde{\rho}_{0}})\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z},

where as before
Q(\omega_{\tilde{\rho}_{0}})\in\mathcal{A}^{3}(P(\tilde{\rho}_{0}),\mathfrak{sl}_{2,\mathbb{C}})
is the Chern-Simons
3
-form of the flat connection
\omega(\tilde{\rho}_{0})
on
P(\tilde{\rho}_{0})
(induced from the trivial connection on
\widetilde{M}\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
), and the
3
-form
Q(-)
is defined using the second Chern polynomial
C_{2}\in I^{2}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2,\mathbb{C}})
.
It is then clear that

(2.2.9.2)

cs(\tilde{\rho}_{0},\tilde{s})=cs(\rho_{0},\hat{s}),

where
\hat{s}
is the section on
P(\rho_{0})
induced from
\tilde{s}
(recall that we use
C_{2}\in I^{2}(\mathfrak{sl}_{2,\mathbb{C}})
in the definition of
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Chern-Simons invariant
cs(\rho_{0},\hat{s})
).

2.3  Ideal triangulation of cusped hyperbolic three-manifolds

In this section,
M
is a complete hyperbolic three manifold (of finite volume). Suppose
M
is non-compact and has an ideal triangulation:
M=\Delta_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\Delta_{N}
.
This means that each simplex
\Delta_{i}
is a (hyperbolic) ideal tetrahedron, i.e. a tetrahedron in
\overline{\mathbb{H}^{3}}
which has its vertices in
\mathbb{CP}^{1}=\partial\overline{\mathbb{H}^{3}}
and has geodesic faces, and that
M
is obtained by glueing together these ideal tetrahedra by means of face-pairing maps and then removing vertices or horospheres (closed balls centered at vertices); in this case
M
is the interior of a compact manifold having a union of tori as boundary, and these
boundary tori
have the induced flat metric. For the question of existence of an ideal triangulation, see the introduction of
[
Fra04
]
.
Any ideal tetrahedron with ordered vertices
\{z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4}\}
is moved by
\mathrm{Iso}^{+}(\mathbb{H}^{3})=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
to the ideal tetrahedron with ordered vertices
\{\infty,0,1,z\}
for some uniquely determined
z\in\mathbb{CP}^{1}-\{0,1,\infty\}
, which is the cross ratio
4
4

4

This definition of cross ratio differs from the ones used in
[
NY99
]
,
[
Neu92
]
by
z\leftrightarrow z^{-1}
.

z=[z_{1}:z_{2}:z_{3}:z_{4}]=\frac{(z_{3}-z_{1})(z_{4}-z_{2})}{(z_{3}-z_{2})(z_{4}-z_{1})}\in\mathbb{C}\backslash\{0,1\}.

Even permutation of the vertices changes
z
to one of
z
,
z^{\prime}=\frac{1}{1-z}
,
z^{\prime\prime}=1-\frac{1}{z}
; one of these numbers is called the
shape parameter
(or
cross-ratios
or
modulus
) of the oriented ideal tetrahedron. We fix an ordering of the vertices of each ideal tetrahedron compatible with the orientation of
M
, and choose one of the three associated shape parameters
z_{j}^{0}
of each
\Delta_{j}
; put

\mathscr{Z}^{0}:=[\log z^{0}_{1},\cdots,\log z^{0}_{N},\log(1-z^{0}_{1}),\cdots,\log(1-z^{0}_{N})]\in\mathbb{C}^{2N}.

(row vectors).
5
5

5

in this subsection, however, we will work with column vectors, in accordance with the convention of Neumann and Zagier
[
NZ85
]
(but in opposite to that in
[
Neu92
]
).

In fact, as we fix an ordering of the vertices, to each edge of the tetrahedron,
one can assign one of the three shape parameters
z
,
z^{\prime}
,
z^{\prime\prime}
, in such a way that the parameters associated with two opposite edges are equal and all
z
,
z^{\prime}
,
z^{\prime\prime}
are attached to some (pairs of opposite) edges (see
[
Neu92
,
\lx@sectionsign
2]
for details).

It is known (
[
NZ85
,
\lx@sectionsign
2]
,
[
Neu92
,
\lx@sectionsign
2]
) that when
M
has
h
cusps,
the complete hyperbolic structure of
M
is determined by
\mathscr{Z}^{0}
subject to
N+2h
linear equations with integer coefficients, written in the form

(2.3.0.1)

U(\mathscr{Z}^{0})^{t}=\pi\sqrt{-1}D,

where
U
is a
(N+2h)\times 2N
integral matrix and
D
is a column vector in
\mathbb{Z}^{N+2h}
. More precisely, this system of equations consists of two parts: we write

(2.3.0.2)

U=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}R\\
C\end{array}\right),\quad R\in M_{N\times 2N}(\mathbb{Z}),\ C\in M_{2h\times 2N}(\mathbb{Z})

and
D=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}D_{1}\\
D_{2}\end{array}\right)
for
D_{1}\in M_{N\times 1}(\mathbb{Z})
,
D_{2}\in M_{2h\times 1}(\mathbb{Z})
, so that (
2.3.0.1
) splits to

(2.3.0.3)

R(\mathscr{Z}^{0})^{t}=\pi\sqrt{-1}D_{1},\quad C(\mathscr{Z}^{0})^{t}=\pi\sqrt{-1}D_{2}.

The geometric meanings of these equations are as follows.
The first equation
R(\mathscr{Z}^{0})^{t}=\pi\sqrt{-1}D_{1}
, called
consistency relations
, expresses the condition that the tetrahedra fit together around each edge of the triangulation.
The second one
C(\mathscr{Z}^{0})^{t}=\pi\sqrt{-1}D_{2}
, called
cusp relations
, means that at each end (or cusp), if
T_{i}
is the corresponding connected component (torus) of
\partial M
, the elements of the peripheral subgroup
\pi_{1}(T_{i},t_{i})
have parabolic holonomy (i.e. are unipotent matrices).

For the following (and later uses), we introduce “tensor-multiplication over
\mathbb{Q}
”
M\otimes N
of two matrices
M\in M_{l\times m}(\mathbb{C})
,
N\in M_{m\times n}(\mathbb{C})
whose output is a matrix in
M_{l\times n}(\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C})
:

(2.3.0.4)

(M\otimes N)_{ij}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}M_{ik}\otimes N_{kj}\in\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}\ (1\leq i\leq l,1\leq j\leq n)

(namely, we multiply each row
X
of
M
and each column
Y
of
N
as in the usual matrix multiplication, but using the rule that two entries
x
,
y
in
X
,
Y
are multiplied to
x\otimes y\in\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathbb{C}
.)
In the similar manner, we can also define wedge product
M\wedge N
of two matrices
M
,
N
. The usual (scalar) matrix product as well as this wedge product are all induced from the tensor product
M\otimes N
via multiplication
\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and the skew-symmetrization
\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\wedge\mathbb{C}
, respectively. Note that for any rational
Q\in M_{m\times m}(\mathbb{Q})
, one has

(2.3.0.5)

MQ\otimes N=M\otimes QN.

Lemma 2.3.1
.

Let
R
and
C
be as in (
2.3.0.2
).
Suppose
x,y\in\mathbb{C}^{2N}
(row vectors) satisfy
Rx^{t}=Ry^{t}=0
. Then,

xJ_{2N}\otimes y^{t}=\frac{1}{2}(Cx^{t})^{t}J_{2h}\otimes Cy^{t}
.

In particular, by applying skew-symmetrization, we recover the Neumann’s formula
[
Neu92
, (3.10)]
:

\text{for }x,y\in\mathbb{C}^{2N},\ Rx=Ry=0\ \Rightarrow\ x\wedge y=\frac{1}{2}Cx\wedge Cy

(He works with column vectors, and his “wedge product”
x\wedge y
is our
xJ_{2N}\wedge y^{t}
). Also, by taking multiplication of our formula, we recover Corollary 2.4. of
[
NZ85
]
; Neumann says that his formula follows from this corollary by ‘‘formal observation’’. Our formula is a further expansion of this ‘‘formal observation’’.
6
6

6

If we write our bilinear form
\langle x,y\rangle:=xJ_{2N}\otimes y^{t}
as a sum of symmetric form
S(x,y)
and alternating form
A(x,y)
in the standard way,
S(x,y)
is the Neumann’s “wedge product”
x\wedge y
, and
A((x_{1},x_{2}),(y_{1},y_{2}))=(x_{1}\otimes y_{2}^{t}+y_{2}\otimes x_{1}^{t})-(x_{2}\otimes y_{1}^{t}+y_{1}\otimes x_{2}^{t})
, which is non-trivial in general; thus our formula is stronger than Neumann’s one. But, for the purpose of showing vanishing of complex Dehn invariant, we just need
x=y
, so the Neumann’s formula suffices.

Proof.

This is a re-enactment of the proof of Corollary 2.4. of
[
NZ85
]
, replacing the usual matrix multiplications by matrix tensor multiplications appropriately.
For any matrix
A
with
2N
columns we shall denote by
[A]\subset\mathbb{C}^{2N}
the subspace of
\mathbb{C}^{2N}
generated by the rows of
A
. Also, we let
(-)^{\perp}
denote the orthogonal complement of
(-)\subset\mathbb{C}^{2N}
with respect to the symplectic form
\frac{1}{2}xJ_{2N}y^{t}
, where
J_{2N}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&I_{N}\\
-I_{N}&0\end{array}\right)
.

We have
0=Rx^{t}=RJ_{2N}(xJ_{2N})^{t}
, i.e.
xJ_{2N}\in[R]^{\perp}
.
Since
[R]^{\perp}=[U]
(
[
NZ85
, Prop.2.3]
), there exists
z\in\mathbb{C}^{N+2h}
such that
xJ_{2N}=zU
. Also,
UJ_{2N}C^{t}=2\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{2h}\\
0\end{array}\right)
by
[
NZ85
, Thm.2.2]
(“Neumann-Zagier symplectic relation”), so
xC^{t}=-xJ_{2N}^{2}C^{t}=-2z\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{2h}\\
0\end{array}\right)
. Now,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}xC^{t}J_{2h}\otimes Cy^{t}=

\displaystyle-z\left(\begin{array}[]{c}J_{2h}\\
0\end{array}\right)J_{2h}\otimes Cy^{t}=z\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{c}C\\
0\end{array}\right)y^{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle z\otimes\left(\begin{array}[]{c}C\\
R\end{array}\right)y^{t}=z\otimes Uy^{t}=zU\otimes y^{t}=zJ_{2N}\otimes y^{t}.\ \qed

2.4  Complex volume of hyperbolic
3
-manifolds as Bloch regulator

The complex volume of a complete hyperbolic three-manifold is expressed in terms of Bloch regulator
[
Blo00
, Lecture6]
. For the brach of
\log(-)
, we take the standard one with branch cut
(-\infty,0]
.

The Bloch regulator is the map
\mathbb{C}-\{0,1\}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\wedge_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}
defined by

(2.4.0.1)

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}(z)=

\displaystyle\frac{\log(z)}{2\pi i}\wedge\frac{\log(1-z)}{2\pi i}+1\wedge\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\int_{0}^{z}\left(\frac{\log(1-t)}{t}+\frac{\log(t)}{1-t}\right)dt.

This expression is interpreted as follows: given the choice of the standard branch of
\log(z)
, a value for any of the functions
\log(z)
,
\log(1-z)
and
\int_{0}^{z}\left(\frac{\log(1-t)}{t}+\frac{\log(t)}{1-t}\right)dt
is determined by the choice of a path from
0
to
z
in
\mathbb{C}-\{0,1\}
. In more detail, we take a path made up of the straight path from
0
to
\frac{1}{2}
followed by a path from
\frac{1}{2}
to
z
and continue analytically the (standard) branches of
\log(t)
,
\log(1-t)
along that path (picking up their values at
z
), while integrating the function in the integrand thus determined along the same path. One can prove (cf. proof of Lemma 6.1.1 of
[
Blo00
]
) that this gives a well-defined (continuous) map
\mathbb{C}-\{0,1\}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\wedge_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}
(i.e. the value is independent of the choice of branch and path): although Bloch’s original regulator (
3.3.0.6
) is different from the above (his regulator is valued in
\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}^{\ast}
), the argument of
loc. cit.
continues to apply in our case, since we are working with wedge products.
Also, the Bloch regulator can be regarded as an “analytic mapping” from
\mathbb{C}-\{0,1\}
to
\mathbb{C}\wedge_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}
, in the sense that locally on an open neighborhood
U
, it is an element of
\mathcal{O}(U)\wedge\mathcal{O}(U)
for the ring
\mathcal{O}(U)
of analytic functions on
U
.

The function appearing in the second term of (
2.4.0.1
) (defined by the same procedure)

(2.4.0.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(z)=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{z}\left(\frac{\log(1-t)}{t}+\frac{\log(t)}{1-t}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\log(z)\log(1-z).

is called
Rogers dilogarithm function
, where
\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)
is the dilogarithm function which is defined on
|z|<1
by
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{n^{2}}
and analytically continued to
\mathbb{C}-[1,\infty)
using the expression

(2.4.0.3)

\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)=-\int_{0}^{z}\log(1-t)\frac{dt}{t}

and the standard branch of
\log(z)
; so,
\mathcal{R}(z)
can be regarded as an analytic function on
\mathbb{C}-(-\infty,0]\cup[1,\infty)
.
However, the Rogers dilogarithm itself (so, the two terms of the Bloch regulator individually) does not extend to
\mathbb{C}-\{0,1\}
as a continuous single-valued function (in the case of the Bloch regulator, the ambiguities in the two terms in (
2.4.0.1
) created by monodromy cancel each out); nevertheless, its restriction to the interval
(0,1)
can be extended to
\mathbb{R}
as a continuous (even analytic except at the two points
\{0,1\}
) real-valued function (
[
Zag07
, II.1.A]
).

For a field
k
, the pre-Bloch group
\mathcal{P}(k)
is the abelian group generated by symbols
[z]
,
z\in k-\{0,1\}
, subject to the relations:

(2.4.0.4)

\displaystyle\left[x\right]-\left[y\right]+\left[\frac{y}{x}\right]-

\displaystyle\left[\frac{1-x^{-1}}{1-y^{-1}}\right]+\left[\frac{1-x}{1-y}\right]=0

(2.4.0.5)

\displaystyle[x]+[1-x]=

\displaystyle[x]+[\frac{1}{x}]=0.

The first of these conditions is usually called the “five term relation” and is equivalent to

\sum_{i=0}^{4}(-1)^{i}\left[[z_{0}:\cdots:\hat{z}_{i}:\cdots:z_{4}]\right]=0

for distinct
z_{0},\cdots,z_{4}\in k-\{0,1\}
(the inner brackets
[-]
are cross-ratios).
Sometimes, one considers the group
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(k)
similarly defined using the first condition only.
Then, it is known (
[
DS82
, 5.4,5.6,5.11]
) that when the characteristic of
k
is not
2
, the elements in
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(k)
of the form (
2.4.0.5
) are torsions of exponent
6
and that when
k
is algebraically closed,
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(k)=\mathcal{P}(k)
.
The map
z\in\mathbb{C}-\{0,1\}\mapsto z\wedge(1-z)
induces a homomorphism

(2.4.0.6)

\delta_{\mathbb{C}}\,:\,\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}\wedge_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}^{\times}\ ;\ [z]\mapsto z\wedge(1-z)

(
[
Sus90
, Lem.1.1]
).
The Bloch regulator (
2.4.0.1
) satisfies the relation (
2.4.0.4
) (
[
DS82
, p.173]
), so it induces a homomorphism (again denoted by
\rho
)

\tilde{\rho}:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{C}).

In the following, we normalize
\tilde{\rho}
by
\rho:=\Xi\circ\tilde{\rho}
, where
\Xi(z\wedge w)=2\pi iz\wedge 2\pi iw:\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{C})
:

(2.4.0.7)

\rho(z)=\log(z)\wedge\log(1-z)+(2\pi i)\wedge\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{0}^{z}\left(\frac{\log(1-t)}{t}+\frac{\log(t)}{1-t}\right)dt.

We recall the commutative diagram with exact rows, where the upper row is (a part of) the Bloch-Wigner exact sequence
[
Dup87
, (1.7)]
,
[
DS82
, Prop.4.17]
:

(2.4.0.8)

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{3}(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})^{\delta},\mathbb{Z})/(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\mathbb{C}}}
\textstyle{\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{C}^{\times})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{=}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(2)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{2\pi i\wedge\frac{1}{2\pi i}\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\textstyle{\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{C}^{\times})}

Here,
H_{3}(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})^{\delta},\mathbb{Z})
is the Eilenberg-MacLane integral group homology of the discrete group
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})^{\delta}
(for a Lie group
G
,
G^{\delta}
denotes the same group with discrete topology);
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
sits inside this homology group and see
[
Dup87
, (1.4)]
for the definition of
\sigma
. Also,
e(z\wedge w)=\exp(z)\wedge\exp(w)
, and
c
is induced by
\rho\circ\sigma
by commutativity. To see the injection
2\pi i\wedge\frac{1}{2\pi i}\mathrm{id}:\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(2)\hookrightarrow\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{C})
, we observe (using that
\mathbb{C}
is uniquely divisible) the canonical identifications

(2.4.0.9)

\mathbb{C}\wedge_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}=\mathbb{C}\wedge_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C},\quad\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}(k)\wedge_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}(k)=\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(k)\wedge_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(k)\ (k\in\mathbb{Z}),

(which hold by divisibility of
\mathbb{C}
,
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}(k)
) and
2\pi i\wedge\frac{1}{2\pi i}(\mathbb{Q}(2))=2\pi i\wedge\mathbb{Q}2\pi i=\mathbb{Q}\cdot 2\pi i\wedge 2\pi i=0
.

Therefore,
\rho:\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\Lambda^{2}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{C})
induces a map from the
Bloch group

\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C}):=\mathrm{Ker}(\delta_{\mathbb{C}})
to
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(2)
:

(2.4.0.10)

\rho:\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(2),

which we once again denote by
\rho
and call (normalized) Bloch regulator.

For the unnormalized Bloch regulator
\tilde{\rho}
(
2.4.0.1
), there is an analogue of
the Bloch-Wigner exact sequence (
2.4.0.8
), where the bottom exact sequence is replaced by
0\rightarrow\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1\wedge\mathrm{id}}}{{\rightarrow}}\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}(\mathbb{C})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{e}}}{{\rightarrow}}\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}(\mathbb{C}^{\times})
with
\tilde{e}(z\wedge w)=\exp(2\pi iz)\wedge\exp(2\pi iw)
(
[
Dup87
, (1.7)]
); so, we obtain the unnormalized Bloch regulator
\tilde{\rho}:\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}
.
It is easy to see that there exists the relation

(2.4.0.11)

\rho=(2\pi i)^{2}\tilde{\rho}.

under the identification
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathbb{C}/(2\pi i)^{2}\mathbb{Q}:x\mapsto(2\pi i)^{2}x
.

Also, via
\sigma
, we identify
\rho
(
2.4.0.10
) as an element of

c\in H^{3}(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})^{\delta},\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(2))=\mathrm{Hom}(H_{3}(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})^{\delta},\mathbb{Z}),\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(2)).

The map
\delta_{\mathbb{C}}
is often called
complex Dehn invariant
map, because the usual Dehn invariant of the ideal tetrahedron
\Delta(z):=\Delta(\infty,0,1,z)
with shape parameter
z=[\infty:0:1:z]
equals twice the “imaginary part” of
\delta_{\mathbb{C}}([z])
; the “imaginary part” of an element of
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}(1)\wedge\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}(1)
refers to the component corresponding to the summand
(\mathbb{R}\otimes\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}(1))
in the decomposition

\mathbb{C}/\Lambda\wedge\mathbb{C}/\Lambda\cong(\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}/\Lambda)\wedge(\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R}/\Lambda)\cong\left[(\mathbb{R}\wedge\mathbb{R})\oplus(\mathbb{R}/\Lambda\wedge\mathbb{R}/\Lambda)\right]\oplus(\mathbb{R}\otimes\mathbb{R}/\Lambda)

with
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}(1)
(
[
Neu98
, 2.5]
).

For a complete hyperbolic three-manifold
M
of finite volume, let
\rho_{0}:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
be the holonomy representation, unique up to conjugacy, of the complete hyperbolic structure of
M
:
M\simeq\mathbb{H}^{3}/\rho_{0}(\pi_{1}(M))
. The
invariant trace field
of
M
is the subfield of
\mathbb{C}
generated by squares of the traces of elements of
\rho_{0}(\pi_{1}(M))\subset\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
; this field is known to be a subfield of
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}\subset\mathbb{C}
.

Theorem 2.4.1
.

Let
M
be a complete hyperbolic three-manifold of finite volume.

(1) The element
\sum_{i}[z_{i}]\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})
attached to any ideal triangulation
M=\Delta(z_{1})\cup\cdots\cup\Delta(z_{N})
of
M
has zero complex Dehn invariant:
\delta_{\mathbb{C}}(\sum_{i}[z_{i}])=0
.

(2) The element
\sum_{i}[z_{i}]
of
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C})
is independent of the choice of ideal triangulation of
M
; we denote it by
\beta(M)
. Moreover, the image of
\beta(M)
in
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathbb{Q}
lies in
\mathcal{B}(k(M))\otimes\mathbb{Q}
.

(3) We have

2\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)=\sqrt{-1}\rho(\beta(M))\mod\sqrt{-1}\pi^{2}\mathbb{Q},

where
\rho
is the (normzalized) Bloch regulator (
2.4.0.10
).
So, by Theorem
2.2.9
,

(2.4.1.1)

2\mathrm{CS}_{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}}(M)=\rho(\beta(M))\ \text{ in }\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(2).

Proof.

(1) This has been known for long, first proved by Thurston (see Introduction of
[
NY99
]
), and different proofs have been given, e.g.
[
NY99
,
\lx@sectionsign
5]
.
We give a proof due to Neumann
[
Neu92
, p.255-256]
(using Lemma
2.3.1
). Let
Z^{0}_{1}:=(\log z^{0}_{1},\cdots,\log z^{0}_{N})\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
,
Z^{0}_{2}:=(\log(1-z^{0}_{1}),\cdots,\log(1-z^{0}_{N}))\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Choose a rational solution
Q\in\mathbb{Q}^{2N}
to (
2.3.0.1
), and put
W:=Z^{0}-Q\pi\sqrt{-1}\in\mathbb{C}^{2N}
; so
W=Z^{0}
in
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(1)
.
Then, adopting our matrix tensor/wedge product notation,

Z^{0}_{1}\wedge(Z^{0}_{2})^{t}=Z^{0}J_{2N}\otimes(Z^{0})^{t}=WJ_{2N}\otimes W^{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ast)}}{{=}}\ \frac{1}{2}(CW^{t})^{t}J_{2h}\otimes CW^{t}=0

Here,
(\ast)
is Lemma
2.3.1
and the last equality follows from the fact that the entries of
CW^{t}\in\mathbb{C}^{2h}
are holonomies of the meridians and the longitudes (
[
Neu92
, (2.6), (3.1), (3.2)]
), so are zeros, as we assume
M
to be complete. Now, in view of (
2.4.0.9
), this proves the claim since
\delta_{\mathbb{C}}(\sum_{i}[z_{i}])
is the image of
Z^{0}_{1}\wedge(Z^{0}_{2})^{t}
under the homomorphism
\exp\wedge\exp:\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}(1)\wedge\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}(1)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}\wedge\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

(2) The first statement is Proposition 4.3 of
[
NY99
]
; see also
[
CMJ03
]
for another proof based on a different construction of
\beta(M)
. The second statement is Theorem 1.2. of
ibid.

(3) This is Theorem 1.3 of
[
NY99
]
, which was proved (with a different formulation) in
[
Neu92
, Thm.1
\&
(3.8)]
