. It seems worth pointing out three main ingredients of that proof: (i) the fact that the Cheeger-Chern-Simon class
\hat{C}_{2}\in H^{3}(\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})^{\delta},\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(2))
(associated with the
2
nd Chern polynomial
C_{2}
) equals half the Bloch regulator
\rho

[
Dup87
, Thm.1.8]
, (ii) Yoshida’s analytic formula for complex volumes
[
Yos85
, Thm.2]
, and finally (iii) Neumann’s explicit description of the (Riemannian) volume of (not-necessarily complete) hyperbolic three-manifolds in terms of shape parameters and the values of “Bloch-Wigner dilogarithm (the imaginary part of the Bloch regulator) at these points, together with a rational solution to (
2.3.0.1
)
[
Neu92
]
.
∎

For later use, we recall one functional equation (among many) of
\tilde{\rho}(z)
(equiv. of
\rho(z)
):

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{2}(1-z)=

\displaystyle-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)+\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}-\log(z)\otimes\log(1-z),

(2.4.1.2)

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}(1-z)=

\displaystyle-\tilde{\rho}(z)\quad(z\in\mathbb{C}-(-\infty,0]\cup[1,\infty))

The first relation is well-known
[
Zag07
,
\lx@sectionsign
2]
and the second one is deduced from the first one.

3
Big and skew-symmetric periods of framed mixed Tate motives

3.1  Period matrix of splitted Hodge-Tate structure

A period matrix of a pure
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge structure
H
is a transition matrix for two bases of
H_{\mathbb{C}}
, one a
\mathbb{Q}
-basis
\{v_{i}\}
of
H
and the other a
\mathbb{C}
-basis
\{e_{i}\}
of
H_{\mathbb{C}}
, often the latter basis being adapted to the Hodge filtration in the sense that there exists a sequence
\{r_{1},r_{2},\cdots,r_{m}\}
of positive integers such that the flag of subspaces in the Hodge filtration matches that of the subspaces spanned by the increasing subsets
\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{r_{1}+\cdots+r_{m}}\}_{m}
of basis elements. When
H
is the singular cohomology space
H=H^{i}_{sing}(X(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{Q})
(of some degree) of a smooth projective variety (or a pure motive) over a field
k\subset\mathbb{C}
, one usually chooses a basis of the algebraic deRham cohomology group
H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X/k)
, regarded as a basis of
H_{\mathbb{C}}
via the canonical isomorphism
\Phi:H^{i}_{sing}(X(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{Q})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}=H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X/k)\otimes_{k}\mathbb{C}
. Namely, the period matrix equals the matrix expression with respect to some bases for the isomorphism
\Phi
, an isomorphism defined over
\mathbb{C}
between two spaces
H
,
H^{i}_{\mathrm{dR}}(X/k)
which are defined over different subfields
\mathbb{Q},k
of
\mathbb{C}
.

For a pure Hodge structure, any period matrix thus defined determines the Hodge structure. While there does not seem to exist an efficient matrix expression for general mixed Hodge structures, the situation for Hodge-Tate structure is just as good as in pure cases, as we explain now. The basic idea is to exploit splittings, defined over
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathbb{C}
, of the weight filtration of Hodge-Tate structure.

Let
E\subset E^{\prime}
be a field extension. An
E^{\prime}
-
splitting
of an increasing filtration
W^{\bullet}H
on a
E
-vector space
H
is a choice of an isomorphism
\varphi:\bigoplus_{k}\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{k}H\otimes_{E}E^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}H_{E^{\prime}}
compatible with the weight filtrations on both sides (i.e. for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\varphi
maps
\bigoplus_{l\leq k}\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{l}H\otimes_{E}E^{\prime}
to
W_{k}H\otimes_{E}E^{\prime}
and the induced (by quotient) automorphism of
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{k}H\otimes_{E}E^{\prime}
is the identity map). Any
E
-splitting
\varphi
gives an
E^{\prime}
-splitting
\varphi_{E^{\prime}}
by scalar extension.
The same discussion also applies to decreasing filtration.

Lemma 3.1.1
.

The following conditions on a mixed
\mathbb{R}
-Hodge structure
H
are equivalent:

(i)
H
is an (
\mathbb{R}
-)Hodge-Tate structure, i.e. for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2k-1}=0
and
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2k}H
is a direct sum of copies of
\mathbb{R}(-k)
.

(ii) For every
p\in\mathbb{Z}
, the canonical map

(3.1.1.1)

F^{p}H_{\mathbb{C}}\cap W_{2p}H_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2p}H\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}

is an isomorphism.

(iii) For every
p\in\mathbb{Z}
, the canonical map

(3.1.1.2)

F^{p}H_{\mathbb{C}}\cap W_{2p}H_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\mathrm{gr}^{p}_{F}H_{\mathbb{C}}

is an isomorphism.

(iv) The canonical map

(3.1.1.3)

\bigoplus_{p}F^{p}H_{\mathbb{C}}\cap W_{2p}H_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow H_{\mathbb{C}}

is an isomorhpism.

(iv’) There exists a decomposition:

H_{\mathbb{C}}=\bigoplus_{p}(H_{\mathbb{C}})_{p}

such that the complex weight and Hodge filtrations are given by

(3.1.1.4)

\displaystyle W_{2k}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{p\leq k}(H_{\mathbb{C}})_{p},

(3.1.1.5)

\displaystyle F^{k}

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{p\geq k}(H_{\mathbb{C}})_{p}.

Proof.

For short, we write
F^{p}
,
W_{2p}
and
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2p}
instead of
F^{p}H_{\mathbb{C}}
,
W_{2p}H_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2p}H_{\mathbb{C}}
, respectively.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii):
We have
F^{p}\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2k}:=\mathrm{Im}(F^{p}\cap W_{2k}\rightarrow\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2k})
. Since
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2p}=F^{p}\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2p}
, the canonical map
F^{p}\cap W_{2p}\rightarrow\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2p}
is surjective. Since
F^{p+1}\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2p}=0
, we have
F^{p+1}\cap W_{2p}\subset W_{2p-2}
for all
p\in\mathbb{Z}
(so that
F^{p+1}\cap W_{2p}=F^{p+1}\cap W_{2p-2}
). By the same reason,
F^{p+1}\cap W_{2p-2}\subset F^{p}\cap W_{2p-2}\subset W_{2p-4}
, and we obtain

F^{p+1}\cap W_{2p}=F^{p+1}\cap W_{2p-2}=F^{p+1}\cap W_{2p-4}\ (\subset F^{p-1}\cap W_{2p-4}\subset W_{2p-6}).

Continuing with this process, we see that
F^{p+1}\cap W_{2p}=F^{p+1}\cap W_{2(p-N)}
for every
N\geq 1
, and taking
N\gg 1
, that the canonical map
F^{p}\cap W_{2p}\rightarrow\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2p}
is injective.

The implication (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) and the equivalences (ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iv)
\Leftrightarrow
(iv’) are obvious.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii): Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. Since the natural map
F^{p-k}\cap W_{2p}\rightarrow\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2p}
has the same image as its subspace
F^{p}\cap W_{2p}
by assumption,
F^{p-k}\cap W_{2p}
is spanned by the two subspaces
F^{p}\cap W_{2p}
,
F^{p-k}\cap W_{2p-2}
whose intersection
F^{p}\cap W_{2p-2}
is zero, again by assumption. Namely, we have a direct sum decomposition:

F^{p-k}\cap W_{2p}=F^{p}\cap W_{2p}\oplus F^{p-k}\cap W_{2p-2}.

From this, we deduce that for all
p\in\mathbb{Z},k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,

\displaystyle F^{p}\cap W_{2(p+k)}

\displaystyle=F^{p+k}\cap W_{2(p+k)}\oplus F^{p}\cap W_{2(p+k-1)}

\displaystyle=F^{p+k}\cap W_{2(p+k)}\oplus F^{p+k-1}\cap W_{2(p+k-1)}\oplus F^{p}\cap W_{2(p+k-2)}=\cdots

\displaystyle=F^{p+k}\cap W_{2(p+k)}\oplus F^{p+k-1}\cap W_{2(p+k-1)}\oplus\cdots\oplus F^{p}\cap W_{2p}.

Taking
k
sufficiently large, we obtain

F^{p}=\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}F^{p+k}\cap W_{2(p+k)}.

which proves the required implications.

(iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii): Both are statements about relative position of two filtrations
F^{\bullet}
,
(W_{\bullet})_{\mathbb{C}}
defined over
\mathbb{C}
of a
\mathbb{C}
-vector space
H_{\mathbb{C}}
(for
(W_{\bullet})_{\mathbb{C}}
, we can ignore odd-degree subspaces and reindex the even-degree subspaces so that the new
W_{k}
is the original
W_{2k}
). Accordingly one can resort to the symmetry of the statements, by using the standard trick of changing a decreasing (resp. increasing) filtration to an increasing (resp. decreasing) filtration:
F^{\prime}_{k}:=W^{-2k}
,
W^{\prime 2p}:=F_{-p}
.
∎

In particular, the weight filtration of every
\mathbb{R}
-Hodge-Tate structure
has a canonical
\mathbb{C}
-splitting:

(3.1.1.6)

S_{HT}:\bigoplus_{p}\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2p}H\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bigoplus_{p}(H_{\mathbb{C}})_{p}=H_{\mathbb{C}}.

Note that while one can always find a (non-canonical)
\mathbb{R}
-splitting of a filtered
\mathbb{R}
-vector space, this
\mathbb{C}
-splitting of a
\mathbb{R}
-Hodge-Tate structure, being constructed by means of Hodge filtration, is canonical.

Definition 3.1.2
.

Let
V=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{k}
be a graded
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space. A
good
basis of
V
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-basis
\{v_{k}\}
of
V
adapted to the splitting (i.e. each
v_{k}
is an element of some
V_{n_{k}}
), and a
fine
change of good basis is a transformation
\varphi
from one good basis
\{v_{k}\}
to another good basis
\{\varphi(v_{k})\}
such that for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\varphi(v_{k})\in V_{n_{k}}
when
v_{k}\in V_{n_{k}}
.

Definition 3.1.3
.

Let
H
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge-Tate structure and
\varphi:\bigoplus_{k}\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2k}H\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}H
a
\mathbb{Q}
-splitting of the weight filtration of
H
. For each
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, choose a
\mathbb{Q}
-basis
\{v^{(k)}_{i};i=1,\cdots,r_{k}\}\ (r_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})
of
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2k}H
, so that
\{v^{(k)}_{i}\}_{i,k}
is a good basis of
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H:=\bigoplus_{k}\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2k}H
.

(1) The
period matrix

\mathcal{M}
of the splitted
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge-Tate structure
(H,\varphi)
with respect to a good basis
\{v^{(k)}_{i}\}_{i,k}
of
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H
is the transition matrix expressing the basis
\mathcal{B}_{1}=\{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}\}
of
H_{\mathbb{C}}
in terms of the basis
\mathcal{B}_{2}=\{e^{(k)}_{i}\}
, where

\lambda^{(k)}_{i}:=\varphi(v^{(k)}_{i})\in H,\quad e^{(k)}_{i}:=(2\pi i)^{-k}S_{HT}(v^{(k)}_{i})\in H_{\mathbb{C}}

That is,
\mathcal{M}
is the matrix
7
7

7

Our convention in this section for matrix expression
M
of an endomorphism
\phi\in\mathrm{End}(V)
with respect to a basis
\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}\}
of
V
is that
\phi(v_{i})=\sum_{j}M_{ij}v_{j}
(i.e. transpose of the usual matrix expression). See Example below.

of the
period operator

(3.1.3.1)

\Phi(\varphi):=J_{H}\circ S_{HT}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}\ \in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H_{\mathbb{C}})

with respect to the (same) basis
\{v^{(k)}_{i}\}_{i,k}
, where
J_{H}
is the endomorphism of
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H_{\mathbb{C}}
that acts as scalar multiplication with
(2\pi i)^{k}
on
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2k}H_{\mathbb{C}}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(2) The
Goncharov period matrix

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Gon}}
of is the matrix of the
Goncharov period operator

(3.1.3.2)

\Phi(\varphi)_{\mathrm{Gon}}:=S_{HT}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}\ \in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H_{\mathbb{C}})

with respect to the basis
\{v^{(k)}_{i}\}_{i,k}
(or equiv. the transition matrix expressing the basis
\mathcal{B}_{1}=\{\lambda^{(k)}_{i}\}
of
H_{\mathbb{C}}
in terms of the basis
\mathcal{B}_{2}^{\prime}=\{S_{HT}(v^{(k)}_{i})\}
.)

Remark 3.1.4
.

Our terminology is different from that used by Goncharov
[
Gon99
]
.
What we call period matrix and denote by
\mathcal{M}
is called canonical period matrix and denoted by
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}
by him, whereas what we call Goncharov period matrix/operator is called period matrix/operator by him:

We

Goncharov

Period operator/matrix

\mathcal{M}

Canonical period operator/matrix

\tilde{\mathcal{M}}

Goncharov period operator/matrix

\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Gon}}

Period operator/matrix

\mathcal{M}

Our choice of names/notations are due to two things: first, the “period matrix’ which is more commonly used (especially, to describe Hodge-Tate structures, as explained below), thus seems to be called by this conventional name is the period matrix in our terminology, and as such there is a danger of confusion if one follows Goncharov’s terminology. Secondly, Goncharov period operator(matrix) has its own significance and uses, thus we believe it to merit a distinguished name.

For our discussion of periods, it is convenient to use the following notational rules for matrices

Notation 3.1.5
.

(i) For
A,B\in\mathrm{M}_{N\times N}(\mathbb{C})
,
A\cdot B
is the matrix multiplication.

(ii) For
A=(A_{ij})\in\mathrm{M}_{N\times N}(\mathbb{C})
and
x=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})^{t}\in\mathrm{M}_{N\times 1}(W)
for a
\mathbb{C}
-vector space
W
(such as
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H_{\mathbb{C}}
),
A\cdot x\in\mathrm{M}_{N\times 1}(W)
is the matrix multiplication, i.e.
(A\cdot x)_{i}=\sum_{j}A_{ij}x_{j}
.

(iii) For
\phi\in\mathrm{End}(W)
and
x=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{N})^{t}\in\mathrm{M}_{N\times 1}(W)
,
\phi(x):=(\phi(x_{1}),\cdots,\phi(x_{N}))^{t}\in\mathrm{M}_{N\times 1}(W)
.

Example
Suppose
W
is a
\mathbb{C}
-vector space with a basis
\mathcal{B}=\{w_{1},\cdots,w_{N}\}
and
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathrm{End}(W)
. The matrix
M_{i}
of
\phi_{i}
with respect to
\mathcal{B}
in our convention is such that for
w:=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{N})^{t}\in\mathrm{M}_{N\times 1}(W)
,
\phi_{i}(w)=M_{i}\cdot w
, and thus

(3.1.5.1)

\phi_{1}\circ\phi_{2}(w)=\phi_{1}(M_{2}\cdot w)=M_{2}\cdot\phi_{1}(w)=M_{2}\cdot M_{1}\cdot w.

Here we are using the notations introduced above.

Now, writing simply
\{v^{(k)}_{i}\}_{i,k}=\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\}
and setting
V:=(v_{1},\cdots,v_{N})^{t}\in M_{N\times 1}(\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H_{\mathbb{C}})
, by definition of
\mathcal{M}
, we have
\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}(V)=\mathcal{M}\cdot S_{HT}\circ J_{H}^{-1}(V)
. Since
\mathcal{M}\cdot S_{HT}\circ J_{H}^{-1}(V)=S_{HT}\circ J_{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{M}\cdot V)
, we obtain
J_{H}\circ S_{HT}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}(V)=\mathcal{M}\cdot V
, showing that
\mathcal{M}
is the matrix of
\Phi(\varphi)
(
3.1.3.1
).
By (
3.1.5.1
), we have

(3.1.5.2)

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Gon}}\cdot J_{\mathcal{M}},

where
J_{\mathcal{M}}
is the matrix of
J_{H}
with respect to the same basis
\{v^{(k)}_{i}\}_{i,k}
, i.e. the block-diagonal matrix of the same block-type as
\mathcal{M}
such that the diagonal block corresponding to
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2k}
is
(2\pi i)^{k}\mathrm{Id}_{r_{i}}
.

Since
\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}
and
S_{HT}
are both splittings of the same filtration, the period matrix
\mathcal{M}
of a splitted Hodge-Tate structure defined in this way is a block upper-triangular matrix such that each block-diagonal equals the identity matrix up to a power of
2\pi i
: more precisely, if we put
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(k)}:={}^{t}(\lambda^{(k)}_{1},\cdots,\lambda^{(k)}_{r_{k}})
and
\mathbf{e}^{(k)}:={}^{t}(e^{(k)}_{1},\cdots,e^{(k)}_{r_{k}})
(column vectors), we have

(3.1.5.3)

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(0)}\\
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(1)}\\
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(2)}\\
\vdots\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\ddots&\ast&\ast&\ast&\ast\\
0&I_{r_{0}}&\ast&\ast&\ast\\
0&0&(2\pi i)I_{r_{1}}&\ast&\ast\\
0&0&0&(2\pi i)^{2}I_{r_{2}}&\ast\\
0&0&0&0&\ddots\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\vdots\\
\mathbf{e}^{(0)}\\
\mathbf{e}^{(1)}\\
\mathbf{e}^{(2)}\\
\vdots\end{array}\right).

Here, the square matrix is the period matrix
\mathcal{M}
.

From the period matrix, we can read off the Hodge-Tate structure information:
H_{\mathbb{C}}
is the
\mathbb{C}
-vector space
\mathbb{C}^{N}\ (N=\sum_{i}r_{i})
with
\{e^{(i)}_{j}\}_{i,j}
as the standard basis, and
H
is the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector subspace of
H_{\mathbb{C}}
with a basis consisting of
\lambda^{(i)}_{j}
, the
j
-th row of the weight
-2i
block of
\mathcal{M}
. The weight and Hodge filtration are given by

(3.1.5.4)

\displaystyle W_{-2k+1}H=W_{-2k}H\,

\displaystyle=\ \mathbb{Q}\left\langle\lambda^{(i)}_{1},\cdots,\lambda^{(i)}_{r_{i}}\,;\,i\geq k\right\rangle\quad\subset H

\displaystyle F^{-p}H_{\mathbb{C}}\,

\displaystyle=\ \mathbb{C}\left\langle e^{(i)}_{1},\cdots,e^{(i)}_{r_{i}}\,;\,i\leq p\right\rangle\quad\subset H_{\mathbb{C}};

so,
W_{-2k}H_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}\left\langle e^{(i)}_{1},\cdots,e^{(i)}_{r_{i}}\,;\,i\geq k\right\rangle
and

(3.1.5.5)

F^{-k}H_{\mathbb{C}}\cap W_{-2k}H_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}\langle e^{(k)}_{1},\cdots,e^{(k)}_{r_{k}}\rangle.

Conversely, by this recipe (
3.1.5.4
), any such matrix
\mathcal{M}
(
3.1.5.3
) determines a
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge-Tate structure
H
; every
H
constructed in this way is already equipped with a
\mathbb{Q}
-splitting (provided by the map
v^{(i)}_{j}\mapsto\lambda^{(i)}_{j}
).
Also, note that with a fixed choice of a basis
\{v^{(k)}_{i}\}_{i,k}
of
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H
, a different choice of a
\mathbb{Q}
-splitting of the weight filtration of
H
corresponds to
N\mathcal{M}
for a block-unipotent matrix
N
of the same block-type as
\mathcal{M}
which has
rational
coefficients.

This discussion establishes the following well-known statement (cf.
[
Hai94
, Prop.9.1]
):

Proposition 3.1.6
.

The set of all Hodge-Tate structures whose weight graded quotients are isomorphic to
H_{0}=\bigoplus_{k}\mathbb{Q}(k)^{r_{k}}
via a fixed isomorphism is the quotient
U(\mathbb{Q})\backslash M(\mathbb{C})
of the set
M(\mathbb{C})
of complex upper triangular matrices of the form (
3.1.5.3
) by the subgroup
U(\mathbb{Q})
of
\mathrm{GL}_{r}(\mathbb{Q})
of block-unipotent matrices of same block-type as (
3.1.5.3
), where
r=\sum r_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
.

In particular, attaching to a period matrix
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\omega\\
0&(2\pi i)^{n}\end{array}\right)
the element
\omega\ \text{mod}\,(2\pi i)^{n}\mathbb{Q}
of
\mathbb{C}/(2\pi i)^{n}\mathbb{Q}
gives a canonical isomorphism

(3.1.6.1)

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{MH}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))=\mathbb{C}/(2\pi i)^{n}\mathbb{Q}\quad(n>0).

Proof.

We just need to prove the formula (
3.1.6.1
). We have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{MH}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))

\displaystyle=\ U(\mathbb{Q})\backslash M(\mathbb{C})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\mathbb{Q}\\
0&1\end{array}\right)\backslash\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\mathbb{C}\\
0&(2\pi i)^{n}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=\ \mathbb{C}/(2\pi i)^{n}\mathbb{Q}.\qquad\qed

See Example 23 of
[
Hai03
]
for an explicit geometric description of the simple extension Hodge-Tate structure corresponding to an element of
\mathbb{C}/(2\pi i)^{n}\mathbb{Q}
.

3.2  Period of framed Hodge-Tate structure: Big period and skew-symmetric period

A period of a Hodge structure is an entry of a period matrix, so is highly choice-sensitive. But, for framed Hodge-Tate structures, Goncharov
[
Gon99
]
found some canonically defined period (“big period”), at the cost of enlarging the domain where the period lives.
For our purpose of interpreting Chern-Simons invariant of hyperbolic three-manifold as the (canonical) period of some Hodge-Tate structure in
\mathrm{Ext}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(2))
, we introduce a variant of the big period: “skew-symmetric period”.

We give the definition of maximal period of splitted
n
-framed Hodge-Tate structures first and then that of big period of
n
-framed Hodge-Tate structures.

Definition 3.2.1
.

Let
(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
An
(m,n)
-framing
on a Hodge-Tate structure
H
over
\mathbb{Q}
is
a choice of a non-zero vector in
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2m}H
and a non-zero covector
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2n}H\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
.

We call a
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge-Tate structure
H

(m,n)
-framed
if it is endowed with an
(m,n)
-framing.
We remark on two things. First, while this definition itself makes sense for every integer pairs
(m,n)
, the case
m>n
will be of little use for our purpose. Secondly, a
(0,n)
-framing is often called simply
n
-framing. In literature, there exist some different conventions for the definition of
n
-framing (
[
Gon99
, p.589]
,
[
Bro13
, Def.5.1]
,
[
GZ18
,
\lx@sectionsign
1.2]
), all of which are related to each other by Tate twist with
\mathbb{Q}(n)
or
\mathbb{Q}(-n)
: our definition of
n
-framing is the same as that used in
[
Bro13
, Def.5.1]
and
[
GZ18
,
\lx@sectionsign
1.2]
, while an
n
-framed Hodge-Tate structure
H^{\prime}
in the sense of
[
Gon99
]
equals the Tate-twist
H(-n)
of an
n
-framed Hodge-Tate structure
H
in our sense.

Given a vector space
V
over a field
k
, for
v\in v
,
f\in V^{\vee}:=\mathrm{Hom}(V,k)
and
\phi\in\mathrm{End}(V)
, we use the notation

\langle v|\phi|f\rangle:=f(\phi(v)).

If we choose a basis
\mathcal{B}=\{v_{i}\}
of
V
such that
v\in\mathcal{B}
and
f\in\mathcal{B}^{\vee}
(dual basis), and
M
is the matrix of
\phi
with respect to
\mathcal{B}
, then
\langle v|\phi|f\rangle
is just the entry of
M
in the position
(v,w)
, where
w\in\mathcal{B}
is dual to
f
(recall that the rows and columns of
M
are indexed by elements of
\mathcal{B}
).

Definition 3.2.2
.

[
Gon99
,
\lx@sectionsign
4.1]

Let
(H,v_{m},f^{n})
be an
(m,n)
-framed
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge-Tate structure,
\varphi:\bigoplus_{k}\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2k}H\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}H
a
\mathbb{Q}
-splitting of the weight filtration of
H
; let
\Phi_{\mathrm{Gon}}(\varphi)=S_{HT}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}\in\mathrm{Aut}(\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H_{\mathbb{C}})
be the Goncharov period operator (
3.1.3.2
) defined by
\varphi
.

The
maximal period
of the splitted
(m,n)
-framed
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge-Tate structure
((H,v_{m},f^{n});\varphi)
is

p((H,v_{m},f^{n});\varphi):=\langle v_{m}|\Phi_{\mathrm{Gon}}(\varphi)|f^{n}\rangle\ \in\mathbb{C},

where
f^{n}
is extended to a map
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
by setting
f^{n}|_{\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2i}}=0
for
i\neq n
.

We have more explicit descriptions for this maximal period.

Lemma 3.2.3
.

(1) Let
\Phi_{\mathrm{Gon}}(m,n)
be the
(m,n)
-component of
\Phi_{\mathrm{Gon}}(\varphi)
in the decomposition
\mathrm{End}(\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H)=\bigoplus_{(m,n)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2m}H_{\mathbb{C}},\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2n}H_{\mathbb{C}})
; so, the maximal period
p((H,v_{m},f^{n});\varphi)
equals the image of
1
under the composite map
\mathbb{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle v_{m}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2m}H_{\mathbb{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi_{\mathrm{Gon}}(m,n)}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2n}H_{\mathbb{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f^{n}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{C}
.

The map
\Phi_{\mathrm{Gon}}(m,n)
is given by the composite map

\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2m}H_{\mathbb{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}W_{-2m}H_{\mathbb{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:can_SP_WF})}}{{=}}\bigoplus_{p\leq-m}(H_{\mathbb{C}})_{p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle pr}}{{\longrightarrow}}(H_{\mathbb{C}})_{-n}=\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2n}H_{\mathbb{C}}.

(3) Suppose chosen a good basis of
\mathcal{B}=\{v^{(k)}_{i}\}_{k,i}
of
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H
such that
v_{m}\in\mathcal{B}
and
f^{n}\in\mathcal{B}^{\vee}
(dual basis). The maximal period
p((H,v_{m},f^{n});\varphi)
equals the entry of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Gon}}=\mathcal{M}J_{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}
in the position
(v_{m},v_{n})
, where
v_{n}\in\mathcal{B}
is dual to
f^{n}
.

Let
V
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space. For
v\in V
,
f\in V^{\vee}
and
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathrm{End}(V_{\mathbb{C}})
, we define

(3.2.3.1)

\langle v|\phi_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\phi_{2}|f\rangle:=\sum_{k}\langle v|\phi_{1}|f^{k}\rangle\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\langle v_{k}|\phi_{2}|f\rangle\ \in\ \mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C},

where
\{v_{k}\}
is an arbitrary
\mathbb{Q}
-basis of
V
with dual basis
\{f^{k}\}
: it follows from (
2.3.0.5
) that this expression is independent of the choice of basis
\{v_{k}\}
over
\mathbb{Q}
(but not necessarily so for a basis over
\mathbb{C}
).

Definition 3.2.4
.

[
Gon99
,
\lx@sectionsign
4.3]

Let
(H,v_{m},f^{n})
be an
(m,n)
-framed
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge-Tate structure, and let

\Phi_{\mathrm{Gon}}(\varphi)=S_{HT}^{-1}\circ\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}
be the Goncharov period operator (
3.1.3.2
) defined by a
\mathbb{Q}
-splitting
\varphi:\bigoplus_{k}\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2k}H\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}H
.

The
big period
of
(H,v_{m},f^{n})
is

(3.2.4.1)

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{n}((H,v_{m},f^{n})):=

\displaystyle\ \langle v_{m}|\Phi_{\mathrm{Gon}}(\varphi)^{-1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\Phi_{\mathrm{Gon}}(\varphi)|f^{n}\rangle\quad\in\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{v_{k}\in\mathcal{B}}\langle v_{m}|\Phi_{\mathrm{Gon}}(\varphi)^{-1}|f^{k}\rangle\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\langle v_{k}|\Phi_{\mathrm{Gon}}(\varphi)|f^{n}\rangle,

where
\mathcal{B}=\{v_{k}\}
is an arbitrary basis of
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H
with dual basis
\mathcal{B}^{\vee}=\{f^{k}\}
such that
v_{m}\in\mathcal{B}
and
f^{n}\in\mathcal{B}^{\vee}
.

Lemma 3.2.5
.

Let
(H,v_{m},f^{n})
be an
(m,n)
-framed
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge-Tate structure.
Choose a splitting
\varphi:\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}H
, a good basis
\mathcal{B}=\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{N}\}
of
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H
such that
v_{m}\in\mathcal{B}
and
f^{n}\in\mathcal{B}^{\vee}
; let
\mathcal{M}
be the associated period matrix (
3.1.3.1
).
Then, the big period
\mathscr{P}_{n}((H,v_{m},f^{n}))
equals

\sum_{k}(2\pi i)^{m}\langle v_{m}|\mathcal{M}^{-1}|f^{k}\rangle\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}(2\pi i)^{-n}\langle v_{k}|\mathcal{M}|f^{n}\rangle,

i.e. is the matrix tensor-product (
2.3.0.4
) of the two matrices in
\mathrm{M}_{1\times N}(\mathbb{C})
,
\mathrm{M}_{N\times 1}(\mathbb{C})
:

(2\pi i)^{m}\left((\mathcal{M}^{-1})_{m,1},\cdots,(\mathcal{M}^{-1})_{m,N}\right),\ (2\pi i)^{-n}\left(\mathcal{M}_{1,n},\cdots,\mathcal{M}_{N,n}\right)^{t}.

This is obvious from definition and the relation
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Gon}}=\mathcal{M}J_{\mathcal{M}}^{-1}
(
3.1.5.2
).

Proposition 3.2.6
.

The big period
\mathscr{P}_{n}
does not depend on the choice of the splitting
\varphi
.

Proof.

This is Prop. 4.2 of
[
Gon99
]
. Indeed, this follows from Lemma
3.2.5
, the fact that the period matrix corresponding to a different splitting is
N\mathcal{M}
for an upper-triangular block-unipotent matrix
N
with rational entries, and the identity (
2.3.0.5
).
∎

We introduce another period which is derived from the big period.

Definition 3.2.7
.

The
skew-symmetric period
of an
n
-framed
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge-Tate structure
(H,v_{0},f^{n})
is the image of the big period
\mathscr{P}_{n}((H,v_{0},f^{n}))
(
3.2.4.1
) under the skew-symmetrization
\mathrm{Alt}:\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\wedge_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}\ :\ x\otimes y\mapsto x\wedge y
:

(3.2.7.1)

\mathscr{A}_{n}((H,v_{0},f^{n})):=\mathrm{Alt}\left(\langle v_{0}|\Phi_{\mathrm{Gon}}(\varphi)^{-1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\Phi_{\mathrm{Gon}}(\varphi)|f_{n}\rangle\right)\in\mathbb{C}\wedge_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}

(for any choice of a
\mathbb{Q}
-splitting
\varphi:\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}H
).

3.3  Example: Polylogarithm Hodge-Tate structure
[
BD94
]

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
k
-th polylogarithm function
\mathrm{Li}_{k}(z)
is an analytic function on
|z|<1
defined by

(3.3.0.1)

\mathrm{Li}_{k}(z)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{n^{k}}.

These are iterated integrals of differential forms with logarithmic poles on
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\backslash\{0,1,\infty\}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=-\log(1-z)=\int_{0}^{z}\frac{dt}{1-t},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{k+1}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{z}\mathrm{Li}_{k}(t)\frac{dt}{t}\ \quad(k\geq 1).

This expression as iterated integrals shows that
\mathrm{Li}_{k}(z)
, defined by (
5.1.0.1
) for
|z|<1
, can be analytically continued as a
multivalued
function on
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\backslash\{0,1,\infty\}
.

The
n
-th polylogarithm Hodge-Tate structure (
n\in\mathbb{N}
) is the Hodge-Tate structure of dimension
n+1
whose period matrix (Definition
3.1.3
, (
3.1.5.3
)) is

(3.3.0.2)

\mathcal{P}^{(n)}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}1&-\mathrm{Li}_{1}(z)&-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)&\cdots&\cdots&-\mathrm{Li}_{n}(z)\\
0&2\pi i&2\pi i\log z&2\pi i\frac{(\log z)^{2}}{2}&\cdots&2\pi i\frac{(\log z)^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\\
\vdots&0&(2\pi i)^{2}&(2\pi i)^{2}\log z&&(2\pi i)^{2}\frac{(\log z)^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}\\
&&&\ddots&&\vdots\\
0&&&&&(2\pi i)^{n-1}\log z\\
0&&&&&(2\pi i)^{n}\end{array}\right);

If
\{e_{0},\cdots,e_{n}\}
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{Q}^{n+1}
and
\lambda_{i}(z)\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\ (0\leq i\leq n)
is the
i
-th
8
8

8

We index the rows and columns from
0
to
n
.

row of
\mathcal{P}^{(n)}
, this Hodge-Tate structure
H
is given by
H=\mathbb{Q}\langle\lambda_{0}(z),\cdots,\lambda_{n+1}(z)\rangle\subset H_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathbb{C}^{n+1}=\oplus_{i=0}^{n}\mathbb{C}e_{i}
(
3.1.5.3
) with the weight and Hodge filtrations (
3.1.5.4
).
Note that each weight graded quotient
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{2k}
is one-dimensional and there exists a natural
n
-framing:

v_{0}:=\text{image of }\lambda_{0}(z)\text{ in }\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{0}H,\quad f^{n}\in(\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2n}H)^{\vee}=\mathbb{Q}\langle(2\pi i)^{n}e_{n}\rangle^{\vee}\ (f^{n}((2\pi i)^{n}e_{n})=1).

We compute the big and skew-symmetric period of this framed Hodge-Tate structure in the case
n=2
. Consider a general period matrix (with its inverse showing first on the left):

(3.3.0.3)

\mathcal{M}(z)^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&-\frac{a}{2\pi i}&\frac{ab-c}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\\
0&\frac{1}{2\pi i}&\frac{-b}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\\
0&0&\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\end{array}\right);\quad\mathcal{M}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&a&c\\
0&2\pi i&2\pi i\,b\\
0&0&(2\pi i)^{2}\end{array}\right)

By Lemma
3.2.5
, the big period
\mathscr{P}_{2}
of this framed Hodge-Tate structure is the matrix tensor-multiplication of the first row
\mathcal{M}^{-1}
and
(2\pi i)^{-2}
times the third column of
\mathcal{M}
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{2}(\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(z))=

\displaystyle 1\otimes\frac{c}{(2\pi i)^{2}}-\frac{a}{2\pi i}\otimes\frac{b}{2\pi i}+\frac{ab-c}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\otimes 1,

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{2}(\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(z))=

\displaystyle 1\wedge\frac{2c-ab}{(2\pi i)^{2}}-\frac{a}{2\pi i}\wedge\frac{b}{2\pi i}.

So, for the framed polylogarithm Hodge-Tate structure of order
2

(3.3.0.4)

\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&-\mathrm{Li}_{1}(z)&-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)\\
0&2\pi i&2\pi i\,\log z\\
0&0&(2\pi i)^{2}\end{array}\right),

we have (
a=\log(1-z)
,
b=\log(z)
,
c=-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)
)

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}_{2}(\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(z))=

\displaystyle 1\otimes\frac{-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)}{(2\pi i)^{2}}-\frac{\log(1-z)}{2\pi i}\otimes\frac{\log(z)}{2\pi i}+\frac{\log(1-z)\log z+\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\otimes\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}},

(3.3.0.5)

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{2}(\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(z))=

\displaystyle 1\wedge\frac{-2}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\left(\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\log(1-z)\log(z)\right)+\frac{\log(z)}{2\pi i}\wedge\frac{\log(1-z)}{2\pi i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}([z])

for the (unnormalized) Bloch regulator
\tilde{\rho}([z])
(
2.4.0.1
), (
2.4.0.2
); this last equality is the reason for us having introduced the notion of skew-symmetric period!

As pointed by Goncharov
[
Gon99
,
\lx@sectionsign
4.5]
, the original Bloch regulator
[
Blo00
, Lem.6.1.1]
:

(3.3.0.6)

2\pi i\otimes\exp(\frac{-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)}{2\pi i})-\log(1-z)\otimes z

is the image of the big period
\mathscr{P}_{2}(\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(z))
under the homomorphism

\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}^{\ast};\ x\otimes y\mapsto 2\pi i\cdot x\otimes\exp(2\pi i\cdot y).

Proposition 3.3.1
.

The skew-symmetric period of the polylogarithm Hodge-Tate structure
\mathcal{M}^{(2)}(z)
of order
2
equals the image
[z]\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{C})
under the Bloch regulator
\tilde{\rho}(z)
(
2.4.0.1
).

3.4  Hodge-Tate Hopf algebra

Let
T
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear abelian category and
\omega:T\rightarrow\mathrm{Vec}_{\mathbb{Q}}
a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear exact faithful functor. For an object
X
of
T
, let
\langle X\rangle
be the strictly full subcategory of
T
whose objects are those isomorphic to a subquotient of
X^{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and let
\mathrm{End}(\omega|_{\langle X\rangle})
denote the endomorphism functor of
\omega|_{\langle X\rangle}
(it is the largest
\mathbb{Q}
-subalgebra of
\mathrm{End}(\omega(X))
stabilizng
\omega(Y)
for all
Y\subset X^{n}
). Then,
\omega
identifies
T
with the category of finite-dimensional left
\mathrm{End}(\omega)
-modules, where
\mathrm{End}(\omega):=\varprojlim\mathrm{End}(\omega|_{\langle X\rangle})
(the endomorphism functor of
\omega
),
[
DM82
, II. 2.13]
. The latter is also equivalent to the category of finite-dimensional right
\mathrm{End}(\omega)^{\vee}
-comodules for the coalgebra
\mathrm{End}(\omega)^{\vee}:=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}-\mathrm{vec}}(\mathrm{End}(\omega),\mathbb{Q})
(loc. cit. II. Prop. 2.14).
In particular, this is true for any neutral Tannakian category, in which case
\mathrm{End}(\omega)
is further a cocommutative Hopf algebra.

Now, for the
\mathbb{Q}
-linear Tannakian category
\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}
of Hodge-Tate
\mathbb{Q}
-structures, every object
H
has a canonical weight filtration
W_{\bullet}H
such that morphisms in the category are strictly compatible with the weight filtration, and there exists a fibre functor
\omega
, now to the category of
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathbb{Q}
-vector spaces:

(3.4.0.1)

\omega(H)=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\omega(H)_{n}:=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Q}(n),\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2n}H),

In this case, it turns out that there exists an explicit description of the graded Hopf algebra
\mathrm{End}(\omega)^{\vee}
in terms of framed mixed Hodge-Tate structures
up to some equivalence relation
. Here, we review this theory. For details, see
[
BMS87
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.1]
(the original source),
[
BGSV90
,
\lx@sectionsign
1]
,
[
Gon98
,
\lx@sectionsign
3]
,
[
Gon99
,
\lx@sectionsign
3.2]
,
[
Gon05
, Appendix.A]
.

This equivalence relation is defined to be the coarsest equivalence relation
\sim
on the set of
n
-framed Hodge-Tate structures for which two
n
-framed Hodge-Tate structures
H
,
H^{\prime}
are
equivalent
if there exists a morphism
H\rightarrow H^{\prime}
of mixed
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge structures preserving
n
-framings. One can show (
[
BGSV90
, 1.3.4]
) that every
n
-framed Hodge-Tate structure
H
is equivalent to
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{0}H/\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2n}H
, i.e. to an
n
-framed Hodge-Tate structure
H^{\prime}
such that
H^{\prime}=W_{\geq 0}H^{\prime}
and
W_{<-2n}H^{\prime}=0
, and further to an
n
-framed Hodge-Tate structure
H^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{0}H^{\prime\prime}\simeq\mathbb{Q}(0)
and
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2n}H^{\prime\prime}\simeq\mathbb{Q}(n)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{n}
be the set of equivalence classes of
n
-framed Hodge-Tate structures; we denote the class of
n
-framed Hodge structure
(H,v_{0},f^{n})
by
[H,v_{0},f^{n}]
. This set
\mathcal{H}_{n}
has a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space structure defined as follows (
[
Gon98
, p.166]
):

(H,v_{0},f^{n})+(H^{\prime},v_{0}^{\prime},{f^{n}}^{\prime})=(H\oplus H^{\prime},v_{0}+v_{0}^{\prime},f^{n}+{f^{n}}^{\prime});\quad c(H,v_{0},f^{n})=(H,v_{0},cf^{n}).

The zero object is
H=\mathbb{Q}(0)\oplus\mathbb{Q}(n)
with the obvious
n
-framing.

The tensor product of mixed Hodge structures induce a commutative multiplication on the graded
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}:=\bigoplus_{n}\mathcal{H}_{n}
:

\mu:\mathcal{H}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{q}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{p+q}.

More interestingly, one can define a comultiplication on
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
:

(3.4.0.2)

\nu=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}\nu_{pq}\ :\ \mathcal{H}_{n}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{p+q=n}\mathcal{H}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{q}.

We define its component
\nu_{pq}:\mathcal{H}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{p}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{q}
as follows: Choose a basis
\{b_{i}\}
of
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Q}(p),\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2p}H)
and let
\{b_{i}^{\vee}\}\subset\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2p}H,\mathbb{Q}(p))
be the dual basis. Then, for
[H,v_{0},f^{n}]\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and every
i
, the datum
(v_{0},b_{i}^{\vee})
is a
p
-framing on
H
. Further,
(b_{i}(-p),f^{n}(-p))
is a
q
-framing on
H(-p)
, since

(3.4.0.3)

\displaystyle b_{i}(-p)\in\ \mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Q}(p),\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2p}H)(-p)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{0}(H(-p))),

\displaystyle f^{n}(-p)\in\ \mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2n}H,\mathbb{Q}(n))(-p)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2q}(H(-p)),\mathbb{Q}(q)).

In view of this, it makes sense to define

(3.4.0.4)

\nu_{pq}([H,v_{0},f^{n}]):=\sum_{i}[H,v_{0},b_{i}^{\vee}]\otimes[H,b_{i},f^{n}](-p).

(Here and later, we write
[H,b_{i},f^{n}](-p)
for the equivalence class
[(H,b_{i},f^{n})(-p)]
of the Tate-twisted
(n-p)
-framed Hodge-Tate structure
(H,b_{i},f^{n})(-p):=(H(-p),b_{i}(-p),f^{n}(-p))
, by abuse of notation.)

Lemma 3.4.1
.

This definition of
\nu_{pq}
is independent of the choice of the basis
\{e_{i}\}
and also depends only on the equivalence class of framed Hodge-Tate structures, so that it gives a well-defined group homomorphism (
3.4.0.2
). It is furthermore an algebra homomorphism (i.e.
\nu(a\cdot b)=\nu(a)\cdot\nu(b)
for
a\cdot b:=\mu(a\otimes b)
).

The abelian group
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
has a structure of graded Hopf algebra with the commutative multiplication
\mu
and the comultiplication
\nu
.

See
[
Gon99
, 3.5]
for a proof.

As
\omega
is a fibre functor to the category of graded vector spaces,
\mathrm{End}(\omega)
has a natural grading
\mathrm{End}(\omega)=\bigoplus_{n\leq 0}\mathrm{End}(\omega)_{n}
with
\mathrm{End}(\omega)_{n}:=\{r\in\mathrm{End}(\omega)\ |\ r_{H}(\omega(H)_{k})\subset\omega(H)_{k-n},\ \forall H\in\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT},k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, where
r_{H}\in\mathrm{End}(\omega(H))
denotes the value on
\omega(H)
of the endomorphism functor
r
. The linear dual
\mathrm{End}(\omega)^{\vee}=\bigoplus_{n}\mathrm{End}(\omega)_{n}^{\vee}
is a graded Hopf algebra. Another way to look at this structure is to observe that
\mathrm{End}(\omega)
is canonically isomorphic to the universal enveloping algebra of the Lie algebra
L(\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT})
of derivations of
\omega
:

L(\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}):=\{E\in\mathrm{End}(\omega)\ |\ E_{H_{1}\otimes H_{2}}=E_{H_{1}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{H_{2}}+\mathrm{id}_{H_{1}}\otimes E_{H_{2}}\},

As
L(\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT})
itself has a similar non-positive grading, so does the universal enveloping algebra, which thus has a structure of a graded Hopf algebra.

Theorem 3.4.2
.

The
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
is canonically isomorphic, as graded Hopf algebra, to the linear dual
R^{\vee}
of
R:=\mathrm{End}(\omega)
by the map which to
[H,v_{0}\in\omega(H)_{0},f^{n}\in\omega(H)_{n}^{\vee}]\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
attaches the dual element in
(\mathrm{End}(\omega)_{-n})^{\vee}:=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{End}(\omega)_{-n},\mathbb{Q})
given by

(3.4.2.1)

\mathrm{End}(\omega)_{-n}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}\,:\,r\mapsto f^{n}(r_{H}\cdot v_{0}).

See
[
Gon98
, 3.3]
,
[
Gon05
, A.2]
for a proof. The unit is
[\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{Q}(0)},\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{Q}(0)}]\in\mathcal{H}_{0}
, from which via the theorem, we obtain an augmentation
\epsilon:R\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
; clearly this also equals the ring homomorphism
r\mapsto r|_{\mathbb{Q}(0)}\in\mathrm{End}(\mathbb{Q}(0))=\mathbb{Q}
.

We generalize slightly more the notion of
n
-framing on a Hodge-Tate structure. For two integers
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we consider Hodge-Tate structures
H
framed by two non-zero morphisms
v_{m}\in\omega(H)_{m}=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Q}(m),\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2m}H)
,
f^{n}\in\omega(H)_{n}^{\vee}=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2n}H,\mathbb{Q}(n))
; for short, we call it
(m,n)
-framed Hodge-Tate structure. Obviously, the previous discussion for
n
-framed Hodge-Tate structures applies to this notion as well, cf.
[
Gon98
,
\lx@sectionsign
3]
.

Definition 3.4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{H}(m,n)
be the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space of equivalence classes of
(m,n)
-framed Hodge-Tate structures.
For
n\geq m
, we let
\varphi:\mathcal{H}(m,n)\rightarrow(\mathrm{End}(\omega)_{m-n})^{\vee}
be the canonical morphism defined by:

\varphi([H,v_{m},f^{n}]):r\in\mathrm{End}(\omega)_{m-n}\mapsto\langle f^{n},r_{H}(v_{m})\rangle\in\mathbb{Q}.

Via Theorem
3.4.2
,
\varphi
is regarded as a map
\mathcal{H}(m,n)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{n-m}
.

Like
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
, the bi-graded space
\underline{\mathcal{H}}:=\bigoplus_{n\geq m}\mathcal{H}(m,n)
has a structure of
\mathbb{Q}
-coagebra; this is a “mixed colagebra” in the sense of
[
BMS87
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.1]
.

In the following, we will view a comodule map
\varphi:V\rightarrow V\otimes\mathcal{H}
for a Hopf algebra
\mathcal{H}
equivalently as a linear map
\varphi^{\prime}:V\otimes V^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
(satisfying some properties) defined by that
\varphi^{\prime}(v,f)=\langle f,\varphi(v)\rangle
for
v\in V
and
f\in V^{\vee}
.

Corollary 3.4.4
.

[
BGSV90
, 1.6]
,
[
Gon99
, 3.6]

The category
\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}
of
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge-Tate structures is canonically equivalent to the category of finite-dimensional graded right
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
-comodules by the map which assigns to a Hodge-Tate structure
H
the graded comodule
\omega(H)=\bigoplus_{n}\omega(H)_{n}
, with
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
-coaction
\omega(H)\otimes\omega(H)^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
being defined by that
v_{m}\otimes f^{n}\in\omega(H)_{m}\otimes\omega(H)_{n}^{\vee}
maps to
\varphi([H,v_{m},f^{n}])
if
n\geq m
, and to zero otherwise, where
\varphi:\mathcal{H}(m,n)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{n-m}
is the map from Definition
3.4.3
.

In particular, the equivalence class
[H,v_{0},f^{n}]\in\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
of an
n
-framed Hodge-Tate structure
(H,v_{0},f^{n})
is determined by the associated
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
-comodule structure
\nu_{H}:\omega(H)\otimes\omega(H)^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
:

[H,v_{0},f^{n}]=\nu_{H}(v_{0}\otimes f^{n})\ \in\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}.

Proof.

(of Corollary)
The
\mathbb{Q}
-linear abelian category
(\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT},\widetilde{\omega})
(
\widetilde{\omega}
being the same fiber functor
\omega
, except for forgetting the grading) is identified with the abelian category of finite-dimensional left
R=\mathrm{End}(\omega)
-modules.
For a general
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
R
, the latter category is equivalent to the category of finite-dimensional right
R^{\vee}
-comodules, via the bijections
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}}(R\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}V,V)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}}(V,\mathrm{Hom}(R,V))\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Q}}(V,V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}R^{\vee})
for
R
-modules
V
. Let
\Delta_{V}:V\rightarrow V\otimes R^{\vee}
be the comodule map corresponding to a given
R
-module
\rho_{V}:R\otimes V\rightarrow V
; then, there exists the relation:

(3.4.4.1)

(f,\Delta_{V}(v))(r)=\langle f,\rho_{V}(r\otimes v)\rangle

for every
r\in R
,
v\in V
,
f\in V^{\vee}
. The same correspondence induced by
\omega
(
3.4.0.1
) is further an equivalence of tensor categories between
\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}
and the tensor category of finite-dimensional graded right
R^{\vee}
-comodules for the graded Hopf algebra
R^{\vee}
, (cf. the proof of Theorem II. 2.11 in
[
DM82
]
).

So, to prove the corollary, we have to show that for every Hodge-Tate structure
H
(so, being endowed with
\mathrm{End}(\omega)
-action
\rho_{\omega(H)}:\mathrm{End}(\omega)\otimes\omega(H)\rightarrow\omega(H)
), the associated comodule structure
\Delta_{\omega(H)}:\omega(H)\otimes\omega(H)^{\vee}\rightarrow R^{\vee}
equals the comodule structure described in the statement, namely the map
\omega(H)\otimes\omega(H)^{\vee}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}\,:\,(v_{m},f^{n})\mapsto\varphi([H,v_{m},f^{n}])
if
n\geq m
, or zero if
n<m
, via the map

\mathcal{H}_{n-m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi}}{{\rightarrow}}(R_{m-n})^{\vee}
(Definition
3.4.3
).
But, by (
3.4.4.1
) applied to
V=\omega(H)
, we see that

\Delta_{\omega(H)}(v_{m},f^{n})(r)=\langle f^{n},\rho_{\omega(H)}(r\otimes v_{m})\rangle=\langle f^{n},r_{H}\cdot v_{m}\rangle,

for every
v_{m}\in\omega(H)_{m}
,
f^{n}\in\omega(H)_{n}^{\vee}
, and
r\in R=\mathrm{End}(\omega)
. Note that
r_{H}\cdot v_{m}\in\bigoplus_{l\geq m}\omega(H)_{l}
since
r
preserves the weight filtrations of Hodge-Tate structures, so that
f^{n}(r_{H}\cdot v_{m})
is zero if
n<m
, and also that
\Delta_{\omega(H)}(v_{m},f^{n})\in(R_{m-n})^{\vee}
.
So, the claim follows from Definition
3.4.3
.
∎

Lemma 3.4.5
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the pure Hodge-Tate structure
\mathbb{Q}(n)

corresponds, via Corollary
3.4.4
, to the one-dimensional
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space
\mathbb{Q}
located in degree
-n
with
R
-module structure being given by the augmentation
\epsilon\in R^{\vee}
; we denote this graded
R
-module by
\mathbb{Q}_{(n)}
.

Clearly, a mixed Hodge structure
H
which is a simple extension of
\mathbb{Q}(0)
by
\mathbb{Q}(n)\ (n\geq 0)
in the abelian category of mixed Hodge structures (i.e. a short exact sequence
0\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(n)\rightarrow H\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(0)\rightarrow 0
) has a natural structure of
n
-framed Hodge-Tate structure, providing a monomorphism

(3.4.5.1)

\iota_{n}:\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{n}.

(The left group is also equals
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{MH}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))

[
Gon99
, (6)]
, since
\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}
is closed under extension.)

Remark 3.4.6
.

The dual Hopf algebra
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}^{\vee}\simeq\bigoplus_{n}\mathcal{H}_{n}^{\vee}
is a universal enveloping algebra of a Lie algebra, a free pro-nilpotent graded Lie algebra
L
with generators in degree
-n
equal to
(\mathbb{C}/(2\pi i)^{n}\mathbb{Q})^{\vee}
, cf.
[
BGSV90
, Lem.1.4.3]
,
[
DG05
,
\lx@sectionsign
2]
.

Let
\bar{\nu}:=\nu|_{\mathcal{H}_{>0}}-1\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{H}_{>0}}-\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{H}_{>0}}\otimes 1
be the reduced comultiplication.
Then, clearly, the image of the inclusion
\iota_{n}:
(
3.4.5.1
) is contained in
\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\nu}|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}}:\mathcal{H}_{n}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{k+n=n,k,l>0}\mathcal{H}_{k}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{l})
, since any
H\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{MH}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))
has
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2k}H=0
for all
0<k<n
.

Proposition 3.4.7
.

The image of
\iota_{n}
is
\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\nu}|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}})
.

In particular, there exists a natural bijection
\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\nu}|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))
.
The mere existence of such a natural bijection can be deduced rather quickly from the following general facts: for an arbitrary augmented, graded Hopf algebra
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
over a field
k
and any graded
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
-comodule
N
, there exists a canonical bijection

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}\mhyphen\text{comod}}(k,N)\cong H^{1}((co\bar{B}^{\ast}(\mathcal{H}_{\bullet})\otimes N)_{0})

between the Yoneda extension group of graded
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
-comodules and the degree-
1
cohomology group of the degree
0
-part of the tensor product (complex) of
N
and the
reduced cobar complex

co\bar{B}^{\ast}(\mathcal{H}_{\bullet})
of the Hopf algebra
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
:

\mathbb{Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{H}_{>0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\bar{\nu}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{H}_{>0}^{\otimes 2}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}_{>0}^{\otimes 3}\longrightarrow\cdots.

When
N
is
k_{(n)}
, i.e. the comodule
k
(via the augmentation
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}\rightarrow k
) located in degree
-n
, it is clear that

H^{1}((co\bar{B}^{\ast}(\mathcal{H}_{\bullet})\otimes N)_{0})=\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\nu}|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}}).

Now, when
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
is the Hodge-Tate Hopf algebra, the comodule
N
corresponding to the Hodge-Tate structure
\mathbb{Q}(n)
is the comodule
\mathbb{Q}_{(n)}
(Lemma
3.4.5
), and also by Corollary
3.4.4
, we have
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}\mhyphen\text{comod}}(k,N)=\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))
. That is, we have a natural bijection:

(3.4.7.1)

\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\nu}|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}})=H^{1}((co\bar{B}^{\ast}(\mathcal{H}_{\bullet})\otimes\mathbb{Q}_{(n)})_{0})\cong\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}\mhyphen\text{comod}}(k,\mathbb{Q}(n))\cong\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n)).

But, in our case, the construction of
\mathcal{H}_{\bullet}
in terms of equivalence classes of framed Hodge-Tate structures provides another, a priori different, map
\iota_{n}:\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\nu}|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}})
(
3.4.5.1
), and the proposition asserts the stronger statement
9
9

9

We will need this stronger fact in the proof of Theorem
4.2.4
: see the last paragraph of that proof.

that
\iota_{n}

is
the bijection (
3.4.7.1
), namely that for every element
\gamma
of
\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\nu}|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}})
, the simple extension in
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))
constructed by the canonical bijection (
3.4.7.1
) has the equivalence class (i.e. image under
\iota_{n}
)
\gamma\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
. We verify this non-obvious statement, by unravelling the constructions involved in (
3.4.7.1
).

Proof.

For the first preliminary step, let us work with a general graded algebra
R=\oplus R_{n}
over a field
k
. We use the bar notation for elements of
R^{\otimes n+1}
:
r_{0}|r_{1}|\cdots|r_{n}:=r_{0}\otimes r_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes r_{n}
.
The
bar resolution
of a left graded
R
-module
B
is the complex
R^{\otimes n+1}\otimes_{k}B\ (n\geq 0)

(3.4.7.2)

\cdots R^{\otimes 3}\otimes_{k}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}R^{\otimes 2}\otimes_{k}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}R\otimes_{k}B(\longrightarrow B)

with differentials given by

\displaystyle d_{n}(r_{0}|\cdots|r_{n}|b)=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}r_{0}|\cdots|r_{i}r_{i+1}|\cdots|b

(here the last summand (
i=n
) is
(-1)^{n}r_{0}|\cdots|r_{n-1}|r_{n}b
(
r_{n+1}:=b
); note that the differentials preserve degrees.
This is known to be a resolution of
B
(by free
R
-modules) in the abelian category of graded
R
-modules, cf.
[
Wei94
, 8.6.12]
. Thus, for a graded (left)
R
-module
C
,
\mathrm{Ext}^{i}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen R}(B,C)
can be computed as
H^{i}
of the dual complex (all tensorizations will be over
k
):

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen R}(R\otimes B,C)\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{0}}}{{\rightarrow}}\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen R}(R^{\otimes 2}\otimes B,C)\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen R}(R^{\otimes 3}\otimes B,C)\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{2}}}{{\rightarrow}}\,\cdots

\displaystyle\cong\,

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen k}(B,C)\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen k}(R\otimes B,C)\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\,\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen k}(R^{\otimes 2}\otimes B,C)\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\,\cdots

\displaystyle\cong\,

\displaystyle\,(C\otimes B^{\vee})_{0}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\,(C\otimes(R\otimes B)^{\vee})_{0}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\,(C\otimes(R^{\otimes 2}\otimes B)^{\vee})_{0}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\,\cdots

(3.4.7.3)

\displaystyle\cong\,

\displaystyle\,(C\otimes B^{\vee})_{0}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\,(C\otimes R^{\vee}\otimes B^{\vee})_{0}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\,(C\otimes(R^{\vee})^{\otimes 2}\otimes B^{\vee})_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\,\cdots

where
(-)_{0}
means the degree
0
-subspace (the tensor product of two graded spaces is equipped with the usual grading).
The differentials
d^{n}:C\otimes(R^{\vee})^{\otimes n}\otimes B^{\vee}\rightarrow C\otimes(R^{\vee})^{\otimes n+1}\otimes B^{\vee}
are

\displaystyle d^{n}(c|f_{1}|\cdots|f_{n}|w)=\Delta_{C}(c)|f_{1}|\cdots|f_{n}|w\,

\displaystyle\,+\,\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i}c|f_{1}|\cdots|\nu(f_{i})|\cdots|f_{n}|w

\displaystyle\ +\,(-1)^{n+1}c|f_{1}|\cdots|f_{n}|\nu_{B^{\vee}}(w),

where
\Delta_{C}:C\rightarrow C\otimes R^{\vee}
is the
right

R^{\vee}
-comodule map for the coalgebra
R^{\vee}
induced from the module map
R\otimes C\rightarrow C
(
3.4.4.1
) and
\nu_{B^{\vee}}:B^{\vee}\rightarrow R^{\vee}\otimes B^{\vee}
is the dual of the module map
R\otimes B\rightarrow B
(which endows
B^{\vee}
with the structure of
left

R^{\vee}
-comodule).
Indeed, for
c|f|w\in C\otimes R^{\vee}\otimes B^{\vee}
(regarded as an element of
\mathrm{Hom}_{R}(R\otimes R\otimes B,C)
) and
1|r_{1}|r_{2}|b
of
R\otimes R^{\otimes 2}\otimes B
, we have

\displaystyle d^{1}(c|f|w)\left(1|r_{1}|r_{2}|b\right)=

\displaystyle c|f|w\left(r_{1}|r_{2}|b-1|r_{1}r_{2}|b+1|r_{1}|r_{2}b\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(r_{2})w(b)(r_{1}c)-f(r_{1}r_{2})w(b)c+f(r_{1})w(r_{2}b)c

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta_{C}(c)(r_{1})f(r_{2})w(b)-\nu(f)(r_{1}|r_{2})w(b)c+f(r_{1})|\nu_{B^{\vee}}(w)(r_{2}|b)c.

The dual complex (
3.4.7.3
) is called the
cobar complex
of
C\otimes_{k}B^{\vee}
.

The bar resolution of
B
has a quasi-isomorphic quotient complex
R\otimes\bar{R}^{\otimes n}\otimes_{k}B\ (n\geq 0)
,
\bar{R}:=\mathrm{Coker}(k\rightarrow R)
, called
normalized bar resolution

\cdots R\otimes\bar{R}^{\otimes 2}\otimes_{k}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}R\otimes\bar{R}\otimes_{k}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}R\otimes_{k}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}B

with differential
d_{n}
being essentially the same as that for the bar complex
[
Wei94
, Ex.8.6.4]
:

\displaystyle d_{n}(r_{0}|\bar{r}_{1}|\cdots|\bar{r}_{n}|b)=r_{0}r_{1}|\bar{r}_{2}|\cdots|\bar{r}_{n}|b\,

\displaystyle\,+\,\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}r_{0}|\cdots|\overline{r_{i}r_{i+1}}|\cdots|b

\displaystyle\,+\,(-1)^{n}r_{0}|\bar{r}_{1}|\cdots|\bar{r}_{n-1}|r_{n}b.

One can check that this is well-defined, i.e. the right expression is independent of the choice of representatives
r_{i}\in R
of
\bar{r}_{i}\in\bar{R}
. Furthermore, when
B=k
, the last term vanishes since for
r_{n}^{\prime}:=r_{n}-\epsilon(r_{n})\cdot 1
, one has
\bar{r}_{n}=\bar{r}_{n}^{\prime}
and
r_{n}^{\prime}m=\epsilon(r_{n}^{\prime})m=0
.

From now on, we assume that
R
has an augmentation
\epsilon:R\rightarrow k
, so that there exist a canonical identification
I_{R}:=\mathrm{Ker}(\epsilon:R\rightarrow k)\cong\mathrm{Coker}(k\rightarrow R)=\bar{R}
and a splitting
R=I_{R}\oplus k
given by
\mathrm{id}_{k}:k\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta}}{{\rightarrow}}R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon}}{{\rightarrow}}k
. In view of this, it follows that the cobar complex of
B^{\vee}
has a natural quasi-isomorphic subcomplex, called the
reduced cobar complex
of
C\otimes_{k}B^{\vee}
:

(3.4.7.4)

(C\otimes_{k}B^{\vee})_{0}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{0}_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ (C\otimes_{k}I_{R}^{\vee}\otimes B^{\vee})_{0}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{1}_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ (C\otimes_{k}(I_{R}^{\vee})^{\otimes 2}\otimes B^{\vee})_{0}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{2}_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots.

Namely, each component
(C\otimes(I_{R}^{\vee})^{\otimes n}\otimes B^{\vee})_{0}
is a component in the decomposition of
(C\otimes(R^{\vee})^{\otimes n}\otimes B^{\vee})_{0}
(
3.4.7.3
) resulting from the splitting
R^{\vee}=I_{R}^{\vee}\oplus k\epsilon
and the differential
d^{n}
is the degree
0
-part of
the map

C\otimes(I_{R}^{\vee})^{\otimes n}\otimes B^{\vee}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{n}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C\otimes(R^{\vee})^{\otimes n+1}\otimes B^{\vee}\twoheadrightarrow C\otimes(I_{R}^{\vee})^{\otimes n+1}\otimes B^{\vee}.

More explicitly, if
\bar{\Delta}_{C}:C\rightarrow C\otimes I_{R}^{\vee}
denotes the projection of
\Delta_{C}:C\rightarrow C\otimes R^{\vee}
via
R^{\vee}=I_{R}^{\vee}\oplus k\epsilon
and
\bar{\nu}_{B^{\vee}}:B^{\vee}\rightarrow I_{R}^{\vee}\otimes B^{\vee}
is a similar projection of
\nu_{B^{\vee}}
,
the differentials
d^{0}
,
d^{1}
are given by the degree
0
-part of

\displaystyle d^{0}=

\displaystyle\,\bar{\Delta}_{C}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B^{\vee}}-\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes\bar{\nu}_{B^{\vee}},

(3.4.7.5)

\displaystyle d^{1}=

\displaystyle\,\bar{\Delta}_{C}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{I_{R}^{\vee}\otimes B^{\vee}}-\mathrm{id}_{C}\otimes\bar{\nu}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{B^{\vee}}+\mathrm{id}_{C\otimes I_{R}^{\vee}}\otimes\bar{\nu}_{B^{\vee}},

where
\bar{\nu}
is the reduced comultiplication

\bar{\nu}:=\nu|_{I_{R}^{\vee}}-\epsilon\otimes\mathrm{id}_{I_{R}^{\vee}}-\mathrm{id}_{I_{R}^{\vee}}\otimes\epsilon\,:\,I_{R}^{\vee}\longrightarrow I_{R}^{\vee}\otimes I_{R}^{\vee}

which also equals the dual of the map
I_{R}\otimes I_{R}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nu}}{{\rightarrow}}R\twoheadrightarrow\bar{R}\cong I_{R}
(
R\twoheadrightarrow\bar{R}\cong I_{R}
is the same as the projection
R\rightarrow I_{R}
).
If
B=k
, then we have
\bar{\nu}_{B^{\vee}}=0
,
d^{0}=\bar{\Delta}_{C}
and
d^{1}=\bar{\Delta}_{C}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{I_{R}^{\vee}}-\bar{\nu}
.

Therefore, for any graded
R
-module
C
, we obtain the canonical isomorphism

(3.4.7.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen R}(B,C)\cong\,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker}(d^{1}_{0}:(C\otimes_{k}I_{R}^{\vee}\otimes B^{\vee})_{0}\longrightarrow(C\otimes_{k}(I_{R}^{\vee})^{\otimes 2}\otimes B^{\vee})_{0})/\mathrm{Im}(d^{0}_{0}).

On the other hand, this Ext group
\mathrm{Ext}^{i}
is also the cohomology group
H^{i}
of the complex (
3.4.7.3
). When
i=1
, for any
\gamma\in\mathrm{Ker}(d^{1}):=\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen R}(R^{\otimes 2}\otimes B,C)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen R}(R^{\otimes 3}\otimes B,C))
, the corresponding extension is constructed explicitly as follows:
from the bar resolution (
3.4.7.2
) there arise two exact sequences of graded
R
-modules

R^{\otimes 3}\otimes B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{2}}}{{\rightarrow}}R^{\otimes}\otimes B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathrm{Im}(d_{1})\rightarrow 0,\quad 0\rightarrow\mathrm{Im}(d_{1})\rightarrow R\otimes B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\rightarrow}}B\rightarrow 0

Applying the right exact functor
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen R}(-,C)
to these sequences, we obtain exact sequences

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker}(\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen R}(R^{\otimes 2}\otimes B,C)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d^{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen R}(R^{\otimes 3}\otimes B,C))\cong\,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen R}(\mathrm{Im}(d_{1}),C),

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen R}(R\otimes B,C)\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen R}(\mathrm{Im}(d_{1}),C)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen R}(B,C)\rightarrow 0.

The coboundary map
\partial
is defined by: given a map
\gamma\in\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen R}(\mathrm{Im}(d_{1}),C)
, we form the push-out

(3.4.7.7)

3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 184.97916pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 184.97916pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

obtaining an extension
M
of
B
by
C
. When
B=k
(as
R
-module via
\epsilon
), the top short exact sequence is identified with
0\rightarrow I_{R}\rightarrow R\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon}}{{\rightarrow}}k\rightarrow 0
, i.e,
\mathrm{Im}(d_{1})=I_{R}
, and the sequence splits (as
k
-vector spaces) as the canonical decomposition
R=I_{R}\oplus k
.

Now, we specialize to our case of the Hodge-Tate endomorphism algebra
R=\mathrm{End}(\omega)
with
B=\omega(\mathbb{Q}(0))=\mathbb{Q}_{(0)}
,
C=\omega(\mathbb{Q}(n))=\mathbb{Q}_{(n)}
(Lemma
3.4.5
).
We have
\Delta_{\mathbb{Q}_{(n)}}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{Q}_{(n)}}\otimes\epsilon
, so
\bar{\Delta}_{\mathbb{Q}_{(n)}}=0
,
d^{0}=0
and
d^{1}=-\bar{\nu}
.
Since
(\mathbb{Q}_{(n)}\otimes I_{R}^{\vee})_{0}\cong(R_{-n})^{\vee}
, the formula (
3.4.7.6
) becomes

(3.4.7.8)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))\cong\,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ker}(d^{1}_{0}:(R_{-n})^{\vee}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{k+n=-n,k,l>0}R_{k}^{\vee}\otimes R_{l}^{\vee})

Then, the simple extension in
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))
attached to
\gamma\in\mathrm{Ker}(d^{1}_{0})
by this isomorphism (
3.4.7.8
) is given as follows:
regarding
\gamma\in\mathrm{Ker}(d^{1}_{0})\subset(R_{-n})^{\vee}\cong(\mathbb{Q}_{(n)}\otimes I_{R}^{\vee})_{0}
as an element of
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{gr}\mhyphen R}(I_{R},\mathbb{Q}_{(n)})
,
the bottom row of the associated diagram (
3.4.7.7
), being a sequence of graded
R
-modules, is the image under
\omega
of a (uniuqe) extension of Hodge-Tate structures

(3.4.7.9)

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}(n)\longrightarrow H\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}(0)\longrightarrow 0:

this is the simple extension attached to
\gamma\in\mathrm{Ker}(d^{1}_{0})
.

Therefore, to prove the proposition, we have to prove that the equivalence class of the Hodge-Tate structure
H
(
3.4.7.9
) endowed with the obvious
n
-framing
v_{0}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{Q}(0)}
and
f^{n}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{Q}(n)}
is
\gamma
, this time regarded as an element of
\mathcal{H}_{>0}
via the identification
(I_{R})^{\vee}\cong\mathcal{H}_{>0}
in Theorem
3.4.2
.
By Corollary
3.4.4
, the equivalence class of this framed Hodge-Tate structure
(H,v_{0},f^{n})
is
\nu_{H}(v_{0},f^{n})\in\mathcal{H}
. By Theorem
3.4.2
, as an element of
R^{\vee}
, this is given by that

\mathrm{End}(\omega)\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}\,:\,r\mapsto\nu_{H}(v_{0},f^{n})(r)=\langle f^{n},r_{H}\cdot v_{0}\rangle,

where
r_{H}\cdot v_{0}
is the action of
r_{H}\in\mathrm{End}(\omega(H))
on
v_{0}\in\omega(H)=\omega(\mathbb{Q}(0))\oplus\omega(\mathbb{Q}(n))
.
Since (
3.4.7.7
) is a diagram of
R
-modules and its two rows split canonically as vector spaces, for
r\in I_{R}
, we have

r_{H}\cdot v_{0}=r_{H}\cdot\tilde{\gamma}(1)=\tilde{\gamma}(r\cdot 1)=\gamma(r),

where
r\cdot 1
is the multiplication in the ring
R
of two elements
r\in I_{R}
and
1\in\mathbb{Q}
(so, lies in
I_{R}
). This means that the map
I_{R}:\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}:r\mapsto\nu_{H}(v_{0},f^{n})(r)
is simply
\gamma\in I_{R}^{\vee}
, as asserted.
∎

A remarkable property of the big period is that it factors through the equivalence relation on framed Hodge-Tate structures.

Theorem 3.4.8
.

The big period induces a homomorphism
\mathscr{P}_{n}:\mathcal{H}_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}
.

This is Theorem 4.5. a) of
[
Gon99
]
. We give another more direct proof.

Proof.

Since the equivalence relation defining
\mathcal{H}_{n}
is generated by maps
H^{\prime}\rightarrow H
of
n
-framed Hodge-Tate structures preserving
n
-framings, it suffices to show that for any such map
g:H^{\prime}\rightarrow H
,
\mathscr{P}_{n}(H^{\prime})=\mathscr{P}_{n}(H)
. But, since the category
\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{MH}
of mixed
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge structures is abelian, we may further assume that
g
is either injective or surjective. First, let us assume that
g
is injective. Since
H\mapsto\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2k}H
is an exact functor on
\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{MH}
, the induced map
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H
is injective.
To compute the big periods of
H^{\prime}
and
H
, we may choose bases of
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H^{\prime}
,
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H
and splittings of
H^{\prime}
,
H
arbitrarily, since the big period is independent of such choice (Proposition
3.2.6
).
Let us first choose a
\mathbb{Q}
-basis of
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H^{\prime}
such that the covector
f^{n}\in\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2n}H^{\prime}
(part of the datum of
n
-framing of
H^{\prime}
) is a member of the associated dual basis. We expand it to a basis
\{v_{k}\}_{k\in J}\ (J\subset\mathbb{Z})
of
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H
; by abuse of notation,
v_{0}
is also the vector in
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{0}H^{\prime}
with the same notation which is a part of the datum of
n
-framing of
H^{\prime}
(and
H
). We have
J=J^{\prime}\sqcup J^{\prime\prime}
with
\{v_{i}\}_{i\in J^{\prime}}
(resp.
\{v_{i}\}_{i\in J^{\prime\prime}}
) a basis of
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H^{\prime}
(resp. of
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{\bullet}H^{\prime\prime}
), where
H^{\prime\prime}:=H/H^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{MH}
; note that
0,n\in J^{\prime}
,
0,n\notin J^{\prime\prime}
.
After choosing a splitting
\varphi:\bigoplus_{k}\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{k}H\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}H
which also gives a splitting of
H^{\prime}
, the Goncharov period matrix
\mathcal{M}
of
H
splits into two:

\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}+\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime},

where
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
(resp.
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}
) is the Goncharov period matrix of
H^{\prime}
(resp.
H^{\prime\prime}
); in this proof only, for simplicity let us write
\mathcal{M}
for the Goncharov period matrix
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{Gon}}
.
This has the property (recalling that the rows and columns of
H
are indexed by
i\in J
) that

(
\star
) for any pair
(i,j)\in J\times J
, the
(i,j)
-entry
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{i,j}
of
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is zero unless
(i,j)\in J^{\prime}\times J^{\prime}
and similarly for
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}_{i,j}
.

We can also split the identity matrix
I
of the same size as
\mathcal{M}
accordingly into
I^{\prime}+I^{\prime\prime}
, where
I^{\prime}
has only nonzero entry for
(i,i)
with
i\in J^{\prime}
where it is
1
, and
I^{\prime\prime}
is similarly defined with respect to
J^{\prime\prime}
.

Now, since
\mathcal{M}-I
is nilpotent, we can rewrite the big period
\mathscr{P}_{n}((H,v_{0},f^{n}))
(
3.2.4.1
) as

\displaystyle\langle v_{0}|\mathcal{M}^{-1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathcal{M}|f^{n}\rangle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\langle v_{0}|(-1)^{k}(\mathcal{M}-I)^{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathcal{M}|f^{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\sum_{i\in J}\left((\mathcal{M}-I)^{k}\right)_{0,i}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{i,n}.

We note that

(
\heartsuit
)
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}-I^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}-I^{\prime\prime}
are both nilpotent (upper-triangular) matrices and have the same property (
\star
) above.

Since
\mathcal{M}-I=(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}-I^{\prime})+(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}-I^{\prime\prime})
, we have

(\mathcal{M}-I)^{k}=\sum_{(\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{k})}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}-I^{\prime})^{\epsilon_{1}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}-I^{\prime\prime})^{1-\epsilon_{1}}\cdots(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}-I^{\prime})^{\epsilon_{k}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}-I^{\prime\prime})^{1-\epsilon_{k}},

where the sum runs through the set of all sequences
(\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{k})
with
\epsilon_{i}\in\{0,1\}
. So, for a fixed
i\in J
, we have

\left((\mathcal{M}-I)^{k}\right)_{0,i}=\sum_{(\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{k})}\sum_{0<i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{k}=i}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}-I^{\prime})_{0,i_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}-I^{\prime\prime})_{0,i_{1}}^{1-\epsilon_{1}}\cdots(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}-I^{\prime})_{i_{k-1},i_{k}}^{\epsilon_{k}}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}-I^{\prime\prime})_{i_{k-1},i_{k}}^{1-\epsilon_{k}},

where the inner sum runs through all sequences
(i_{1},\cdots,i_{k})
of strictly increasing positive integers in
J
with
i_{k}=i
.
It is easy to see from the property (
\heartsuit
) that

\left((\mathcal{M}-I)^{k}\right)_{0,i}=\left((\mathcal{M}^{\prime}-I^{\prime})^{k}\right)_{0,i},\text{ and both are zero unless }i\in J^{\prime}:

indeed, first, if
\epsilon_{1}=0
, then
(\mathcal{M}^{\prime\prime}-I^{\prime\prime})_{0,i_{1}}^{1-\epsilon_{1}}=0
by (
\heartsuit
), and if
i_{1}\in J^{\prime\prime}
, then
(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}-I^{\prime})_{0,i_{1}}^{\epsilon_{1}}=0
, so in the summation we may assume that
\epsilon_{1}=1
and
i_{1}\in J^{\prime}
. Next, by the same reason, we may assume that
\epsilon_{2}=1
and
i_{2}\in J^{\prime}
, and can continue.
Since clearly
\mathcal{M}_{i,n}=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}_{i,n}
for
i\in J^{\prime}
, hence we have

\displaystyle\langle v_{0}|\mathcal{M}^{-1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathcal{M}|f^{n}\rangle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\sum_{i\in J}\left((\mathcal{M}-I)^{k}\right)_{0,i}\otimes\mathcal{M}_{i,n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\langle v_{0}|(-1)^{k}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}-I^{\prime})^{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathcal{M}|f^{n}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle v_{0}|(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})^{-1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathcal{M}^{\prime}|f^{n}\rangle,

as claimed. The case that
g
is surjective is proved similarly.
∎

Composing
\mathscr{P}_{n}:\mathcal{H}_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}
with the skew-symmetrization
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}\wedge_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}
, we see that the skew-period map (
3.2.7.1
) also induces a homomorphism

(3.4.8.1)

\mathscr{A}_{n}\ :\ \mathcal{H}_{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}\wedge_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}.

Lemma 3.4.9
.

For
n>0
, the composite
\mathscr{A}_{n}\circ\iota_{n}
(
3.4.5.1
) induces an isomorphism

\mathscr{A}_{n}\circ\iota_{n}:\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\ 1\wedge\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}=\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q},

which equals twice the canonical isomorphism (
3.1.6.1
), under the identification
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathbb{C}/(2\pi i)^{n}\mathbb{Q}:x\mapsto(2\pi i)^{n}x
.

Proof.

Let
H\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))
.
After choosing a splitting
\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{0}H\oplus\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{-2n}H\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}H
(equiv. a lift of
1\in\mathrm{gr}^{W}_{0}H=\mathbb{Q}(0)
to
H
), we obtain the period matrix
\mathcal{M}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\omega\\
0&(2\pi i)^{n}\end{array}\right)

(\omega\in\mathbb{C})
. One computes that
\mathscr{P}_{n}(H)=1\otimes\frac{\omega}{(2\pi i)^{n}}-\frac{\omega}{(2\pi i)^{n}}\otimes 1
, so
\mathscr{A}_{n}(H)=1\wedge\frac{2\omega}{(2\pi i)^{n}}
. On the other hand, to
H
, the isomorphism (
3.1.6.1
) attaches
\omega\text{ mod }(2\pi i)^{n}\mathbb{Q}
.
∎

4
Chern-Simons mixed Tate motive

4.1  Mixed Tate motives

We give a condensed review of the theory of mixed Tate motives, cf.
[
DG05
,
\lx@sectionsign
1,
\lx@sectionsign
2]
,
[
Lev98
]
,
[
Lev93
]
,
[
Lev13
]
.

Let
k
be a field of characteristic zero (so that the resolution of singularities is available). A triangulated category of mixed motives over
k
was constructed by Hanamura
[
Han95
]
, Levine
[
Lev98
]
and Voevodsky
[
Voe00
]
independently, and Levine
[
Lev98
]
, VI 2.5.5, constructs an equivalence between his triangulated category and that of Voevodsky.
We will work with the Voevodsky’s category (of geometric motives), which we denote by
\mathrm{DM}(k)
; let
\mathrm{DM}(k)_{\mathbb{Q}}
be the triangulated category deduced from
\mathrm{DM}(k)
by tensoring with
\mathbb{Q}
.
We have in
\mathrm{DM}(k)
Tate objects
\mathbb{Z}(n)
(
n\in\mathbb{Z}
), whose images in
\mathrm{DM}(k)_{\mathbb{Q}}
will be also denoted
\mathbb{Q}(n)
:
\mathbb{Q}(n)=\mathbb{Q}(1)^{\otimes n}
and different
\mathbb{Q}(n)
’s are mutually nonisomorphic. We will be interested only in the triangulated subcategory
\mathrm{DMT}(k)_{\mathbb{Q}}
of
\mathrm{DM}(k)_{\mathbb{Q}}
generated by the
\mathbb{Q}(n)
’s, i.e. the smallest strictly full subcategory of
\mathrm{DM}(k)_{\mathbb{Q}}
containing all the objects
\mathbb{Q}(n)[m]\ (n,m\in\mathbb{Z})
and closed under extensions (recall that
E
is an extension of
B
by
A
if there exists a distinguished triangle
A\rightarrow E\rightarrow B\rightarrow A[1]
); its objects are the “iterated extensions” of various
\mathbb{Q}(n)[m]
’s.

Among many properties enjoyed by this triangulated category; we just mention two properties (specialized to the case of the motive of
\mathrm{Spec}(k)
): first, a relation of the Hom groups between shifts of Tate objects (“motivic cohomology” of the smooth
k
-variety
\mathrm{Spec}(k)
) with other known invariants (
[
Voe00
,
\lx@sectionsign
4.2]
,
[
Lev98
,
\lx@sectionsign
VI,2.1.9, II.3.6.6]
): for
q\geq 0
and
p\in\mathbb{Z}
, there exist canonical isomorphisms

(4.1.0.1)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{DM}(T)(k)_{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(q)[p])=\mathrm{CH}^{q}(\mathrm{Spec}(k),2q-p)=K_{2q-p}(k)^{(q)}.

Here,
\mathrm{CH}^{q}(\mathrm{Spec}(k),2q-p)
is the Bloch’s higher Chow group of
\mathrm{Spec}(k)
, and

K_{n}(k)^{(q)}
is the
q
-th eigen-subspace of
K_{n}(k)_{\mathbb{Q}}
for the Adams operator, which is isomorphic to
\mathrm{gr}^{q}_{\gamma}K_{2q-p}(k)_{\mathbb{Q}}
, the
q
-th associated graded part of the
\gamma
-filtration on
K_{2q-p}(k)_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
It follows that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{DM}(T)(k)_{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(q)[p])=0\text{ for }\begin{cases}\ p>2q\geq 0&(\text{by the first equality})\\
q=0\text{ and }p\neq 0&(\text{by the second equality})\end{cases};

one also has
K_{0}(k)^{(0)}=\mathbb{Q}
.
Secondly, we have (
[
Lev13
, 10.6]
)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{DM}(T)(k)_{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(q)[p])=0\quad\text{ for }q<0.

Also, on
\mathrm{DM}(k)
, there exists an associative and commutative tensor product
\otimes
with unity which is compatible with the triangulated structure and rigid (existence of a dual of every object with natural properties).

Recall the Beilinson-Soule vanishing conjecture:

(4.1.0.2)

K_{2q-p}(k)^{(q)}=0\quad\text{ for }p\leqslant 0\text{ and }q>0

When this condition holds for a field
k
,
Levine
[
Lev93
]
constructs a
t
-structure on
\mathrm{DMT}(k)_{\mathbb{Q}}
whose heart
\mathrm{MT}(k)
(an admissible abelian category) has objects the iterated extensions of
\mathbb{Q}(n)
’s
(n\in\mathbb{Z})

[
BBD82
, 1.3.14]
; we call
\mathrm{MT}(k)
the category of
mixed Tate motives
over
k
.

In general, when a triangulated category
D
has a
t
-structure with heart
\mathcal{A}
, there exists a canonical map from
\mathrm{Ext}^{p}_{\mathcal{A}}(A,B)
, the Yoneda extension groups of objects in
\mathcal{A}
, to
\mathrm{Hom}_{D}(A,B[p])
, the Hom groups in
D
of their shifts, which is a bijection for
p=0,1
and an injection for
p=2
(
[
DG05
, (1.1.4),(1.1.5)]
).
So in the case when the Beilinson-Soule vanishing conjecture holds, we have a map

(4.1.0.3)

\mathrm{Ext}^{p}_{\mathrm{MT}(k)}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(q))\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{DMT}(k)_{\mathbb{Q}}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(q)[p])=K_{2q-p}(k)^{(q)}.

From this point, until the end of this subsection, we assume that the Beilinson-Soule conjecture (
4.1.0.2
) holds for
k
, so that
\mathrm{MT}(k)
exists.

Then, first the tensor structure
\otimes
on
\mathrm{DM}(k)
provides
\mathrm{MT}(k)
with a tensor product which is exact in each variable, associative, commutative, with unity and rigid.
On the other hand, from
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\mathbb{Q}(m),\mathbb{Q}(n))=0
for
n\leq m
, it follows that every object
M
of
\mathrm{MT}(k)
admits a unique “weight” filtration
W
, finite, increasing and indexed by the even integers, such that

\mathrm{Gr}^{W}_{-2n}(M):=W_{-2n}(M)/W_{-2(n+1)}(M)

is a sum of copies of
\mathbb{Q}(n)
.
The filtration
W
is functorial, strictly compatible with morphisms, exact, and compatible with the tensor product.
Set

\omega_{n}(M):=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Q}(n),\mathrm{Gr}^{W}_{-2n}(M)).

So we have

\mathrm{Gr}^{W}_{-2n}(M)=\mathbb{Q}(n)\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\omega_{n}(M).

The exact functor
M\mapsto\omega(M):=\oplus\omega_{n}(M)
is a
\otimes
-functor, thus a fiber functor and the category
\mathrm{MT}(k)
is
\mathbb{Q}
-linear neutral Tannakian.
Therefore, we can repeat the constructions and arguments from the previous section on the Tannakian category
\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}
of Hodge-Tate
\mathbb{Q}
-structures. One can define
n
-framed mixed Tate motives over
k
, the abelian group
\mathcal{A}_{n}(k)
of their equivalence classes, and
\mathcal{A}_{\bullet}(k):=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{A}_{n}(k)
has a structure of an augmented commutative graded Hopf algebra over
\mathbb{Q}
; we denote the comultiplication
\mathcal{A}_{\bullet}(k)\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\bullet}(k)\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\bullet}(k)
by
\Delta
and call it the
motivic comultiplication
. The category
\mathrm{MT}(k)
is canonically equivalent to the category of finite-dimensional graded (right)
\mathcal{A}_{\bullet}(k)
-comodules, and the analogue of Proposition
3.4.7
holds with
\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}
and
\mathcal{H}_{n}
replaced by
\mathrm{MT}(k)
and
\mathcal{A}_{n}
respectively:

(4.1.0.4)

\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(k)}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))=H^{i}_{(n)}(\mathcal{A}_{\bullet}(k))=\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\Delta}|_{\mathcal{A}_{n}(k)}),

where the middle term is the degree-
n
part of the cohomology of the reduced cobar complex of
\mathcal{A}_{\bullet}(k)
(graded vector space) and
\bar{\Delta}:=\Delta-1\otimes\mathrm{id}-\mathrm{id}\otimes 1:\mathcal{A}_{\bullet}(k)_{>0}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\bullet}(k)_{>0}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\bullet}(k)_{>0}
is the reduced motivic comultiplication.

Furthermore, for any embedding
\sigma:k\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, there exist the realization functor

\mathrm{real}_{\sigma}:\mathrm{MT}(k)\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}

[
DG05
,
\lx@sectionsign
1.5]
. It is clear from functoriality of the construction that the canonical isomorphisms (
4.1.0.4
), Proposition
3.4.7
are compatible with the induced realization functors, i.e. the diagram

(4.1.0.5)

\textstyle{\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(k)}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
4.1.0.4
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{real}_{\sigma}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\Delta}|_{\mathcal{A}_{\bullet}(k)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{real}_{\sigma}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
3.4.7
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\nu}|_{\mathcal{H}_{n}}).}

commutes.

Now, suppose that
k
is a number field. According to Borel
[
Bor77
]
, the Beilinson-Soule vanishing conjecture holds. Moreover, he showed that for
q>0
,

(4.1.0.6)

K_{2q}(k)_{\mathbb{Q}}=0\text{ and }K_{2q-1}(k)^{(q)}=K_{2q-1}(k)_{\mathbb{Q}},

so that one has

K_{2q-p}(k)^{(q)}=0\text{ unless }(p,q)=(0,0)\text{ or }(1,\geq 1).

From this last property, Levine
[
Lev93
, 4.3]
deduces that for any number field
k
, the abelian category
\mathrm{MT}(k)
has the desired property that the map (
4.1.0.3
) is an isomorphism for all
p
and
q
:

(4.1.0.7)

\mathrm{Ext}^{p}_{\mathrm{MT}(k)}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(q))=K_{2q-p}(k)^{(q)};

in fact, these groups are both zero for
p\geq 2
, and this implies (cf.
[
Gon99
, Prop.5.8]
) that
\mathrm{DMT}(k)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is also the derived category of
\mathrm{MT}(k)
.
Also, the realization functor
\mathrm{real}_{\sigma}
(
4.1.0.5
) is compatible with the Beilinson regulator

(4.1.0.8)

R^{Be}:K_{2n-1}(\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow H^{1}_{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{Q}(n))=\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(n)

(where
H^{1}_{\mathscr{D}}(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{Q}(n))
is the Deligne-Beilinson cohomology of
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{C})
)
via the map (
4.1.0.3
), i.e. the diagram

(4.1.0.9)

\textstyle{\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(k)}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{real}_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{eq:Ext_{MT}->K-group})}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{MH}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\ref{eq:Ext^1_MH(Q(0),Q(n)})}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathbb{C}/(2\pi i)^{n}\mathbb{Q}}
\textstyle{K_{2n-1}(k)_{\mathbb{Q}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{K_{2n-1}(\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{Q}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R^{Be}}
