commutes (cf. the last paragraph on p.9 of
[
DG05
]
).

4.2  Polylogarithm mixed Tate motive

Lemma 4.2.1
.

The reduced comultiplication
\bar{\nu}
(
3.4.0.2
) of the framed polylogarithm Hodge-Tate structure
\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(z)
(
3.3.0.4
):

\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&-\mathrm{Li}_{1}(z)&-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)\\
0&2\pi i&2\pi i\log z\\
0&0&(2\pi i)^{2}\end{array}\right)

is given by

\bar{\nu}(\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(z))=\log(1-z)\otimes\log(z)\ \in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}(1)\otimes\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}(1)\

under the canonical isomorphism
\mathcal{H}_{1}\cong\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}(1)
(Lemma
3.4.9
).

Proof.

This is obvious from the definition (
3.4.0.4
) and the isomorphism
\mathcal{H}_{1}\cong\mathbb{C}/2\pi i\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

Theorem 4.2.2
.

For every
z\in\ \mathbb{C}-\{0,1\}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a framed mixed Tate motive
(M^{(n)}_{z},v_{0}(z),f^{n}(z))
defined over
\mathbb{Q}(z)\subset\mathbb{C}
whose Hodge realization is the framed polylogarithm Hodge-Tate structure
\mathcal{P}^{(n)}
(
3.3.0.2
).

The first proof is attributed to Deligne, and later more constructions (especially, in the case
n=2
) were given, cf.
[
Wan06
]
,
[
DF23
]
. The property about the field of definition
\mathbb{Q}(z)
is a consequence of these constructions of the motive.

Remark 4.2.3
.

Strictly speaking, it is not clear (at least to this author) that a priori all these constructions are the same objects in the category
\mathrm{MT}(\mathbb{Q}(z))
, although one expects this to be the case. But, we observe that there will be a unique framed mixed Tate motive defined over
k\subset\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
whose Hodge realization is the framed polylogarithm Hodge-Tate structure
\mathcal{P}^{(n)}(z)
if it is the fiber at
z
of a mixed Tate motive
\mathcal{M}
over
X:=\mathbb{P}^{1}\backslash\{0,1,\infty\}
(or “mixed Tate motivic sheaf”, as one says) in the sense of
[
CD19
]
or
[
HW98
]
whose Hodge realization is the polylogarithm variation of mixed Hodge structure
[
BD94
, 1.1-1.3]
,
[
Hai94
,
\lx@sectionsign
7]
(see also Subsec.
5.1
); such
\mathcal{M}
is the same datum as a
\mathrm{MT}(k)
-representation (in the sense of
[
Del89
,
\lx@sectionsign
7]
) of the motivic fundamental group
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{mot}}(X,\overrightarrow{v})
with tangential base point, where
\overrightarrow{v}
is the tangent vector
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
at
0
for the standard coordinate
z
on
\mathbb{A}^{1}
, cf.
[
Lev10
, Thm.1]
. Indeed,
then there exists a “parallel transport” morphism
\mathcal{M}_{\overrightarrow{v}}\otimes A_{\overrightarrow{v},z}(X)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{z}
in the Tannakian category
\mathrm{MT}(k)
, where
A_{\overrightarrow{v},z}(X)
is the pro-object in
\mathrm{MT}(k)
whose dual is the affine algebra of the motivic path torsor
P_{\overrightarrow{v},z}^{\mathrm{mot}}(X)
as constructed in
[
DG05
, Thm.4.4]
. It is known
[
Hai94
,
\lx@sectionsign
11]
that
\mathcal{M}_{\overrightarrow{v}}
contains
\mathbb{Q}(0)
and that the induced morphism
\mathrm{real}^{Hod}(A_{\overrightarrow{v},z}(X))\rightarrow\mathrm{real}^{Hod}(\mathcal{M}_{z})
of mixed Hodge structures is surjective and the kernel of its fiber at
\overrightarrow{v}
has an explicit set of generators given by local monodromoies. Since local monodromies are motivic
[
DG05
, 4.4]
, hence
\mathcal{M}_{z}
must be the quotient mixed Tate motive of
A_{\overrightarrow{v},z}(X)
by some ideal of
A_{\overrightarrow{v},\overrightarrow{v}}(X)
which is determined only by the Hodge realization.

Therefore, we call any framed mixed Tate motive which satisfies the above property
polylogarithm mixed Tate motive
of order
n
(framed by
v_{0}(z)
,
f^{n}(z)
).

Theorem 4.2.4
.

Let
X
be a hyperbolic three-manifold with invariant trace field
k(M)\subset\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Then there exists a mixed Tate motive
M(X)
in
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(k(M))}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(2))
, whose image under the Beilinson regulator (
4.1.0.8
) equals the normalized
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Chern-Simons invariant
\mathrm{CS}_{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}}(X)
(
2.2.8.1
)

Proof.

The Bloch invariant
\beta(X)
constructed by Neumann and Yang lies in
\mathcal{B}(k(X))_{\mathbb{Q}}
, where
k(X)\subset\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
is the invariant trace field (
[
NY99
, Thm.1.2]
). Suppose that
\beta(X)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}[z_{i}]
for
z_{i}\in k(X)
.
For each
z\in\mathbb{C}-\{0,1\}
, let
M(z)
be the framed mixed Tate motive defined as a difference of two framed polylogarithm mixed Tate motives:

(4.2.4.1)

M(z):=\frac{1}{2}\left([M^{(2)}_{z},v_{0}(z),f^{n}(z)]-[M^{(2)}_{1-z},v_{0}(1-z),f^{n}(1-z)]\right)

(Theorem
4.2.2
), and put
M(X):=\sum_{i}M(z_{i})\in\mathrm{MT}(F)
, where
F:=k(M)
.

First, we show that
M(X)\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(F)}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(2))
, more precisely, we will check that
\bar{\Delta}_{2}(M(X))=0
(
\bar{\Delta}_{2}=\bar{\Delta}|_{\mathcal{A}_{2}}:\mathcal{A}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{1}
being the reduced motivic comultiplication) and obtain an element of
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(F)}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(2))
via the canonical isomorphism (
4.1.0.4
).
For any embedding
\sigma:F\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we have the Hodge realization functor
\mathrm{real}_{\sigma}:\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(F)}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{MH}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))
.
Since the Hodge realization functor

(4.2.4.2)

\mathrm{real}^{Hod}_{F}:=\bigoplus_{\sigma}\mathrm{real}_{\sigma}\ :\ \mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(F)}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{\sigma}\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{MH}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))

is fully faithful
[
DG05
, Prop.2.14]
, by compatibility of motivic/Hodge-theoretic reduced comultiplications, it suffices to verify that for the reduced comultiplication
\bar{\nu}
on
\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}
,
\sum_{i}\bar{\nu}(\mathrm{real}_{\sigma}(M(z_{i})))=0
for every embedding
\sigma:F\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
But, for
z\in\mathbb{C}-\{0,1\}
, by Lemma
4.2.1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\bar{\nu}([\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(z_{i})])-\bar{\nu}([\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(1-z_{i})])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\log(1-z_{i})\otimes\log(z_{i})-\log(z_{i})\otimes\log(1-z_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\sum_{i}\log(1-z_{i})\wedge\log(z_{i})

(Here, for a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space
V
, we identify
V\wedge_{\mathbb{Q}}V
with a subspace of
V\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}V
via
x\wedge y\mapsto\frac{1}{2}(x\otimes y-y\otimes x)
).
This is the complex Dehn invariant
\delta_{\mathbb{C}}(\sum_{i}[z_{i}])
, hence is zero by Theorem
2.4.1
, (1). Since
\delta_{\mathbb{C}}([z])=(1-z)\wedge z
obviously commutes with the action of
\mathrm{Aut}(\mathbb{C})
, the claim is proved.

Next, for the inclusion
\mathrm{id}:F\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, the Beilinson regulator
K_{2n-1}(F)_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow K_{2n-1}(\mathbb{C})_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(2)
is the same as the Hodge realization
\mathrm{real}_{\mathrm{id}}
(
4.1.0.5
) under the canonical isomorphism
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(n))=\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(2)
(
3.1.6.1
): commutativity of the diagram (
4.1.0.9
). Since the normalized
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-Chern-Simons invariant
\mathrm{CS}_{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}}(X)
is one-half of the normalized Bloch regulator
\rho(\beta(M))
(
2.4.1.1
), to prove the second statement, we need to show the equality

\mathrm{real}_{\mathrm{id}}(M(X))=\frac{1}{2}\rho(\beta(X)).

By commutativity of (
4.1.0.5
), the Hodge realization
\mathrm{real}_{\mathrm{id}}(M(X))
of the mixed Tate motive
M(X)=\sum_{i}M(z_{i})
is the Hodge-Tate structure
H(X)
corresponding to
\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(z_{i})-\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(1-z_{i})\in\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\nu}|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}})
under the isomorphism
\iota_{2}^{-1}:\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\nu}|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(2))
(Proposition
3.4.7
).
Now, the composite of the isomorphisms

\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\nu}|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{2}^{-1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(2))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:Ext^1_MH(Q(0),Q(n)})}}{{=}}\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}(2)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Q}

is the map
\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{A}_{2}
(Lemma
3.4.9
). Hence, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{real}_{\mathrm{id}}(M(X))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\star)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\mathscr{A}_{2}(\sum_{i}\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(z_{i})-\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(1-z_{i}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i}\left[\mathscr{A}_{2}(\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(z_{i}))-\mathscr{A}_{2}(\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(1-z_{i}))\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i}\left[\tilde{\rho}([z_{i}])-\tilde{\rho}([1-z_{i}])\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(ii)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\rho}(\sum_{i}[z_{i}]).

Here,
\tilde{\rho}
is the unnormalized Bloch regulator (
2.4.0.1
) and we have
\rho=(2\pi i)^{2}\tilde{\rho}
(
2.4.0.11
). The equalities (i) and (ii) are respectively (
3.3.0.5
) and the functional equation for
\tilde{\rho}(z)
(
2.4.1.2
). This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.2.5
.

(1) We note that the first equality
(\star)
in the above uses Lemma
3.4.9
ant thus requires that the Hodge realization of our mixed Tate motive
M(X)
is constructed from
\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(z_{i})-\mathcal{P}^{(2)}(1-z_{i})\in\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\nu}|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}})
via the specific isomorphism
\iota_{2}^{-1}:\mathrm{Ker}(\bar{\nu}|_{\mathcal{H}_{2}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(2))
. So, we had to know that
\iota_{2}
was an isomorphism (Proposition
3.4.7
).

(2) The mixed Tate motive
M(X)
constructed in the theorem depends on the choice of a representative
\sum_{i=1}^{N}[z_{i}]\ (z_{i}\in k(M))
of the Bloch invariant
\beta(M)
. At the moment we do not know whether this mixed Tate motive is independent of such choice. The independent of our construction amounts to showing the “five term relation” for the framed mixed Tate motive

M(z)=\frac{1}{2}\left(M^{(2)}_{z}-M^{(2)}_{1-z}\right)
(
4.2.4.1
).

Meanwhile, uniqueness of a mixed Tate motive with specified Hodge realization would be a consequence of the full faithfulness of the Hodge realization
\mathrm{real}_{\mathrm{id}}:\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(k(M))}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(2))\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(2))
(
\mathrm{id}:k(M)\subset\mathbb{C}
). Note that this is stronger than the full faithfulness of the usual Hodge realization
\oplus_{\sigma:k(M)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}}\mathrm{real}_{\sigma}
which is known
[
DG05
, 2.14]
, and the author does not know whether such faithfulness is even reasonable to expect, like a consequence of some motivic conjectures.
But, thanks to our second main theorem (Theorem
7.5.3
), the argument of Remark
4.2.3
can be invoked to show that any (Chern-Simons) mixed Tate motive as in Theorem
4.2.4
will be unique, if one assumes existence of (the maximal Tate quotient of) the motivic path torsor of the augmented character variety of the manifold with arbitrary tangential base point and existence of “Chern-Simons motivic sheaf” over the augmented character variety whose Hodge realization is the Chern-Simons variation of Hodge-Tate structure which will be defined in Section 7; we think that existence of these objects is a reasonable assumption and perhaps can be deduced from existence of the motivic
t
-structure
[
Bei12
]
.

4.3  Comparison with Goncharov’s mixed Tate motives

For any hyperbolic manifold
M
of any odd dimension
2m-1
, Goncharov gave two constructions of elements in
K_{2m-1}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})_{\mathbb{Q}}=\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(2))
whose images under the Borel regulator
R^{Bo}:K_{2m-1}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
both equal the volume of
M
, and asked whether they are the same, expecting this to be the case.

When
m=2
, his second method of construction is at the very basis of our construction method of a mixed Tate motive in
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(2))
.
But, as we will explain, Goncharov’s mixed Tate motive by his second construction is not canonical and not necessarily equal to our mixed Tate motive since it cannot capture the Chern-Simons invariant in general. Since his K-theory element by the first construction is expected to be the same as our mixed Tate motive which should be canonical, this would answer negatively Goncharov’s question above.
We now give detailed explanation.

Goncharov’s first method is of homological nature, constructing an element
c(M)
of the group homology of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})^{\delta}
, the discrete group underlying the Lie group
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
:

K_{3}^{\mathrm{ind}}(\mathbb{C})\cong H_{3}(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})^{\delta},\mathbb{Z})

(an isomorphism induced by the Hurewicz map
K_{3}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow H_{3}(\mathrm{GL}(\mathbb{C})^{\delta},\mathbb{Z})
).
For this he first constructs an element
\tilde{c}(M)
of the relative group homology
H_{3}(\mathrm{SO}(3,1)^{\delta},T(s)^{\delta},\mathbb{Q})
for some subgroup
T(s)
of the stabilizer subgroup
E(s)
of an ideal point
s\in\partial\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Then,
c(M)
is a lift to
H_{3}(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\mathbb{Q})
of
\varphi(\tilde{c}(M))
along
H_{3}(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})^{\delta},\mathbb{Q})\rightarrow H_{3}(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})^{\delta},P^{\delta},\mathbb{Q})
, where
\varphi:(Spin(3,1),T(s))\rightarrow(\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),P)
is the half-spin representation and
P
is some subgroup of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.
We believe that this element
c(M)
equals our mixed Tate motive via the above isomorphism.

Next, we explain why the mixed Tate motive produced by Goncharov’s second construction method is non-canonical and unsatisfactory from the viewpoint of Chern-Simons invariant, and as such is not equal to our mixed Tate motive.

Let
X
be one of the three geometries:
\mathbb{E}
(Euclidean geometry),
\mathbb{S}
(Spherical geometry),
\mathbb{H}
(Hyperbolic geometry), and
G
be the isometry group.
The
scissors congruence group

\mathcal{P}(X)
of
X
is the quotient group of the free abelian group generated by “polytopes”
P
in
X
by the relations
\sim
:

\displaystyle(i)

\displaystyle\quad P\sim P_{1}+P_{2}\Leftrightarrow P=P_{1}\dot{\sqcup}P_{2}\quad(\text{``interior disjoint union''})

\displaystyle(ii)

\displaystyle\quad P\sim gP\quad\text{for all }g\in G.

For the definition of a polytope, we refer to
[
Dup82
,
\lx@sectionsign
2]
,
[
Dup01
]
(it needs a little caution, when
X=\mathbb{S}
). We denote the class of a polytope
P
by
[P]
. For two polytopes
P
,
P^{\prime}
, we have
[P]=[P^{\prime}]
in
\mathcal{P}(X)
if and only if
P
and
P^{\prime}
are
stably scissors congruent
(not scissors congruent!) in the sense that there exist polytopes
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
such that
P\sqcup Q\simeq P^{\prime}\sqcup Q^{\prime}
with
Q^{\prime}=gQ
for some
g\in G
.
Also, when we fix an orientation of
X
,
\pm[P]
can be regarded as an oriented polytope with the negative sign corresponding to the orientation opposite to that of
X
.

Goncharov
[
Gon99
,
\lx@sectionsign
1.4,
\lx@sectionsign
3.1]
also defines scissors congruence group as the abelian group generated by pairs
[M,\alpha]
, where
M
is an oriented geodesic simplex and
\alpha
is an orientation of
X
, with the relations:

\displaystyle(i^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad[M,\alpha]=[M_{1},\alpha]+[M_{2},\alpha]\ \text{ if }M=M_{1}\dot{\sqcup}M_{2}

\displaystyle(i^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\quad[M,\alpha]\text{ changes sign if we change orientation of }M\text{ or }\alpha

\displaystyle(ii^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad[M,\alpha]=[gM,g\alpha]\ \text{ for any }g\in G.

Lemma 4.3.1
.

When
X\neq\mathbb{S}
,
Goncharov’s scissors congruence group is the same as
\mathcal{P}(X)
.

Proof.

When we fix an orientation
\alpha
of
X
, Goncharov’s scissors congruence group is also generated by
[M,\alpha]
, and we claim that the map

(4.3.1.1)

[M,\alpha]\mapsto\epsilon(M)[|M|]

(defined on generators) induces an isomorphism between the two scissors congruence groups, where
\epsilon(M)=\pm 1
according as the orientation of
M
agrees with
\alpha
or not and
[|M|]
is the class in
\mathcal{P}(X)
of the polytope
|M|
underlying
M
(i.e ignoring orientation). This map is invariant under the relation (i
′′
), hence it suffices to show that
in the presence of the relations (i), (i
′
)
which are obviously equivalent under this correspondence, the two relations (ii), (ii
′
) match. If we set
\epsilon(g)=\pm 1
according as
g\in G
is orientation preserving or not, we have
\epsilon(gM)=\epsilon(g)\epsilon(M)
, so that

[gM,g\alpha]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(i^{\prime\prime})}}{{=}}\epsilon(g)[gM,\alpha]\mapsto\epsilon(g)\epsilon(gM)[|gM|]=\epsilon(M)[|gM|],

thus, (ii
′
) holds if and only if
[P]=[gP]
for all polytopes
P=|M|
and
g\in G
(
g|M|=|gM|
), namely if and only if
P
and
gP
are stably scissors congruent. Since
G
acts transitively on
X
, it is known
[
Dup01
, p.5]
that stable scissors congruence implies scissors congruence, i.e. (ii).
∎

Goncharov’s second construction of a mixed Tate motive for cusped hyperbolic manifold hinges crucially on the equality
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{H}^{n})=\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{n})
, where the latter is the scissors congruence group of the extended hyperbolic space
\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{n}=\mathbb{H}^{n}\sqcup\partial\mathbb{H}^{n}
and the equality is induced by the inclusion
\mathbb{H}^{n}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{n}
.
It is the group generated by geodesic
n
-simplices whose vertices are allowed to lie on
\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{n}
while all other points must be finite, modulo the same relations as in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{H}^{n}
) (either for
G
or
G^{+}
) (recall that the isometry group
G=\mathrm{SO}(n,1)
of
\mathbb{H}^{n}
still acts on
\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{n}
and
\partial\mathbb{H}^{n}
). Dupont
[
Dup82
,
\lx@sectionsign
2.Remark2]
and Sah (cf.
[
DS82
,
\lx@sectionsign
3]
) also introduce the scissors congruence group
\mathcal{P}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
of ideal (or “totally asymptotic” in their terminology) polytopes (i.e. polytopes with all vertices lying on the boundary
\partial\mathbb{H}^{n}
). When
n=3
, this group is related to the pre-Bloch group.

Definition 4.3.2
.

(1) The scissors congruence group
\mathcal{P}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
of
\partial\mathbb{H}^{n}
is the abelian group generated by all (ordered)
(n+1)
-tuples
(a_{0},\cdots,a_{n})
of ideal points
a_{i}\in\partial\mathbb{H}^{n}
subject to the relations

\displaystyle(i)

\displaystyle\quad(a_{0},\cdots,a_{n})=0\text{ if all $a_{i}$'s lie in a geodesic subspace of dimension less than $n$},

\displaystyle(ii)

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{0\leq i\leq n+1}(-1)^{i}(a_{0},\cdots,\hat{a}_{i},\cdots,a_{n+1})=0\quad(a_{0},\cdots,a_{n+1}\in\partial\mathbb{H}^{n}),

\displaystyle(iii)

\displaystyle\quad\epsilon(g)(ga_{0},\cdots,ga_{n})=(a_{0},\cdots,a_{n}),\quad(\forall a_{i}\in\partial\mathbb{H}^{n},\ \forall g\in G).

(2) Thurston scissors congruence group
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
is the abelian group defined similarly to

\mathcal{P}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
, except that (i) and (iii) in the definition of
\mathcal{P}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
are replaced respectively by

\displaystyle(i^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad(a_{0},\cdots,a_{n})=0\text{ if }a_{i}=a_{j}\text{ for some }i\neq j,

\displaystyle(iii^{\prime})

\displaystyle\quad(ga_{0},\cdots,ga_{n})=(a_{0},\cdots,a_{n}),\quad(\forall a_{i}\in\partial\mathbb{H}^{n},\ \forall g\in G^{+}).

Remark 4.3.3
.

(1) We remark that in the definitions of the scissors congruence groups
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
(both definitions) and
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{n})
, one could have used the subgroup
G^{+}
of orientation-preserving isometries, instead of the full isometry group
G
(
[
Dup01
, Thm.2.2]
). As
G^{+}
is a normal subgroup of
G
of index
2
which is generated by
G^{+}
and any reflection in a hyperplane, this means that any two polytopes in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
in mirror image are (stably) scissors congruent under orientation-preserving motions only (the trick is to use barycentric subdivision,
loc. cit.
).
But, the two definitions of
\mathcal{P}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
are distinguished mainly by use of different groups
G
or
G^{+}
. The reason why in this situation use of different groups results in different definitions is that for
ideal
polytopes in mirror image, the barycentric subdivision trick which was efficient for
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
,
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{n})
(
lot. cit.
) does not work, since the barycentric subdivision of an ideal simplex is not any longer made of ideal simplices. As we will see later in this subsection, this difference makes the group
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
a better object than the scissors congruence groups
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
,
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{n})
,
\mathcal{P}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
.

(2) In the definition of
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
, we obtain the same group if we impose the restriction that all
a_{i}\neq a_{j}
for
i\neq j
in all the conditions, except for (i
′
) which is replaced back by (i) (
[
DS82
]
, Remark after Corollary 4.7). Therefore, when
n=3
,
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{3})
is identified with the pre-Bloch group
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})
, via the map
(g_{0},\cdots,g_{3})\mapsto[[g_{0}:\cdots:g_{3}]]
(so,
(\infty,0,1,z)\mapsto[z]
). It is obvious that the composite map
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})=\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{3})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{3})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{3})
is given by (cf. the bijection (
4.3.1.1
)):

(4.3.3.1)

\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{3}):[z]\mapsto\epsilon(z)[|\Delta(\infty,0,1,z)|],

where
\epsilon(z)=\pm
depending on whether the oriented (by vertex order) simplex
\Delta(\infty,0,1,z)
has the same orientation as the ambient
\mathbb{H}^{3}
or not, and
|-|
is the underlying polytope with
[-]
being its class.

Any reflection
\tau
with respect to a geodesic hyperplane acts on
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
, and likewise the complex conjugation
\iota
on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})
. Let
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})_{-}:=\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})/\langle\tau x+x\rangle
and
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})_{-}:=\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})/\langle[\bar{z}]+[z]\rangle
denote the corresponding
(-1)
-coeigenspaces. Note that the ideal simplices
\Delta(\infty,0,1,z)
,
\Delta(\infty,0,1,\bar{z})
are the mirror image of each other (with respect to the geodesic hyperplane spanned by
\{\infty,0,1\}
).
Therefore, as
\epsilon(\bar{z})=-\epsilon(z)
, the natural map (
4.3.3.1
) factors through
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})_{-}=\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{3})_{-}
.

Theorem 4.3.4
.

Suppose
n=3
. There are natural isomorphisms (induced by natural maps)

\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})_{-}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{3})_{-}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{P}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{3})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{3})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{H}^{3}).

In particular, we have
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})_{-}\cong\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
(
[
Neu98
, Thm. 2.4]
), which can be interpreted as saying that the pre-Bloch group
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})
is an “orientation-sensitive scissors congruence group”. One should not take the equality
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{H}^{3})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{3})
as meaning that every polytope in
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{3})
is scissors congruent to a polytope in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
: “an infinite geodesic can never be cut up into a finite number of pieces and placed inside
\mathbb{H}^{n}\ (n>0)
”. It just says that it is
stably scissors congruent
in
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{3})
to a finite polytope (
[
Sah81
, Remark after Cor.4.7]
).

Proof.

This is Corollary 8.18 of
[
Dup01
]
. More precisely, we have seen the isomorphism
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})_{-}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{3})_{-}
.
The bijectivity
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{3})_{-}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{P}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{3})
is shown in
[
DS82
, (5.24)]
.
The bijectivity
\mathcal{P}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{3})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{3})
follows from that for odd
n>2
, the natural map
\mathcal{P}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{n})
is surjective with at most
2
-torsion kernel
[
DS82
, Prop.3.7,(ii)]
and torsion-freeness of
\mathcal{P}(\partial\mathbb{H}^{3})
(which holds since
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{C})
is uniquely divisible
[
Dup01
, Thm.8.16]
).
The last isomorphism
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{3})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\leftarrow}}\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
(which holds for general
n>1
) is shown in
[
DS82
, Thm.2.1]
(see also
[
Sah81
, Prop.3.3]
).
∎

In the Goncharov’s second method of constructing a mixed Tate motive in
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathrm{MT}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})}(\mathbb{Q}(0),\mathbb{Q}(2))
associated with a complete hyperbolic three-manifold of finite volume, the input is the scissor congruence class in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{H}^{3})
of the manifold (rather than the Neumann-Yang invariant
\beta(M)
in the Bloch group); especially, even in the cusped case he works only with finite geodesic simplices, instead of ideal simplices, using the equality
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{H}^{3})\cong\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathbb{H}}^{3})
. But, as W. Neumann
[
Neu98
, p.388-399]
points out, elements of the scissors congruence group cannot detect orientation-sensitive invariants, such as Chern-Simons invariants
CS
. Indeed, if an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism
\phi:M\rightarrow\bar{M}
of complete oriented hyperbolic three-manifolds is a mirror reflection, in the sense that for some geodesic triangulation of
M
, the restriction of
\phi
to each constituent geodesic simplex is a mirror reflection (like the reflection
\Delta(\infty,0,1,z)\mapsto\Delta(\infty,0,1,\bar{z})
, both being oriented by vertex orders), then
M
and
-\bar{M}
(
\bar{M}
with reversed orientation) have the same associated scissors congruence classes, but
CS(-\bar{M})=-CS(\bar{M})=-CS(M)
is not equal to
CS(M)
in general (unless it is trivial).
10
10

10

This can be also deduced from the equality
2\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathbb{C}}(M)=\sqrt{-1}\rho(\beta(M))
(Theorem
2.4.1
) and
\overline{\rho(z)}=\rho(\bar{z})

which follows from the same property for
\mathcal{R}(z)=\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\log(z)\log(1-z)
(
2.4.0.2
).

So there is no hope of expressing the complex volume of a hyperbolic three-manifold in terms of the Goncharov’s mixed Tate motive constructed from scissors congruence classes.

5
Interlude: Polylogarithm variation of mixed Hodge structure

5.1  Polylogarithm variation of mixed Hodge structure over
\mathbb{P}^{1}\backslash\{0,1,\infty\}

Here, we follow
[
Hai94
]
,
[
BD94
]
to give an account of the relevant part of the theory of polylogarithms. We also recommend
[
BGF
]
for a detailed expansion of
[
BD94
]
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the
k
-th polylogarithm function
\mathrm{Li}_{k}(z)
is an analytic function on
|z|<1
defined by

(5.1.0.1)

\mathrm{Li}_{k}(z)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{z^{n}}{n^{k}}.

These are iterated integrals of differential forms with logarithmic poles on
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\backslash\{0,1,\infty\}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{1}(z)

\displaystyle=-\log(1-z)=\int_{0}^{z}\frac{dt}{1-t},

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{k+1}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{z}\mathrm{Li}_{k}(t)\frac{dt}{t}\ \quad(k\geq 1).

This expression as iterated integrals shows that
\mathrm{Li}_{k}(z)
, defined by (
5.1.0.1
) for
|z|<1
, can be analytically continued as a
multivalued
function on
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\backslash\{0,1,\infty\}
. Many of the properties of these polylogarithms, especially their monodromy properties, are understood best when considered together. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we recall the framed polylogarithm Hodge-Tate structure
\mathcal{P}^{(n)}
(
3.3.0.2
); we denote it by
\Lambda^{(n)}(z)
when we regard it as a matrix with entries in the ring of multivalued functions on
\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\backslash\{0,1,\infty\}
:

(5.1.0.2)

\Lambda^{(n)}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}1&-\mathrm{Li}_{1}(z)&-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)&\cdots&\cdots&-\mathrm{Li}_{n}(z)\\
0&2\pi i&2\pi i\log z&2\pi i\frac{(\log z)^{2}}{2}&\cdots&2\pi i\frac{(\log z)^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\\
\vdots&0&(2\pi i)^{2}&(2\pi i)^{2}\log z&&(2\pi i)^{2}\frac{(\log z)^{n-2}}{(n-2)!}\\
\vdots&&&\ddots&&\vdots\\
0&&&&(2\pi i)^{n-1}&(2\pi i)^{n-1}\log z\\
0&&&&&(2\pi i)^{n}\end{array}\right)

(when we index the rows and columns by the set
[0,n]:=\{0,1.\cdots,n\}
, for
j\geq 1
the
j
-th row
\lambda_{j}
of
\Lambda(z)
equals
(2\pi i)^{j}
times

[0,\cdots,0,1,\log z,\frac{\log z}{2},\cdots,\frac{(\log z)^{n-j}}{(n-j)!}]

with
1
appearing in the
j
-th position and for
k\geq j
the
k
-th entry is
\frac{(\log z)^{k-j}}{(k-j)!}
).
It is known that the row vectors
\lambda_{j}(z)
of
\Lambda^{(n)}(z)
are fundamental solutions of the first order differential equation

d\lambda=\lambda\mathbb{\omega},

where

(5.1.0.3)

\mathbb{\omega}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}0&\omega_{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
&\ddots&\omega_{0}&\ddots&\vdots\\
\vdots&&\ddots&\ddots&0\\
&&&\ddots&\omega_{0}\\
0&&\cdots&&0\end{array}\right)\quad(\omega_{0}:=\frac{dz}{z},\ \omega_{1}:=\frac{dz}{1-z}).

and that the monodromy representation

M:\pi_{1}(\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\backslash\{0,1,\infty\})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(\mathbb{C})

(i.e. the analytic continuation of
\Lambda(z)
along
\gamma
equals
M(\gamma)\Lambda^{(n)}(z)
) is valued in
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(\mathbb{Q})
.

In other words,
\lambda_{i}(z)\ (0\leq i\leq n)
is a flat section (in fact, a multivalued global flat section) of the trivial vector bundle
\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\oplus n+1}
of rank
n+1
over
X:=\mathbb{P}^{1}\backslash\{0,1,\infty\}
with integral connection

(5.1.0.4)

\nabla f=df-f\mathbb{\omega}

and the sections
\lambda_{0},\cdots,\lambda_{n}
generate a
\mathbb{Q}
-local system
\mathbb{V}
over
X
. This is called the
n
-th polylogarithm local system
.

Theorem 5.1.1
.

[
Hai94
,
\lx@sectionsign
2,
\lx@sectionsign
7]
,
[
BD94
,
\lx@sectionsign
1]

The
n
-th polylogarithm local system underlies a good variation of mixed Hodge structure whose weight-graded quotients are canonically isomorphic to
\mathrm{Gr}^{W}_{-2i}=\mathbb{Q}(i)
,
\mathrm{Gr}^{W}_{-2i+1}=0
(
0\leq i\leq n
). It has unipotent monodromy at each point of
\{0,1,\infty\}
. They form a projective system of mixed Hodge structures as
n
varies.

For the notion of a good variation of mixed Hodge structure, see
[
HZ87a
]
,
[
HZ87b
]
, where a good variation of mixed Hodge structure whose weight-graded quotients are sums of copies of some
\mathbb{Q}(i)
as in the theorem is called a
Tate variation of mixed Hodge structure
.

The Hodge filtration
\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}
and the weight filtration
\mathbb{W}_{\bullet}
of the
n
-th polylogarithm variation of mixed Hodge structure
\mathcal{P}^{(n)}=(\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\oplus n+1},\nabla,\mathbb{W}_{\bullet},\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
in the theorem are defined as follows:
Let
\{e_{0},\cdots,e_{n}\}
be the standard basis of
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
. Define
complex
weight filtration and Hodge filtration on
V=\mathbb{C}^{3}
by:

(5.1.1.1)

\displaystyle W_{-2l+1}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}=

\displaystyle W_{-2l}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}=\mathbb{C}\langle e_{l},\cdots,e_{n}\rangle,

\displaystyle F^{-p}\mathbb{C}^{n+1}=

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}\langle e_{0},\cdots,e_{p}\rangle,

We put
\mathscr{F}^{p}:=F^{p}V\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}\subset\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}
.
We define
\mathbb{V}_{z}
to be the
\mathbb{Q}
-subspace of
V=\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
spanned by
\{\lambda_{0}(z),\cdots,\lambda_{n}(z)\}
endowed with the filtration by
\mathbb{Q}
-subspaces:

(5.1.1.2)

W_{-2l+1}\mathbb{V}_{z}=W_{-2l}\mathbb{V}_{z}=\mathbb{Q}\langle\lambda_{l}(z),\cdots,\lambda_{n}(z)\rangle

This
\mathbb{Q}
-filtration
W_{\bullet}\mathbb{V}_{z}
is well-defined, i.e. independent of the choice of the branch of
\log z
(as the monodromy is valued in
\mathrm{GL}_{n+1}(\mathbb{Q})
), and is a
\mathbb{Q}
-structure of the
\mathbb{C}
-filtration
W_{\bullet}V
, so it follows that there exists an increasing filtration
\mathbb{W}_{l}
of
\mathbb{V}
by
\mathbb{Q}
-local sub-systems; the graded quotients of the mixed Hodge structure
V_{z}
are
\mathbb{Z}(0)
,
\mathbb{Z}(1)
,
\cdots
,
\mathbb{Z}(n)
.

When
V(z)
denotes the variation of mixed Hodge structure which is the extension of
\mathbb{Q}
by
\mathbb{Q}(1)
corresponding to
z\in\mathcal{O}^{\times}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\backslash\{0,1,\infty\})
, the
n
-th polylogarithm variation of mixed Hodge structure is an extension of
\mathbb{Q}
by the shift
(\mathrm{Sym}^{n-1}V(z))\otimes\mathbb{Q}(1)
of the
(n-1)
-rd symmetric power of
V(z)
(
[
Hai94
, Prop.9.5]
), and the polylogarithm functions
\{\mathrm{Li}_{k}\}_{0\leq k\leq n}
can be regarded as an extension data.
In the case
n=2
, the entries of
\Lambda(z)
can be understood more directly as encoding the various extensions of the Hodge-Tate structures (see
\lx@sectionsign
9 of
[
Hai94
]
or subsection
7.1
of the main body for more details); in particular, the extension of
\mathbb{Q}
by
V(z)\otimes\mathbb{Q}(1)
corresponds to the nontrivial
(0,2)
-entry of
\Lambda(z)
, namely the dilogarithm function
\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)
which appears in many different areas of mathematics.

On the other hand, some well-defined limits of polylogarithm functions at the points at infinity
\{0,1,\infty\}
(obtained as sutiable regularized integrals) turn out to be quite interesting numbers. These limits are expressed best in terms of limit mixed Hodge structures (à la W. Schmid
[
Sch73
]
) at suitable tangent vectors at the points at infinity.

Theorem 5.1.2
.

[
Hai94
, Thm.7.2]

Let
z
be the natural coordinate function on
\mathbb{C}-\{0,1\}
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
be the associated tangent vector at
0
. The limit mixed Hodge structure at
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
of the
n
-th polylogarithm variation of mixed Hodge structure splits, i.e. equals the split mixed Hodge-Tate structure
\mathbb{Q}(0)\oplus\mathbb{Q}(1)\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathbb{Q}(n)
, and that at

-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
(regarded as a tangent vector at
1
“pointing towards
0
”) has
\zeta(2),\cdots,\zeta(n)
as periods (of mixed Hodge-Tate structure).

Beilinson and Deligne
[
BD94
]
conjectured that the polylgarithmic variation of mixed Hodge structure is motivic, i.e. is the Hodge realization of a “mixed motivic sheaf” over
\mathbb{P}^{1}\backslash\{0,1,\infty\}
; lacking a good formalism of (mixed) motivic sheaves at the time, their conjecture instead stated that it is the Hodge realization of a “realization system” in the sense of Deligne
[
Del89
]
, which admits a geometric description in terms of the
fundamental groupoid
of the projective line minus three points. We now explain this in more detail.

For a topological space
X
, let
P_{x,y}X
denote the set of homotoy classes of paths in
X
from
x
to
y
, and
\mathbb{Q}[P_{x,y}X]
the free abelian group on
P_{x,y}X
. As
x
,
y
vary, these form a local system
\{\mathbb{Q}[P_{x,y}X]\}_{(x,y)}\rightarrow X\times X
. There exists a filtration (in local systesms) given by the powers of the augmentation ideal
J
; denote by
\mathbb{Q}[P_{x,y}X]^{\wedge}
the completion of
\mathbb{Q}[P_{x,y}X]
with respect to the powers of
J
.

Now, when
X
is an algebraic variety over
\mathbb{C}
, Hain showed that the local system
\{\mathbb{Q}[P_{x,y}X]^{\wedge}\}_{(x,y)}\rightarrow X\times X
underlies a good variation of mixed Hodge structure whose fiber over
(x,x)
is the canonical mixed Hodge structure constructed by Chen on the unipotent completion
\mathbb{Q}\pi_{1}(X,x)^{\wedge}
of the group algebra
\mathbb{Q}\pi_{1}(X,x)=\mathbb{Q}[P_{x,y}X]
.
Moreover, Hain and Zucker
[
HZ87a
]
(cf.
[
Hai94
, 11.2]
) showed that

Theorem 5.1.3
.

If
\mathcal{V}\rightarrow X
is a
unipotent
variation of mixed Hodge structure over a smooth curve, the natural map

\mathcal{V}_{x}\otimes\mathbb{Q}[P_{x,y}X]^{\wedge}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{y}

induced by parallel transport is a morphism of mixed Hodge structures for every
x,y\in X
.

Here, we stress that for base point
x
and/or
y
, one can allow a “tangential base point”
\overrightarrow{v}
in the sense of
[
Del89
,
\lx@sectionsign
15]
, in which case the fiber at
\overrightarrow{v}
of
\mathcal{V}
is nothing other than the limit mixed Hodge structure of Schmid and
P_{\frac{\partial}{\partial z},x}X
is interpreted as the set of homotopy classes of paths leaving
0
in the direction of
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
towards
x
.
11
11

11

or from a point in the punctured tangent space
T_{0}^{\ast}X
with coordinate
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
to
x

Now, when
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
and
\overrightarrow{v}
is
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
for the standard coordinate
z
of
\mathbb{C}
, by Theorem
5.1.2
, there exists a canonical inclusion of mixed Hodge structure
\mathbb{Q}(0)\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{(n)}_{\frac{\partial}{\partial z}}
, hence the parallel transport map induces a canonical morphism of MHS

\mathbb{Q}[P_{\frac{\partial}{\partial z},x}X]^{\wedge}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}^{(n)}_{x}

Theorem 5.1.4
.

[
Hai94
, Thm.11.3]

The polylogarithm variation
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}
is the quotient of the variation of mixed Hodge structure
\mathbb{Q}[P_{\frac{\partial}{\partial z},\ast}X]^{\wedge}
whose fiber
at
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
is the quotient of
\mathbb{Q}\pi_{1}(X,\frac{\partial}{\partial z})^{\wedge}
by the right ideal generated by

M(\sigma_{0})-1,\quad J(M(\sigma_{1})-1),\quad J^{n}

where
M(\sigma_{0})
is the local monodromy at the tangential base point
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
wrapping
0
counterclockwise,
M(\sigma_{1})
is the local monodromy at
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
which moves along the path
(0+\epsilon,1-\epsilon)\ (0<\epsilon\ll 1)
in the real line and wrapping
1
counterclockwise once and follows the same path back, and
J
is the augmentation ideal.

This has the following remarkable consequence, observed by Deligne. For
X=\mathbb{P}^{1}\backslash\{0,1,\infty\}
, Deligne and Goncharov
[
DG05
, 3.12]
(cf.
[
BGF
, Thm.4.144]
) constructed the space of
motivic paths
from
a
to
b
:
P_{b,a}(X)=\mathrm{Spec}(A_{b,a}(X))
(when
b=a
, by definition this is the
motivic fundamental group

\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{mot}}(X,a)
of
X
with base point
a
). This is a scheme in the Tannakian category
\mathrm{MT}(\mathbb{Q})
of mixed Tate motives over
\mathbb{Q}
(see
[
Del89
,
\lx@sectionsign
5]
for the notion of “schemes in a Tannakian category”).
Under each realization functor, this motivic path torsor becomes the path torsor scheme in that realization category. For example, the Betti realization of
\pi_{1}^{\mathrm{mot}}(X,x)
is the pro-unipotent algebraic envelope of
\pi_{1}(X(\mathbb{C}),s)

[
Del89
,
\lx@sectionsign
9]
, that is the spectrum of the (commutative) Hopf algebra
(\mathbb{Q}\pi_{1}(X,x)^{\wedge})^{\vee}
dual to the (cocommutative) Hopf algebra
\mathbb{Q}\pi_{1}(X,x)^{\wedge}
.
When
a,b
are (tangential) base points of
X
defined over
\mathcal{O}_{S}
for a ring
\mathcal{O}_{S}
of
S
-integers (
k
and
S
being a number field and a finite set of places of
k
), this also defines an object in the similarly defined Tannakian category
\mathrm{MT}(\mathcal{O}_{S})_{\mathbb{Q}}
of mixed Tate motives over
\mathcal{O}_{S}
.
They also showed (
[
DG05
, Prop.2.4]
) that the Hodge realization functor from the abelian category
\mathrm{MT}(\mathbb{Q})
of mixed Tate motives over
\mathbb{Q}
to the category
\mathbb{Q}\text{-}\mathrm{HT}
of Hodge-Tate structures is fully faithful and its essential image is stable under taking sub-objects.
Then, since the local monodromy is also a mixed Tate motive (
ibid.
5.4), from the above theorem (Theorem
5.1.4
) we conclude that:

Corollary 5.1.5
.

At any tangential base point
\overrightarrow{v}
, the limit mixed Hodge structure
\mathcal{M}^{(n)}_{\overrightarrow{v}}
is “motivic”. More precisely, it is the Hodge realization of a mixed Tate motive over
\mathbb{Z}
.

We remark that Beilinson and Deligne
[
BD92
]
sketched an explicit construction of
motivic polylgoarithm
, “motivic sheaf” over
\mathbb{P}^{1}\backslash\{0,1,\infty\}
whose Hodge realization is the polylogarithm VMHS) as an element of a certain K-group (see
[
HW98
]
for details of their construction).

5.2  What we do in the second part

First we construct a unipotent variation of mixed
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge structure over the smooth locus
\tilde{X}(M)_{0}^{\mathrm{sm}}
of the canonical component
\tilde{X}(M)_{0}
of the augemented character variety
\tilde{X}(M)
; by abuse of terminology, we will call
\tilde{X}(M)_{0}

canonical curve
. Our construction follows the idea of Morishita-Terashima
[
MT09
]
who constructed a similar variation of mixed Hodge structure using Chern-Simons invariant, which is regarded as a section of a line bundle. Their variation of mixed Hodge structure however lives on the (Thurston) deformation curve, instead of our curve
\tilde{X}(M)_{0}^{\mathrm{sm}}
: the deformation curve of a hyperbolic three-manifold
M
depends on the choice of an ideal triangulation of
M
, while the character variety (and thus
\tilde{X}(M)_{0}^{\mathrm{sm}}
) is canonically attached to
M
. We give a precise relation between Morishita-Terashima’s construction and ours (based on Kirk-Klassen’s work) in Proposition
6.6.4
.
As will be seen, our construction of Chern-Simons variation of mixed Hodge structure (CS VMHS, for short) is quite similar to the well-known construction of
2^{\text{nd}}
-polylogarithm variation of mixed Hodge structure (compare (
5.1.0.4
), (
5.1.1.1
), (
5.1.1.2
) with the corresponding constructions in
7.2
): the role of the dialogarithm function in the polylogarithm variation of mixed Hodge structure will be played by a sum of the Chern-Simons invariant and the product of the log-holonomies of the meridian and the longitude.
Then, from this CS VMHS, it is natural to aim to establish statements corresponding to Theorem
5.1.2
, Theorem
5.1.4
(with a view towards proving the motivicity of our CS VMHS)
following the same strategy of proof
. Hence we need to consider the points at infinity of the canonical curve
\tilde{X}(M)_{0}
(this curve is affine, so there always exist “points at infinity”, which in three-dimensional topology are more often called
idea points
).
Here, we make the statement corresponding to
5.1.2
in our setting as an assumption (Conjecture
7.4.2
); this concerns an asymptotic behavior of the Chern-Simons invariant near an ideal point. We verify this assumption in the appendix in the case of the figure-eight knot complement and another knot. Assuming this conjecture, we proceed to prove the analogue of Theorem
5.1.4
by the same method of using local monodromies at tangential base points at ideal points. Here, we establish such an analogue whose proof requires some nontrivial facts in three-dimensional topology of
M
(this is another satisfying aspect of our work). Namely, we show (Theorem
7.5.3
) that this so-called Chern-Simons variation of mixed Hodge structure is a quotient of the mixed Hodge structure on the unipotent completion of the path torsor.

At this point, if we accept some popular expectations in the theory of motives (most notably, existence of the motivic
t
-structure in the triangulated category of mixed motives
[
Bei12
]
),
our main theorem implies that the Chern-Simons variation of mixed Hodge structure is motivic, i.e. is the Hodge realization of a mixed motive over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Here, we bring the reader’s attention to the fact that unlike in the case of the projective line minus three points, there is no guarantee that the object in the triangulated category of mixed motives constructed by Deligne and Goncharov using cosimplicial model of the path torsor belongs to the triangulated subcategory of mixed Tate motives.

6
Chern-Simons line bundle and Chern-Simons section

6.1  Chern-Simons invariant as a section of a line bundle

For a closed Riemannian three-manifold
M
, the Chern-Simons integral
\int_{M}Q(A)
of a flat connection
A
on a trivial(ized) principal
(\mathrm{P})\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-bundle (regarded as
\mathfrak{sl}_{2,\mathbb{C}}
-valued
1
-form on
M
) is gauge-invariant, thus gives a well-defined
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}
-valued invariant
cs_{M}(A)
of
A
.
For non-closed manifolds, this integral is not gauge-invariant. For cusped hyperbolic three-manifolds,
Kirk and Klassen
[
KK90
]
,
[
KK93
]
defined the Chern-Simons invariant of an aribtrary
flat
connection
A
by

(6.1.0.1)

cs_{M}(A):=cs_{M}(g\cdot A)=\int_{M}Q(g\cdot A),

where
g\in\mathcal{G}
is chosen such that the gauge transformed connection
g\cdot A
is in normal form (Proposition
2.2.6
, (1)).
If one can choose a canonical such gauge transform
g\cdot A
, then this will give a well-defined invariant of
A
likewise. But in general, there is no unique
A^{\prime}
that is gauge equivalent to
A
and is in normal form, and the Chern-Simons integrals mod
\mathbb{Z}
(
6.1.0.1
) of such
A^{\prime}
and
A
still might differ; especially, it is not possible to define
cs([\rho])
unambiguously for a conjugacy class
[\rho]
of holonomy representation
\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

However, Kirk and Klassen determined the precise difference in the Chern-Simons integrals of two gauge equivalent
1
-forms
A^{\prime}
,
A
in normal form which turn out to depend only on their normal forms near boundary (Theorem
6.4.1
). This allowed them to view the Chern-Simons invariant (
6.1.0.1
) as a section over the character variety
X(M)
of
M
of certain principal
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundle(=line bundle) on the character variety
X(\partial M)
of the boundary
\partial M
(we will call this section
Kirk-Klassen Chern-Simons section
). We remark that this viewpoint is originally due to Ramadas-Singer-Weitsman
[
RSW89
]
.

On the other hand, following the ideas of Kirk and Klassen, Morishita and Terashima
[
MT07
]
constructed another line bundle over the algebraic torus
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2h}
(
h
being the number of boundary tori) and a section of it over the Thurston deformation curve, again by means of the Chern-Simons integral (
6.1.0.1
) (we will call this section
Morishita-Terashima Chern-Simons section
). But, their construction of line bundle is based on Heisenberg group and its relation to the one of Kirk and Klassen is not clear. Here, we provide an exact relation between these two line bundles.

A remarkable aspect of the Morishita-Terashima Chern-Simons section, which will be a key to our work, is that it can be interpreted (in fact, was constructed from the beginning) as a (good unipotent) variation of Hodge-Tate structures. But, whereas the Kirk-Klassen Chern-Simons section exists on the character variety and uniquely determined by the hyperbolic manifold, the Morishita-Terashima Chern-Simons section exists over the deformation curve, thus depends on the choice of an ideal triangulation and as such is non-canonical.
For our purpose of construction of canonical motivic invariants of hyperbolic manifolds, we combine these two works. Especially, we mimic the Morishita-Terashima constructions over the
augmented character variety
, a certain double covering of the character variety which can be thought of as a triangulation-choice-free analogue of the Thurston deformation curve.

6.2  Character varieties

In the works on three-manifolds, there are used a few related, but slightly different definitions of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
-character varieties. Here, we explain three definitions: Culler-Shalen construction, GIT quotient of representation variety, affine scheme of trace ring. The latter two definitions are equivalent over fields and most commonly used nowadays, while old works (such as
[
KK90
]
,
[
KK93
]
) are built on the pioneering definition introduced by Culler and Shalen
[
CS83
]
. We also discuss
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}
character varieties. In this work, by a variety we mean a separated scheme of finite type over a field, which is not necessarily reduced or irreducible.

Over a field
k
of characteristic
zero
, the character variety of an abstract group is most often defined as the GIT-quotient of the representation variety.

First, it is easily seen (
[
Sik12
,
\lx@sectionsign
5]
) that there exists an affine
\mathbb{Z}
-scheme
R(\Gamma)
that represents the functor:

(6.2.0.1)

(\text{algebras})\rightarrow(\text{sets})\ :\ A\mapsto\mathrm{Hom}_{\text{gp}}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{2}(A));

we call it (
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
-)representation
\mathbb{Z}
-scheme of
\Gamma
.
More precisely, there exist an algebra
A(\Gamma)
over
\mathbb{Z}
(called the
universal representation ring
) and a “universal representation”

(6.2.0.2)

\rho^{\mathrm{univ}}:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(A(\Gamma))

such that every representation
\rho:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(A)
is obtained from
\rho^{\mathrm{univ}}
via a unique homomorphism
\phi:A(\Gamma)\rightarrow A
:

(6.2.0.3)

\mathrm{Hom}_{\text{gp}}(\Gamma,\mathrm{SL}_{2}(A))=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{alg}}(A(\Gamma),A).

Since for any algebra
A
, the group
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}(A)
acts on the left-hand side of
6.2.0.3
via the conjugation action on
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(A)
, it follows that the group scheme
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}
acts on
A(\Gamma)
(this is also clear from an explicit construction of
A(\Gamma)
, cf. loc. cit.).

Definition 6.2.1
.

The
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
-character variety of a group
\Gamma
is the affine
\mathbb{Q}
-variety:

X(\Gamma):=\mathrm{Spec}(A(\Gamma)_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\mathrm{PGL}_{2}})

(Spec of the subalgeba of functions in
A(\Gamma)_{\mathbb{Q}}
invariant under
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}
).

This is also known as the (affine) GIT quotient of the representation variety
R(\Gamma)_{\mathbb{Q}}
and is written as
R(\Gamma)_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathrm{SL}_{2}
.

The following fact is deduced from a standard result in geometric invariant theory, cf.
[
Mar16
, Thm.2.2]
:

Theorem 6.2.2
.

If
k
is algebraically closed field, there is a bijection among the following sets:

(i)

the
k
-points of
X(\Gamma)
(=
\mathrm{Hom}_{k\mhyphen\text{alg}}([A(\Gamma)_{k}]^{\mathrm{PGL}_{2}},k)
);

(ii)

the
closed
orbits of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(k)
acting on
R(\Gamma)(k)=\mathrm{Hom}(A(\Gamma)_{k},k)
;

(iii)

the conjugacy classes of
semi-simple
representations
\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(k)
;

(iv)

the set of characters of representations
\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(k)
.

For
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}
-character varieties, there does not seem to be a standard definition for an arbitrary finitely generated group
\Gamma
: one difficulty is that the group-valued functor
R\mapsto\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(R):=\mathrm{SL}_{2}(R)/\{\pm 1\}
on the category of
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras is not representable, so neither is the functor (
6.2.0.1
) for
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}
.
12
12

12

Some people , e.g.
[
BZ98
,
\lx@sectionsign
3]
, use the embedding
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{3}
(adjoint representation) to avoid this issue.

However, when
H^{2}(\Gamma,\mathbb{Z}/2)=0
, there is a reasonable definition of
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}
-character variety, at least over
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
, as the quotient of
X(\Gamma)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
by
H^{1}(\Gamma,\mathbb{Z}/2)=\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\{\pm 1\})
, where
\sigma\in\mathrm{Hom}(\Gamma,\{\pm 1\})
acts on
\chi\in X(\Gamma)(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
by
\sigma\chi(\gamma):=\sigma(\gamma)\chi(\gamma)
. This is because every
\bar{\rho}\in R_{\mathrm{PSL}_{2}}(\Gamma)(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
determines a cohomology class
w_{2}(\bar{\rho})\in H^{2}(\Gamma,\mathbb{Z}/2)
, which is zero if and only if
\bar{\rho}
lifts to
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
. The condition
H^{2}(\Gamma,\mathbb{Z}/2)=0
holds, for example, when
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(S^{3}-K)
for a knot
K
in
S^{3}
, cf.
[
BZ98
,
\lx@sectionsign
3]
. In this case that
\Gamma=\pi_{1}(S^{3}-K)
, we further have
H^{1}(\Gamma,\mathbb{Z}/2)=\langle\iota\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
, and in fact,
A(\Gamma)\subset A(\Gamma)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
is stable under
\iota
, hence we define the
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}
-character variety over
\mathbb{Q}
as

Y(\Gamma):=\mathrm{Spec}(B(\Gamma)_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\langle\iota\rangle})

for
B(\Gamma)_{\mathbb{Q}}=A(\Gamma)_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\mathrm{PGL}_{2}}
, cf.
[
MPvL11
, 2.1.2]
. For example, for the meridian
m\in\pi_{1}(S^{3}-K)
,
\iota(\tau_{m})=-\tau_{m}
, so that
\tau_{m^{2}}=\tau_{m}^{2}-2\in B(\Gamma)_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\langle\iota\rangle}
.

There is another candidate for the
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
-character scheme of
\Gamma
which is defined as the
\mathbb{Z}
-affine scheme
\mathfrak{X}(\Gamma):=\mathrm{Spec}(T(\Gamma))
, where the affine algebra is the
trace ring
:

(6.2.2.1)

T(\Gamma):=\mathbb{Z}\langle\,\tau_{\gamma}:=\mathrm{tr}(\rho^{\mathrm{univ}}(\gamma))\ |\ \gamma\in\Gamma\,\rangle\ \subset A(\Gamma)

(subring of
A(\Gamma)
generated by
\tau_{\gamma}
for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
).

Let
S(\Gamma)
be the quotient algebra (
Skein algebra
):

\mathbb{Q}[Y_{\gamma},\gamma\in\Gamma]/\langle Y_{e}-2,\ Y_{\alpha\beta}+Y_{\alpha^{-1}\beta}-Y_{\alpha}Y_{\beta}\ \forall\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma\rangle.

By the skein relation for traces in
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
: for any representation
\rho:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}
, if
\tau:=\mathrm{tr}(\rho)
,

\tau(\alpha\beta)+\tau(\alpha^{-1}\beta)-\tau(\alpha)\tau(\beta)=0,

there exists a natural ring homomorphism

(6.2.2.2)

\displaystyle S(\Gamma)\

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\theta}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}\ T(\Gamma)_{\mathbb{Q}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i}}{{\hookrightarrow}}A(\Gamma)_{\mathbb{Q}}^{\mathrm{PGL}_{2}}

\displaystyle Y_{\gamma}\

\displaystyle\longmapsto\ \tau_{\gamma}:=\mathrm{tr}(\rho^{\mathrm{univ}}(\gamma)).

Theorem 6.2.3
.

[
PS00
, Thm.7.1]

The maps
\theta
,
i
are isomorphisms.

See also
[
Mar16
, Thm.2.5]
.

Remark 6.2.4
.

There are at least two advantages of
\mathfrak{X}(\Gamma)
over
X(\Gamma)
: first,
\mathfrak{X}(\Gamma)
is a scheme defined over
\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})
having a nice moduli interpretation, thus provides a natural integral model of
X(\Gamma)=\mathfrak{X}(\Gamma)_{\mathbb{Q}}
, whereas GIT quotient seems to work best only for field bases and flat base changes, so that it is not clear at all whether
\mathrm{Spec}(A(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{PGL}_{2}})
is a “good” integral model of
X(\Gamma)
.
The second advantage is the property (
[
Pro98
]
,
[
Sai96
]
) that over the open subset of
\mathfrak{X}(\Gamma)
consisting of
absolutely irreducible
representations/characters, the morphism
R(\Gamma)\rightarrow\mathfrak{X}(\Gamma)
(of
\mathbb{Z}
-schemes) becomes a torsor under
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}
for the étale topology, where absolutely irreducible characters in
\mathfrak{X}(\Gamma)
are defined in terms of certain discriminant, cf.
[
Nak00
,
\lx@sectionsign
4]
.

Suppose that
\Gamma
is finitely generated, say by
\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{r}
. Let
\{\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{N}\}
be the set of all elements of
\Gamma
of the form
\gamma_{i_{1}}\cdots\gamma_{i_{r}}
, where
i_{1},\cdots,i_{r}
are positive integers in
\{1,\cdots,m\}
with
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{r}
(e.g. if
m=2
, this set is
\{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}=\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}\}
).
Then, Culler and Shalen (
[
CS83
, 1.4.1,1.4.5]
) show that as a subalgebra of
A(\Gamma)
,
T(\Gamma)
is generated by
\tau_{\gamma_{1}},\cdots,\tau_{\gamma_{N}}
, and that the image of the map

\displaystyle R(\Gamma)(\mathbb{C})\ \rightarrow

\displaystyle\quad\mathbb{C}^{N}

\displaystyle\rho\quad\mapsto

\displaystyle\ (\tau_{\gamma_{1}}(\rho),\cdots,\tau_{\gamma_{N}}(\rho))=(\mathrm{tr}\rho(\gamma_{1}),\cdots,\mathrm{tr}\rho(\gamma_{N})).

is a Zariski-closed subset of
\mathbb{C}^{N}
.
We give this image the structure of a reduced affine variety and denote it by
X(\Gamma;\mathbb{C})
: this is called
Culler-Shalen character variety
.
If
z_{1},\cdots,z_{N}
are the coordinate functions of
\mathbb{A}^{N}
, we have an algebra homomorphism

t^{\ast}:\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\cdots,z_{N}]=\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{A}^{N})\rightarrow T(\Gamma)=\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{X}(\Gamma))
defined by
t^{\ast}(z_{i})=\tau_{\gamma_{i}}
, thus obtain a morphism of affine varieties
t:\mathfrak{X}(\Gamma)\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{N}
.
Clearly, the set
X(\Gamma;\mathbb{C})(\mathbb{C})
is contained in the image of the map
\mathfrak{X}(\Gamma)(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{N}
: the map
\phi:T(\Gamma)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}:\tau_{\gamma}\mapsto\mathrm{tr}(\rho(\gamma))
attached to a representation
\rho:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is a ring homomorphism.
In fact, it follows from Theorem
6.2.2
and Theorem
6.2.3
that

X(\Gamma;\mathbb{C})(\mathbb{C})
is the entire image, namely that any ring homomorphism
\phi:T(\Gamma)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is the character of a representation
\rho:\Gamma\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
: for (absolutely) irreducible characters, this is a part of the statement cited above about
R(\Gamma)\rightarrow\mathfrak{X}(\Gamma)
being a torsor over the irreducible locus (a torso is a surjective map, among others). Therefore, we have a finite surjective morphism of reduced affine varieties:

t:X(\Gamma)_{\mathbb{C}}^{\mathrm{red}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathfrak{X}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow X(\Gamma;\mathbb{C}),

which are bijective on
\mathbb{C}
(or any geometric) points.
Such a morphism does not need to be an isomorphism in general (but, it is so, for example, if the target is a normal variety).

6.3  Thurston deformation space of cusped hyperbolic three-manifolds

For the discussion in this subsection, our main references are
[
NZ85
]
and Ch. E, Sec. 5 (E.5) of
[
BP92
]
.
Let
M
be the interior of a compact three-manifold
\overline{M}
whose boundary
\partial\overline{M}
is a union of tori (we will just write
\partial M
for
\partial\overline{M}
).
Suppose that there exists a
topological ideal triangulation

\mathcal{T}
of
M
, namely a collection of standard tetrahedra with vertices removed which glue together by a set of face-pairing maps with the resulting space
M
; removing open stars at vertices from the glued union of tetrahedra gives
\overline{M}
, cf.
[
BP92
, E.5-i]
.

Thurston’s idea for finding a hyperbolic structure on
M
endowed with such a (topological) ideal triangulation is, starting with some hyperbolic structures on the constituent ideal tetrahedra, to find conditions ensuring that these local hyperbolic structures glue consistently giving a global hyperbolic metric on
M
. This gluing condition is that at each edge
e
in
\mathcal{T}
, the product of the shape parameters of the tetrahedra joined to
e
is
1
and the sum of the dihedral angles at
e
of these tetrahedra is equal to
2\pi
. When this condition is satisfied at all edges, we obtain a hyperbolic structure on
M
which is in general not complete.
We recall that when an ordering of the vertices is fixed, to each edge of the tetrahedron,
one can assign one of the three shape parameters
z
,
z^{\prime}=\frac{1}{1-z}
,
z^{\prime\prime}=1-\frac{1}{z}
, in such a way that the parameters associated with two opposite edges are equal and all
z
,
z^{\prime}
,
z^{\prime\prime}
are attached to some different (pairs of opposite) edges (
[
Neu92
,
\lx@sectionsign
2]
).
Since the dihedral angle at an edge whose associated parameter is
z
(resp.
z^{\prime}
,
z^{\prime\prime}
) is
\log z
(resp.
\log z^{\prime}
,
\log z^{\prime\prime}
), from the condition on dihedral angle sum, by exponentiating we obtain an algebraic relation in the shape parameters
\{z_{i}\}_{i=1,\cdots,N}
of the form:

(6.3.0.1)

\prod_{i=1}^{N}z_{i}^{r_{ij}^{\prime}}(1-z_{i})^{r_{ij}^{\prime\prime}}=\pm 1.

The set of these equations for all edges define a closed complex subvariety

Y(M;\mathcal{T})\subset(\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\backslash\{0,1,\infty\})^{N},

where
N
is the number of edges in
\mathcal{T}
;
N
is also equal to the number of the tetrahera in
\mathcal{T}
since the Euler characteristic of
\overline{M}
is zero
[
BP92
, Lem.E.5.6]
. This is called the
deformation variety
for the chosen ideal triangulation, and the algebraic relations (
6.3.0.1
) are called
gluing equations
.

Remark 6.3.1
.

(1) The gluing equation does not capture faithfully the dihedral-angle-sum condition (it only tells that the dihedral angle sum is a multiple of
2\pi i
). But, it is easy to see (
[
BP92
, Lem.E.6.1]
) that a
positive
solution (i.e.
\mathrm{Im}(z_{i})>0
) does fulfill the dihedral angle sum condition, so indeed defines a hyperbolic structure. Unfortunately, given a topological ideal triangulation, there might not exist such positive solution giving the hyperbolic structure on
M
.

(2) Nevertheless, Epstein and Penner
[
EP88
]
showed that every complete hyperbolic three-manifold with cusps of finite volume admits an ideal topological triangulation that supports a solution to the gluing equations and completeness equations which “give the hyperbolic structure”. But, the shape parameters have only non-negative imaginary parts and can be real numbers (i.e.

(M;\mathcal{T})
can have
degenerate
ideal tetrahedra).

(3) Epstein-Penner decomposition above can be used to give an
degree-one ideal triangulation
in the sense of
[
NY99
, Def.2.1]
, which suffices for our purpose in this work.
13
13

13

Indeed, the only input for our construction of the mixed Tate motive attached to any complete hyperbolic three-manifold of finite volume in Theorem
4.2.4
was the Bloch invariant
\beta(X)\in\mathcal{B}(k(X))_{\mathbb{Q}}
which was constructed by Neuamnn and Yang (
loc. cit.
) using any degree-one ideal triangulation.

It is known
[
Thu97
]
that the dimension of the deformation space of a complete hyperbolic structure equals the number of cusps, and there exists a unique component in
Y(M;\mathcal{T})
containing every solution giving the
complete
hyperbolic structure: it is called the
canonical component
.

For example, in the case of the Figure-eight knot complement
M=S^{3}\backslash K
, there exists an ideal triangulation with two tetrahedra, and such that

Y(M;\mathcal{T})=V(x(1-x)y(1-y)-1)\subset(\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\backslash\{0,1,\infty\})^{2}.

The smooth projective completion
\overline{Y(M;\mathcal{T})}
of this curve is an elliptic curve over
\mathbb{Q}
with four ideal points (=points in
\overline{Y(M;\mathcal{T})}\backslash Y(M;\mathcal{T})
whose images under the map
\overline{Y(M;\mathcal{T})}\rightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{1}(\mathbb{C})\backslash\{0,1,\infty\})^{2}
have all of its coordinates in
\{0,1,\infty\}
). Its (minimal) Weierstrass model is:
y^{2}+xy+y=x^{3}+x^{2}
,
[
Fuj05
]
. On the other hand, the
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
-character variety
X(M)
of
M
has Weierstrass model
y^{2}=x^{3}-2x+1
,
[
Har11
]
.
14
14

14

These two curves are both
\mathbb{Q}
-elliptic curves and have very similar arithmetic properties:
\overline{Y(M;\mathcal{T})}
has conductor
15
, rank
0
, torsion subgroup
\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}
, while the character variety has conductor
40
, rank
0
, torsion subgroup
\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}
.

Suppose the chosen triangulation
\mathcal{T}
supports a hyperbolic structure
\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
which is not necessarily complete. For simplicity, we assume that
M
has a single cusp.
We fix an ordered basis
\{\mu,\nu\}
of
\pi_{1}(\partial M)
such that the induced orientation on
\partial M
(i.e. on the universal cover of
\partial M
which is identified with
\mathbb{R}\mu\oplus\mathbb{R}\nu
) agrees with the orientation on
\partial M
as the boundary of
M

(in the case of a knot complement, we will choose
\mu
and
\nu
respectively to be the classes of the meridian and the longitude
15
15

15

In this work, to denote the longitude, we use
\nu
instead of the conventional
\lambda
, which we reserve for other use.

endowed with orientations, well-defined up to simultaneous reversing).
Any small horosphere at each cusp cuts the boundary in a torus and this torus has an affine structure provided by its triangulation into the triangles
\Delta(0,1,z)\subset\mathbb{C}
. This defines a holonomy representation
\pi_{1}(\partial M)\rightarrow\mathrm{Aff}(\mathbb{C})
. Assume that the hyperbolic structure is complete, namely that the affine structure is Euclidean.
Then, the
derivatives
(i.e.
a
of
x\mapsto ax+b
) of the holonomy along
\mu
and
\nu
are expressed as

(6.3.1.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}(z)=\prod_{i=0}^{N}\left(\frac{z_{i}}{z_{i}^{0}}\right)^{m_{i}^{\prime}}\left(\frac{1-z_{i}}{1-z_{i}^{0}}\right)^{m_{i}^{\prime\prime}},\quad\mathfrak{l}(z)=\prod_{i=0}^{N}\left(\frac{z_{i}}{z_{i}^{0}}\right)^{l_{i}^{\prime}}\left(\frac{1-z_{i}}{1-z_{i}^{0}}\right)^{l_{i}^{\prime\prime}},

where
z^{0}
is the point on
Y(M;\mathcal{T})
corresponding to the complete hyperbolic structure (
[
NZ85
, the formula right after (27)]
).
(The constants
(m_{i}^{\prime},m_{i}^{\prime\prime})
,
(l_{i}^{\prime},l_{i}^{\prime\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
are determined by the coefficients of the cusp relations
C(\mathscr{Z}^{0})^{t}=\pi\sqrt{-1}D_{2}
(
2.3.0.3
).)
These can be regarded as regular functions on the affine variety
Y(M;\mathcal{T})
:

(6.3.1.2)

\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{l}\ :\ Y(M;\mathcal{T})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}.

Take an open neighborhood
U
of
z^{0}
in
Y(M;\mathcal{T})
and branches of

(6.3.1.3)

\mathfrak{u}=\log\mathfrak{m},\quad\mathfrak{v}=\log\mathfrak{l}

on
U
with
\mathfrak{u}(z^{0})=\mathfrak{v}(z^{0})=0
.
If
\rho:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is a honomomy representation corresponding to a point
z=\{z_{i}\}
of
Y
, then there exists a conjugate of
\rho_{z}
such that

(6.3.1.4)

\rho_{z}(\mu)=\pm\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{\frac{\mathfrak{u}(z)}{2}}&\ast\\
0&e^{-\frac{\mathfrak{u}(z)}{2}}\end{array}\right),\quad\rho_{z}(\nu)=\pm\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{\frac{\mathfrak{v}(z)}{2}}&\ast\\
0&e^{-\frac{\mathfrak{v}(z)}{2}}\end{array}\right)

(the associated affine transformations on any boundary torus cut by a horosphere are then
w\mapsto e^{\mathfrak{u}(z)}w+e^{-\frac{\mathfrak{u}(z)}{2}}\ast
,
w\mapsto e^{\mathfrak{v}(z)}w+e^{-\frac{\mathfrak{v}(z)}{2}}\ast
).

By a basic theorem of W. Thurston (cf.
[
NZ85
,
\lx@sectionsign
4]
), we may assume (after shrinking
U
if necessary) that
\mathfrak{u}:U\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a local holomorphic coordinate around
z^{0}
(in this situation
U
is often called a
Dehn surgery space
).

6.4  Kirk-Klassen construction of Chern-Simons line bundle and CS-section

Let
D(\partial M)
be the split algebraic torus over
\mathbb{Q}
with character group
\pi_{1}(\partial M)
: it is the affine
\mathbb{Q}
-scheme whose affine algebra is the group algebra
\mathbb{Q}[\pi_{1}(\partial M)]\simeq\mathbb{Q}[\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus 2h}]
, so that it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{m}}^{2h}
(an isomorphism being determined by a choice of a basis of
\pi_{1}(\partial M)
such as ours
\{\mu_{i},\nu_{i}\}_{i}
).
In particular, for any
\mathbb{Q}
-algebra
k
, the group
D(\partial M)(k)
of
k
-rational points consists of homomorphisms
\lambda:\pi_{1}(\partial M)\rightarrow k^{\times}
(group of units in
k
). We regard
D(\partial M)
as a subvariety of
R(\partial M)
via

\lambda\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\lambda&\\
&\lambda^{-1}\end{array}\right)
.
If
\iota
denotes the involution on
D(\partial M)
defined by
\lambda\mapsto\lambda^{-1}
, the canonical map
D(\partial M)\rightarrow X(\partial M)
induces a morphism

(6.4.0.1)

D(\partial M)\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\langle\iota\rangle\rightarrow X(\partial M)=R(\partial M)\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathrm{SL}_{2}:[\lambda]\mapsto\lambda+\lambda^{-1}.

This is an isomorphism of algebraic varieties
[
Ben20
, 1.14]
,
[
Sik14
, Thm.2.1]
.

For a separated scheme
X
of finite type over a field
k\subset\mathbb{C}
, let
X_{\mathbb{C}}^{an}
(or even
X^{an}
) denote the complex analytic variety associated with the reduced closed subscheme
X^{red}
of
X
.

For the next discussion, for simplicity, let us assume
h=1
: the argument easily generalizes to arbitrary
h
.
Let
\Delta:=\mathbb{Z}^{2}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
with
\iota\neq 1\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
acting on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
by
(a,b)\mapsto(-a,-b)
; let
\{\mu,\nu\}
be the standard basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\subset\Delta
(this choice of the notation
\{\iota,\mu,\nu\}
will be compatible with the previous choice). We make
\Delta
act on
(\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathbb{C})\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
by

(6.4.0.2)

\displaystyle\nu:

\displaystyle(x,y;z)\mapsto(x+2\pi i,y;ze^{y})

\displaystyle\mu:

\displaystyle(x,y;z)\mapsto(x,y+2\pi i;ze^{-x})

\displaystyle\iota:

\displaystyle(x,y;z)\mapsto(-x,-y;z)

(be wary of the roles of
\mu
and
\nu
).
Taking quotient of the trivial fibration
\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}
by this analytic group action, we obtain a principal
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundle over the analytic variety
X(\partial M)^{an}
:

E(\partial M)\rightarrow X(\partial M)^{an}.

In more detail, we first take the quotient of the trivial fibration
\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}
by the subgroup
\langle\mu,\nu\rangle
of
\Delta
(which acts freely on both spaces), obtaining a principal
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundle
Q(\partial M)
over the analytic variety
D(\partial M)^{an}
:

Q(\partial M)\rightarrow D(\partial M)^{an}.

Then from the fact that
\iota
induces an action of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundle
Q(\partial M)
covering an action on
D(\partial M)_{\mathbb{C}}
which acts trivially on the fibers over its fixed points in
D(\partial M)_{\mathbb{C}}

[
KK93
, p.525]
, it follows that this principal
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundle
Q(\partial M)
descends to
X(\partial M)^{an}
, namely there exists a principal
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundle
E(\partial M)
over
X(\partial M)^{an}
whose pull-back to
D(\partial M)^{an}
is isomorphic to
Q(\partial M)
. Indeed, the condition implies that for a suitable local trivialization of
Q(\partial M)
over
D(\partial M)^{an}
, the transition functions are invariant under
\iota
, thus since the natural map
\mathcal{O}(X(\partial M)^{an})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}(D(\partial M)^{an})^{\mathrm{PGL}_{2}}
is an isomorphism (
[
Nee88
, Thm.8]
), they belong to analytic functions on
X(\partial M)^{an}
, defining a
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundle on the latter (for more details, see
[
DN89
, Theoreme2.3]
whose arguments in the algebraic setup carry over to our analytic setup, given the fact just cited).
This analytic principal
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundle
E(\partial M)
over
X(\partial M)^{an}
is unique up to isomorphism; we call it
Chern-Simons
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-(or line) bundle
. Although the description makes use of the choice of an oriented basis of
\pi_{1}(\partial M)
compatible with the orientation of
\partial M
, the bundle
E(\partial M)
itself depends only on the orientation of
\partial M
.

Theorem 6.4.1
.

[
KK93
, 2.5, 3.4]

Suppose that
A
,
B
are gauge-equivalent connection
1
-forms on
M\times\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
which are in normal form near boundary and such that

A=A^{(1)}(\alpha,\beta),\quad B=\epsilon A^{(1)}(\alpha+p,\beta+q),

(
2.2.4.1
) for some
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
,
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
in Case 1, and

A=A^{(2)}(-2\alpha,-2\beta;a,b),\quad B=\epsilon A^{(2)}(-2(\alpha+p),-2(\beta+q);a^{\prime},b^{\prime})

(
2.2.4.2
) for some
\alpha,\beta\in\frac{\mathbb{Z}}{2}
,
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
a,b,a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}
,
\epsilon\in\{\pm 1\}
in Case 2.

Then, we have
cs(B)-cs(A)=p\beta-q\alpha\mod\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

When the boundary holonomy is diagonalizable, the proof of Theorem
6.4.1
in the
\mathrm{SU}(2)
case works without change, too. When the boundary holonomy is parabolic, this is proved in
ibid.
Lemma 3.4, 3.5.
∎

Theorem 6.4.2
.

Define a map
\bar{C}_{\mathbf{K}\!\mathbf{K}}:R(M)(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow E(\partial M)
by

(6.4.2.1)

C_{\mathbf{K}\!\mathbf{K}}(\rho)=[\,2\pi i\,\alpha_{1},\,2\pi i\,\beta_{1},\,\cdots,\,2\pi i\,\alpha_{h},\,2\pi i\,\beta_{h}\,;\,e^{2\pi i\,cs(A)}\,].

Here, given
\rho
, for each
i=1,\cdots,h
, we choose any
(\alpha,\beta)
such that a conjugate of
\rho|_{\pi_{1}(\partial M)}
is of the form (
2.2.5
) for
(\alpha,\beta)
if
\rho|_{\pi_{1}(\partial M)}
is in Case 1, or any
(u,v,a,b)
such that a conjugate of
\rho|_{\pi_{1}(\partial M)}
is of the form (
2.2.5
) for
(u,v,a,b)
if
\rho|_{\pi_{1}(\partial M)}
is in Case 2, and set
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
to be
(\alpha,\beta)
in Case 1 or
(-\frac{u}{2},-\frac{v}{2})
in Case 2. And,
A
is any flat connection with holonomy being (a conjugate of)
\rho
and such that for each
i
,
A
is in normal form for the just chosen constants
(\alpha,\beta)
,
(u,v,a,b)
(Definition
2.2.4
).

Then,
\bar{C}_{\mathbf{K}\!\mathbf{K}}
induces a well-defined map
C_{\mathbf{K}\!\mathbf{K}}:X(M)(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow E(\partial M)
, giving a holomorphic section over
X(M)_{\mathbb{C}}
of
E(\partial M)\rightarrow X(\partial M)_{\mathbb{C}}
:

\textstyle{E(\partial M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X(M)_{\mathbb{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C_{\mathbf{K}\!\mathbf{K}}}
\textstyle{X(\partial M)_{\mathbb{C}}.}

(2) Let
\rho_{t}:\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C}),\ t\in[0,1]
be a path of representations avoiding non-central boundary-parabolic ones.
Suppose that
\rho_{t}|_{\pi_{1}(\partial M)}
is of the shape (
2.2.5
) for some smooth
(\underline{\alpha}(t),\underline{\beta}(t)):[0,1]\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2h}
, where
\underline{\alpha}(t)=(\alpha_{1}(t),\cdots,\alpha_{h}(t))
,
\underline{\beta}(t)=(\beta_{1}(t),\cdots,\beta_{h}(t))
. If
C_{\mathbf{K}\!\mathbf{K}}([\rho_{t}])=[\underline{\alpha}(t),\underline{\beta}(t);z(t)]
, we have

z(1)z(0)^{-1}=\exp(\frac{1}{2\pi i}(\int_{0}^{1}\underline{\alpha}(t)\frac{\underline{\beta}(t)}{dt}-\underline{\beta}(t)\frac{\underline{\alpha}(t)}{dt})).

Proof.

This is
[
KK93
, Thm.3.2]
. The holomorphicity in (1) is a consequence of (2).
∎

6.5  Augmented character variety

We introduce the augmented character variety of three manifolds with toral boundary.

Definition 6.5.1
.

The
augmented character variety

\tilde{X}(M)
is the fiber product of
X(M)\rightarrow X(\partial M)
and
D(\partial M)\rightarrow R(\partial M)\rightarrow X(\partial M)
:

\tilde{X}(M):=X(M)\times_{X(\partial M)}D(\partial M).

Remark 6.5.2
.

(1) In view of the isomorphism (
6.4.0.1
),
\tilde{X}(M)
is a double covering of
X(M)
.
For an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic zero, a
k
-rational point of
\tilde{X}(M)
is a
k
-point
(\chi,\lambda)
in
X(M)\times D(\partial M)
such that the restriction of
\chi
to
\pi_{1}(\partial M)
is the character of the diagonal representation
\delta_{\lambda}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\lambda&\\
&\lambda^{-1}\end{array}\right)
. Be warned, however, that this condition implies, but not equivalent to, that
\lambda(\gamma)+\lambda^{-1}(\gamma)=\chi(\gamma)
for all
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\partial M)
: for any given
\chi
, there are in general
2^{2h}
many
\lambda\in\mathrm{Hom}(\pi_{1}(\partial M),k^{\times})
’s satisfying this latter condition since
\pi_{1}(\partial M)\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{2h}
, while in the definition of
\tilde{X}(M)
we are choosing one among two
\lambda
’s satisfying the condition that there exists a representation
\rho\in R(M)(k)
with
\chi(\rho)|_{\pi_{1}(\partial M)}=\lambda+\lambda^{-1}
(equiv. that some conjugate of the diagonal representation
\delta_{\lambda}
lifts to a representation of
\pi_{1}(M)
).

(2) A major weakness of the character variety
X(M)
, compared with the Thurston deformation curves, is the absence of a single-valued eigenvalue (global regular) function
\lambda
with the property as above: on the deformation curve, we have
\mathfrak{m}
,
\mathfrak{l}
, the derivatives of the holonomy along
\mu
and
\nu
, which are of great utility in the study of cusped hyperbolic
3
-manifolds, as in the example of hyperbolic Dehn surgery.
But Thurston deformation curves also has the weakness that their definition depends on the choice of an ideal triangulation supporting the complete hyperbolic structure, while
X(M)
is canonically defined by
M
(equivalently,
\pi_{1}(M)\subset\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
) only. The augmented character variety has both features. It is a triangulation-choice-free analogue of deformation curve.

(3) The augmented character variety is also a GIT quotient
\tilde{X}(M)=\tilde{R}(M)\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\mathrm{PGL}_{2}
, where

\tilde{R}(M)
is the
augmented representation variety

\tilde{R}(M):=R(M)\times_{R(\partial M)}D(\partial M)

and
\mathrm{PGL}_{2}
acts on
\tilde{R}(M)
via
R(M)
.

(4) The augmented character variety was introduced earlier. Recently, Benard
[
Ben20
]
showed that the adjoint Reidemeister torsion can be interpretated as a rational volume form on the augmented character variety, and related its vanishing order at a point is related to the singularity of the image of the point in the character variety.

Recall that
N_{1},\cdots,N_{h}
are boundary tori of
\partial M
and that the affine algebra
\mathbb{Q}[D(\partial M)]
is isomorphic to the group algebra
\mathbb{Q}[\pi_{1}(\partial M)]
, so every element of
\pi_{1}(\partial M)
can be regarded as a regular function on
D(\partial M)
.

Definition 6.5.3
.

For each
i=1,\cdots,h
, let
f_{\mu_{i}}(z)
and
f_{\nu_{i}}(z)
be the regular functions on
D(\partial M)
corresponding to
\mu_{i},\nu_{i}\in\pi_{1}(N_{i})
, and
m_{i}(z)
and
l_{i}(z)
the regular functions on
\tilde{X}(M)
obtained by pull-back from
f_{\mu_{i}}(z)
and
f_{\nu_{i}}(z)
, respectively. Put
\underline{l}:=(l_{1},\cdots,l_{h})\in\mathcal{O}(\tilde{X}(M))^{\oplus h}
,
\underline{m}:=(m_{1},\cdots,m_{h})\in\mathcal{O}(\tilde{X}(M))^{\oplus h}
.

When we fix a point
\tilde{z}_{0}
of
\tilde{X}(M)
whose image in
X(M)
is the conjugacy class of a lift to
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
of the geometric representation
\rho_{0}
(corresponding to the complete hyperbolic structure), one can choose a small neighborhood
\tilde{V}
of
\tilde{z}^{0}
and branches of

u_{i}=\log m_{i}\text{ and }v_{i}=\log l_{i},

defined on
\tilde{V}
, with
u_{i}(\tilde{z}^{0})=0
,
v_{i}(\tilde{z}^{0})=0
, and analytically continue them, obtaining multi-valued functions to the irreducible component of
\tilde{X}(M)
containing
\tilde{z}^{0}
. On a small enough neighborhood
U
of
z^{0}
in the deformation curve
Y(M,\mathcal{T})
one can find an analytic function
f:U\rightarrow\tilde{X}(M)
with
f(z^{0})=\tilde{z}^{0}
. It follows from (
6.3.1.4
) that there exists the relation

(6.5.3.1)

\mathfrak{u}=2u\circ f,\quad\mathfrak{v}=2v\circ f.

6.6  Heisenberg line bundle and Morishita-Terashima Chern-Simons section

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(n)}\subset H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(n)}
be the integral and complex Heisenberg groups “of order
n
”: they consist of
(2n+1)\times(2n+1)
matrices such that

H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(n)}=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1_{n}&a&c\\
&\mathrm{I}_{n}&b^{t}\\
&&1_{n}^{t}\\
\end{array}\right)\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{c}a,b\in\mathbb{Z}(1)^{n},\\
c\in\mathbb{Z}(2)\end{array}\right\}\,\subset\,H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(n)}=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1_{n}&a&c\\
&\mathrm{I}_{n}&b^{t}\\
&&1_{n}^{t}\\
\end{array}\right)\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{c}a,b\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\\
c\in\mathbb{C}\end{array}\right\},
