where
1_{n}=(1,\cdots,1)\in\mathrm{M}_{1\times n}
.
We write
(a,b;c)^{\prime}
for the matrix
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1_{n}&a&c\\
&\mathrm{I}_{n}&b^{t}\\
&&1_{n}^{t}\\
\end{array}\right)
.
The quotient manifold
H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(n)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(n)}
is a principal
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundle over
(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}\times(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
via the map
H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(n)}(a,b;c)^{\prime}\mapsto(\exp(a),\exp(b))
and carries a flat connection
1
-form
\theta=dc-a\cdot db^{t}
. To see the principal
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundle structure more explicitly, let us define put a group structure on the complex manifold
\mathbb{C}^{2n}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
by

(v;z)\ast(v^{\prime};z^{\prime})=(v+v^{\prime};zz^{\prime}e[v_{1}\cdot(v_{2}^{\prime})^{t}]),

where
v=(v_{1},v_{2}),v^{\prime}=(v_{1}^{\prime},v_{2}^{\prime})\,(v_{i},v_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{n})
,
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, and
e[u]:=\exp(\frac{1}{2\pi i}u)
. This complex Lie group
H^{(n)}
is an extension of Lie groups:

1\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\longrightarrow}}H^{(n)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{H}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{C}^{2n}\longrightarrow 0

with
j(z)=(0;z)
and
p_{H}(v;z)=v
.
Then, the map
H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(n)}\rightarrow H^{(n)}
defined by
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1_{n}&a&c\\
&\mathrm{I}_{n}&b^{t}\\
&&1_{n}^{t}\\
\end{array}\right)\mapsto(a,b;e[c])

induces an isomorphism of discrete subgroups
H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(n)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}(1)^{\oplus 2n}\times\{1\}
, thus
an analytic isomorphism of principal
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundle

H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(n)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(n)}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\ \mathbb{Z}(1)^{\oplus 2n}\backslash H^{(n)}

over
\mathbb{Z}(1)^{\oplus 2n}\backslash\mathbb{C}^{2n}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2n}
, cf.
[
Ram89
,
\lx@sectionsign
4]
.

Let
X
be a smooth irreducible subvariety of a Thurston deformation variety
Y(M;\mathcal{T})
containing the geometric point, and let
\langle\underline{l},\underline{m}^{2}\rangle
denote the line bundle over
X
obtained as the pull-back of
H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(n)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(n)}
by the holomorphic map
(\underline{l},\underline{m}^{2}):X\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}\times(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{n}
.
When
h=1
and
X
is the canonical (curve) component of , Morishita and Terashima
[
MT09
, Thm.4.1]
constructed a section of this bundle
\langle\underline{l},\underline{m}^{2}\rangle
over
X
, which thus depends on the choice of an ideal triangulation defining the deformation curve. Here, we emulate their construction to (the canonical component of) the augmented character variety. Put
\zeta:(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2h}\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2h}:(l,m)\mapsto(l,m^{2})
.

Theorem 6.6.1
.

Define a map
\bar{C}_{\mathbf{M}\!\mathbf{T}}:\tilde{R}(M)\rightarrow H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(h)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}
by

(6.6.1.1)

z\mapsto H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(h)}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1_{n}&\underline{v}(z)&(2\pi i)^{2}cs([A_{z}])+\underline{u}(z)\cdot\underline{v}(z)^{t}\\
&\mathrm{I}_{n}&2\underline{u}(z)^{t}\\
&&1_{n}^{t}\\
\end{array}\right),

where given
z
, for each
i=1,\cdots,h
, we take
(\alpha_{i},\beta_{i})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
, plus
(a_{i},b_{i})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
in Case 2, as in Theorem
6.4.2
and put
\underline{v}(z):=(\alpha_{1}(z),\cdots,\alpha_{h}(z))
,
\underline{u}(z):=(\beta_{1}(z),\cdots,\beta_{h}(z))
,
and
A_{z}
is a flat connection with holonomy being (a conjugate of)
\rho_{z}
and such that for each
i
,
A_{z}
is in normal form for the constants
(\alpha_{i}(z),\beta_{i}(z))
, plus
(a_{i}(z),b_{i}(z))
in Case 2, just chosen.

Then,
\bar{C}_{\mathbf{M}\!\mathbf{T}}
induces a well-defined map
C_{\mathbf{M}\!\mathbf{T}}:\tilde{X}(M)_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(h)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}
covering the composite of the canonical map
\tilde{X}(M)_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow D(\partial M)_{\mathbb{C}}=(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2h}
and
\zeta
. This is a holomorphic flat section over
\tilde{X}(M)_{\mathbb{C}}
of
H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(h)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{h}\times(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{h}
:

\textstyle{H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(h)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tilde{X}(M)_{\mathbb{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{C_{\mathbf{M}\!\mathbf{T}}}
\textstyle{(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{h}\times(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{h}.}

The same proof of Morishita-Terashima
[
MT07
, Thm.4.1]
, which is based on
[
KK93
, 2.5, 3.4]
(Theorem
6.4.1
here), works without change for our definition. Next, we compare the Kirk-Klassen section
C_{\mathbf{K}\!\mathbf{K}}
(Theorem
6.4.2
) and this Morishita-Terashima section
C_{\mathbf{M}\!\mathbf{T}}
.

We denote by
\langle\nu,\mu\rangle
the free abelian group with generators
\{\nu_{1},\mu_{1},\cdots,\nu_{h},\mu_{h}\}
endowed with the action on the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{C}^{2h}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
as defined in (
6.4.0.2
) and by
\Delta_{h}
the group
\langle\nu,\mu\rangle\rtimes\langle\iota\rangle
with
\iota
being also as defined there.

Proposition 6.6.2
.

(1) The natural map
\langle\nu,\mu\rangle\backslash\mathbb{C}^{2h}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}\rightarrow E(\partial M)=\Delta_{h}\backslash\mathbb{C}^{2h}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
covering the quotient map
q:D(\partial M)=(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2h}\rightarrow D(\partial M)\mathbin{/\mkern-6.0mu/}\langle\iota\rangle=X(\partial M)
induces an isomorphism

\langle\nu,\mu\rangle\backslash\mathbb{C}^{2h}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}q^{\ast}E(\partial M)

of principal
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundles.

(2) The (multi-valued) map
f:\mathbb{C}^{2h}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}\rightarrow H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}
defined by

(x,y;z)\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1_{h}&x&\pi i\log z+\frac{1}{2}x\cdot y^{t}\\
&\mathrm{I}_{h}&y^{t}\\
&&1_{h}^{t}\end{array}\right)

induces an isomorphism of analytic spaces

(6.6.2.1)

\langle\nu,\mu\rangle\backslash\mathbb{C}^{2h}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\tilde{H}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(h)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}

covering the identity map of
D(\partial M)=(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2h}
, where

\tilde{H}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(n)}=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1_{n}&a&c\\
&\mathrm{I}_{n}&b^{t}\\
&&1_{n}^{t}\\
\end{array}\right)\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{c}a,b\in\mathbb{Z}(1)^{n},\\
c\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}(2)\end{array}\right\}.

(3) Let
g\ :\ H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}\rightarrow H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}
be the group homomorphism
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1_{h}&x&z\\
&\mathrm{I}_{h}&y^{t}\\
&&1_{h}^{t}\\
\end{array}\right)\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1_{h}&x&2z\\
&\mathrm{I}_{h}&2y^{t}\\
&&1_{h}^{t}\\
\end{array}\right)
.
The induced map

(6.6.2.2)

\tilde{H}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(h)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}\twoheadrightarrow H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(h)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}

which covers the map
\zeta:(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2h}\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2h}:(l,m)\mapsto(l,m^{2})
is a pull-back diagram.

Note that the two maps in (2) and (3) are not compatible with the right
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-actions, so are not maps of principal
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundles, while their composite
g\circ f
is so.

Proof.

For simplicity, we assume that
h=1
and write
H_{\mathbb{C}}=H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(1)}
,
H_{\mathbb{Z}}=H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(1)}
,
\tilde{H}_{\mathbb{Z}}=\tilde{H}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(1)}
.

(1) This is obvious since
E(\partial M)=\Delta_{h}\backslash\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.

(2) First, a different choice of
\log z
changes the original image by a left-translation:

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&x&\pi i(\log z+2\pi in)+\frac{1}{2}xy\\
&1&y\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&(2\pi i)^{2}\frac{n}{2}\\
&1&0\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&x&\pi i\log z+\frac{1}{2}xy\\
&1&y\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right).

The action
\nu:(x,y;z)\mapsto(x+2\pi i,y;ze^{y})
corresponds to a left-translation:

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&x+2\pi i&\pi i(\log z+y)+\frac{1}{2}(x+2\pi i)y\\
&1&y\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&2\pi i&0\\
&1&0\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&x&2\pi i\log z+\frac{1}{2}xy\\
&1&y\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right).

Similarly, the action
\mu:(x,y;z)\mapsto(x,y+2\pi i;ze^{-x})
corresponds to another left-translation:

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&x&\pi i(\log z-x)+\frac{1}{2}x(y+2\pi i)\\
&1&y+2\pi i\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
&1&2\pi i\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&x&\pi i\log z+\frac{1}{2}xy\\
&1&y\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right).

Hence, the given map induces an isomorphism
\langle\nu,\mu\rangle\backslash\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\rightarrow}}\tilde{H}_{\mathbb{Z}}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}
of (analytic) spaces covering the identity map.

(3) For
x\in\mathbb{C}
, write
\bar{x}
for
x\text{ mod }2\pi i\mathbb{Z}
. We have to show that for each
(\bar{x},\bar{y})\in(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}
, the fiber of
H_{\mathbb{Z}}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2}
over
(\bar{x},\bar{y}^{2}=\overline{2y})
is in bijection under
g
with the fiber of
\tilde{H}_{\mathbb{Z}}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}
over
(\bar{x},\bar{y})
. The former fiber is the union of the
H_{\mathbb{Z}}
-right cosets

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&2\pi i\,l&(2\pi i)^{2}n\\
&1&2\pi i\,m\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&x&z\\
&1&2y\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&x+2\pi i\,l&2(\frac{z}{2}+2\pi i\,ly+(2\pi i)^{2}\frac{n}{2})\\
&1&2(y+2\pi i\,m)\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)

with
z
running through
\mathbb{C}
(
l,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
).
But, for each
z\in\mathbb{C}
, the inverse image under
g
of this subset:

\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&x+2\pi i\,l&\frac{z}{2}+2\pi i\,ly+(2\pi i)^{2}\frac{n}{2}\\
&1&y+2\pi i\,m\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)\ |\ l,m,n\in\mathbb{Z}\right\}

is precisely the
\tilde{H}_{\mathbb{Z}}
-right coset of
\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&x&\frac{z}{2}\\
&1&y\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)
.
∎

Corollary 6.6.3
.

There exist canonical isomorphisms of principal
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundles over
D(\partial M)
:

q^{\ast}E(\partial M)\cong\langle\nu,\mu\rangle\backslash\mathbb{C}^{2h}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}\cong\zeta^{\ast}(H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(h)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}).

Let us introduce another integral subgroup
\bar{H}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(n)}
of
H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(n)}
containing
H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(n)}
:

\bar{H}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(n)}=\left\{\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1_{n}&a&c\\
&\mathrm{I}_{n}&b^{t}\\
&&1_{n}^{t}\\
\end{array}\right)\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{c}a,b\in 2\mathbb{Z}(1)^{n},\\
c\in 4\mathbb{Z}(2)\end{array}\right\},

and define a map
d:H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(n)}\rightarrow H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(n)}
by

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1_{h}&x&z\\
&\mathrm{I}_{h}&y^{t}\\
&&1_{h}^{t}\\
\end{array}\right)\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1_{h}&2x&4z\\
&\mathrm{I}_{h}&2y^{t}\\
&&1_{h}^{t}\\
\end{array}\right).

Proposition 6.6.4
.

(1) The two sections
q^{\ast}C_{\mathbf{K}\!\mathbf{K}}
,
\zeta^{\ast}C_{\mathbf{M}\!\mathbf{T}}
of
q^{\ast}E(\partial M)\cong\zeta^{\ast}(H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(h)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)})
over
\widetilde{X}(M)
are equal.

(2) For any (sufficiently small) Dehn surgery space
V
of
Y(M;\mathcal{T})
which thus admits a map into
\widetilde{X}(M)
, the two maps
V\rightarrow\bar{H}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(h)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}
, one induced by
C_{\mathbf{M}\!\mathbf{T}}:V\rightarrow H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(h)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}
and the original Morishita-Terashima section
Y(M;\mathcal{T})\rightarrow\bar{H}_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(h)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}

[
MT07
, Thm.4.1]
coincide.

Proof.

These are obvious from Theorem 4.1 in
[
MT07
]
, taking into account that their
u(z)
and
v(z)
are
\mathfrak{u}(z)
and
\mathfrak{v}(z)
in our notations (
6.3.1.3
) which are equal to our
2u(z)
and
2v(z)
(
6.5.3.1
), and thus the Chern-Simons invariant
CS_{X}(z)
used in their definition of
\tilde{\Phi}(z)
is four times our
cs_{X}(z)
.
∎

Here, all square diagrams are pull-back diagrams.

Before finishing this subsection, we give another description of the Heisenberg bundle
H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(h)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}
.
Let
\langle\nu^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}\rangle
be the free abelian group of rank
2h
with generators
\{\nu_{1}^{\prime},\mu_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,\nu_{h}^{\prime},\mu_{h}^{\prime}\}
endowed with the action on
\mathbb{C}^{2h}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
by

(6.6.4.1)

\displaystyle\nu^{\prime}_{j}:

\displaystyle(x,\,y\,;\,z)\mapsto(x+2\pi i\,e_{j},\,y\,;\,ze^{y_{j}/2})

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}_{j}:

\displaystyle(x,\,y\,;\,z)\mapsto(x,\,y+2\pi i\,e_{j}\,;\,ze^{-x_{j}/2}),

where
x=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{h})
,
y=(y_{1},\cdots,y_{h})
, and
\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{h}\}
is the standard basis of
\mathbb{Z}^{h}
.

Proposition 6.6.5
.

The (multi-valued) map
\mathbb{C}^{2h}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}\rightarrow H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}
defined by

(x,y;z)\mapsto\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1_{h}&x&2\pi i\log z+\frac{1}{2}x\cdot y^{t}\\
&\mathrm{I}_{h}&y^{t}\\
&&1_{h}^{t}\end{array}\right)

induces an isomorphism

P:=\langle\nu^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}\rangle\backslash\mathbb{C}^{2h}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}\rightarrow H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(h)}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(h)}

of principal
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundles over
D(\partial M)=(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{2h}
.

The principal
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
-bundle
\langle\nu,\mu\rangle\backslash\mathbb{C}^{2h}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is
P^{\otimes 2}
, tensor square of the line bundle
P
.

The proof is an obvious modification of the argument of Proposition
6.6.2
. The second statement follows from comparison of the two actions (
6.4.0.2
), (
6.6.4.1
).

7
Chern-Simons variation of mixed Hodge structure over
\tilde{X}(M)_{0}^{\mathrm{sm}}

7.1  Tate variation of mixed Hodge structure

In the rest of this article, we use the notation
H_{\mathbb{Z}}:=H_{\mathbb{Z}}^{(1)}
,
H_{\mathbb{C}}:=H_{\mathbb{C}}^{(1)}
for the Heisenberg groups of order
1
(Subsection
6.6
).
For the split mixed
\mathbb{Z}
-Hodge structure
V_{0}=\mathbb{Z}(0)\oplus\mathbb{Z}(1)\oplus\mathbb{Z}(2)
, the simply connected
\mathbb{Q}
-algebraic group
G
with Lie algebra
W_{-1}\mathrm{End}(V_{0})_{\mathbb{Q}}
is
H_{\mathbb{Z}}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
.
So, by
[
Hai94
, Prop.9.1]
(cf. Proposition
3.1.6
), the complex manifold
H_{\mathbb{Z}}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}
can be regarded as a moduli space of mixed Hodge structures endowed with an isomorphism of the associated weight-graded quotient with
\mathbb{Z}(0)\oplus\mathbb{Z}(1)\oplus\mathbb{Z}(2)
. With this interpretation, the projection map
H_{\mathbb{Z}}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
sends a mixed Hodge structure
V\in H_{\mathbb{Z}}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}
to

(V/W_{-4}V,W_{-2}V)\in\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z}(1))\times\mathrm{Ext}^{1}(\mathbb{Z}(1),\mathbb{Z}(2))\simeq\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}.

We refer to
[
HZ87a
]
,
[
HZ87b
]
for discussions of “a good unipotent variation of mixed Hodge structure”.
Its associated graded quotient variations of pure Hodge structures are all known to be constant. A
Tate variation of mixed Hodge structure
16
16

16

or a
mixed Tate variation
, following
[
BD94
]

is a good unipotent variation of mixed Hodge structure whose associated graded quotient Hodge structures have only even weights and
\mathrm{Gr}_{2p}^{W}\mathbb{V}
is of Tate Hodge type
(-p,-p)
.

Theorem 7.1.1
.

[
HZ87a
]

If
\mathbb{V}\rightarrow X
is a Tate variation of mixed Hodge structure, then its canonical extension
\overline{\mathcal{V}}\rightarrow\overline{X}
(à la P. Deligne) is trivial as a holomorphic vector bundle and the extended weight and Hodge filtrations are also constant. Namely, there exists a complex vector space
V
endowed with filtrations
F^{\bullet}V
,
W_{\bullet}V
such that there exists an isomorphism of vector bundles
\overline{\mathcal{V}}\simeq V\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}
over
\overline{X}
under which the extended Hodge and weight filtrations are
F^{\bullet}V\times\overline{X}
,
W_{\bullet}V\times\overline{X}
.

Theorem 7.1.2
.

[
Hai94
, Prop. 9.4]

For a smooth variety
X
, a map

f:X\rightarrow H_{\mathbb{Z}}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}

is the classifying map of a variation of mixed Hodge structure over
X
with weight-graded quotients canonically isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}(0)
,
\mathbb{Z}(1)
,
\mathbb{Z}(2)
if and only if

(i)
f
is holomorphic;

(ii) the composite
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\rightarrow}}H_{\mathbb{Z}}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}
of
f
with the canonical projection is algebraic;

(iii) the map
f:X\rightarrow H_{\mathbb{Z}}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}
is a flat section of the bundle
H_{\mathbb{Z}}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{\times}\times\mathbb{C}^{\times}

7.2  Chern-Simons variation of mixed Hodge structure

From now on, in the rest of this article we assume that
h=1
so that the canonical component
X(M)_{0}
of
X(M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
, the irreducible component containing all lifts of
[\rho_{0}]
, is a curve. We define the
augmented canonical (curve) component
of
\tilde{X}(M)
as

(7.2.0.1)

\tilde{X}(M)_{0}:=X(M)_{0}\times_{X(\partial M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}D(\partial M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}.

Let
X
denote the smooth locus
\tilde{X}(M)_{0}^{\mathrm{sm}}
of the irreducible curve
\tilde{X}(M)_{0}
. It is equipped with regular functions
m(x)
,
l(x)
(Definition
6.5.3
).

We now describe the variation
(\mathcal{V},\nabla,\mathbb{W}_{\bullet},\mathcal{F}^{\bullet})
of mixed Hodge structure over
X
corresponding, via Theorem
7.1.2
, to the Chern-Simons section
C_{\mathbf{M}\!\mathbf{T}}:X\rightarrow H_{\mathbb{Z}}\backslash H_{\mathbb{C}}
(Theorem
6.6.1
), which turns out to be a Tate variation of mixed Hodge structure (cf.
[
Hai94
,
\lx@sectionsign
7]
,
[
HZ87b
]
):
the canonical extension
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
of
\mathcal{V}
is the trivial bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}^{\oplus 3}=\mathcal{O}_{\overline{X}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{3}
. Let
\{e_{0},e_{1},e_{3}\}
be a basis of
\mathbb{C}^{3}
. Define
complex
weight filtration and Hodge filtration on
V=\mathbb{C}^{3}
by:

\displaystyle W_{-4}V=W_{-3}V=\{e_{2}\}

\displaystyle\quad\subset\quad W_{-2}V=W_{-1}V=\{e_{1},e_{2}\}\quad\subset\quad W_{0}V=V

\displaystyle F^{-p}V=

\displaystyle\langle e_{0},\cdots,e_{p}\rangle\quad(p=0,\cdots,2),

and then, after choosing a smooth projective variety
\overline{X}
containing
X
with
\overline{X}\backslash X
being a normal crossing divisor, we put
\overline{\mathscr{F}}^{p}:=F^{p}V\times\overline{X}\subset\overline{\mathcal{V}}
,
\overline{\mathbb{W}}_{l}:=W_{l}V\times\overline{X}\subset\overline{\mathcal{V}}
.

For
x\in X
, we put

(7.2.0.2)

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}w_{0}\\
w_{1}\\
w_{2}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&v(x)&(2\pi i)^{2}cs(x)+u(x)v(x)\\
&2\pi i&(2\pi i)\,2u(x)\\
&&(2\pi i)^{2}\\
\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}e_{0}\\
e_{1}\\
e_{2}\end{array}\right)

where
cs(x)=cs(A_{x})
for an
1
-form
A_{x}\in\mathcal{A}^{1}(M,\mathfrak{sl}_{2,\mathbb{C}})
as defined in Theorem
6.4.2
: in particular, it depends on
u(x)
and
v(x)
. We define
\mathbb{V}_{x}
to be the
\mathbb{Q}
-subspace of
V
spanned by
\{w_{0},w_{1},w_{2}\}
endowed with the filtration by
\mathbb{Q}
-subspaces:

W_{-4}\mathbb{V}_{x}=W_{-3}\mathbb{V}_{x}=\{w_{2}\}\,\subset\,W_{-2}\mathbb{V}_{x}=W_{-1}\mathbb{V}_{x}=\{w_{1},w_{2}\}\,\subset\,W_{0}\mathbb{V}_{x}=\mathbb{V}_{x}

This
\mathbb{Q}
-filtration
W_{\bullet}\mathbb{V}_{x}
is well-defined, i.e. independent of the choice of the branches
u(x)
,
v(x)
, and is a
\mathbb{Q}
-structure of the
\mathbb{C}
-filtration
W_{\bullet}V
. So, for each
x\in X
, we obtain a mixed
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge structure
(\mathbb{V}_{x},\mathscr{F}^{\bullet}_{x},\mathbb{W}_{\bullet,x}:=W_{\bullet}\mathbb{V}_{x})
whose weight-graded quotients are canonically identified with
\mathbb{Z}(0)
,
\mathbb{Z}(1)
,
\mathbb{Z}(2)
.

Now, we show that this is a Tate variation of mixed
\mathbb{Q}
-Hodge structure.
Let
\Lambda(x)
denote the
3\times 3
-matrix in (
7.2.0.2
). Then, the holomorphicity of
f
implies that the connection
\nabla
on the trivial bundle
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{C}^{3}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}
for which the
row
vectors of
\Lambda(x)
are horizontal sections is holomorphic (so that each
\mathbb{W}_{l}
is flat): we have

\nabla s=ds-s\omega,\quad(s\in(\mathcal{O}_{X})^{\oplus 3}),

for

\omega:=\Lambda(x)^{-1}\cdot d\Lambda(x).

Secondly, the fact that
f
defines a flat section implies that
(\mathcal{V},\nabla,\mathscr{F}^{\bullet})
satisfies the Griffiths transversality. To see this, we only need to verify that
\nabla e_{0}\in\mathscr{F}^{-1}=\mathcal{O}_{X}e_{0}+\mathcal{O}_{X}e_{1}
, as the other cases of the transversality are trivial. This amounts to vanishing of the
(1,3)
-entry of
\omega
. It is pleasing to see that this entry is the connection
1
-form
dc-adb
:

\text{if }\Lambda(x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&a(x)&c(x)\\
&2\pi i&2\pi i\,b(x)\\
&&(2\pi i)^{2}\\
\end{array}\right),\text{ then }\omega:=\Lambda(x)^{-1}\cdot d\Lambda(x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&da(x)&dc(x)-a(x)db(x)\\
&0&\,db(x)\\
&&0\\
\end{array}\right).

For
a(x)=v(x)
,
b(x)=2u(x)
,
c(x)=(2\pi i)^{2}cs(x)+u(x)v(x)
, the vanishing of
dc(x)-a(x)db(x)
is
[
KK93
, 2.7]
:

(2\pi i)^{2}d\,cs(x)=v(x)du(x)-u(x)dv(x).

Note that
\omega
is a nilpotent matrix in
1
-forms which have simple poles at each point at infinity as
m(x),l(x)\in\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times}
and have values
0
or
\infty
at any point at infinity in
\overline{X}\backslash X
.
Since the variation of pure Hodge structure
\mathrm{Gr}^{W}_{k}\mathbb{V}
is constant, the variation of mixed Hodge structure
(\mathcal{V},\nabla,\mathbb{W}_{\bullet},\mathscr{F}^{\bullet})
over
X
is unipotent
[
HZ87b
, (1.3)]
, and clearly good
[
HZ87b
, (1.9)]
.
Hence it is a Tate variation of mixed Hodge structure.

7.3  Computation of the limit mixed Hodge structure of CS VMHS at an ideal point

Definition 7.3.1
.

Let
\overline{X}
be the smooth projective completion of the smooth irreducible affine curve
X
, the smooth locus
\tilde{X}(M)_{0}^{\mathrm{sm}}
of the canonical curve component.

An
ideal point
of
X
is any point in
\overline{X}\backslash X
which is a zero or pole of either of
m(x)
and
l(x)
regarded as rational functions on
\overline{X}
.

For a path
\gamma:[0,1]\rightarrow X(\mathbb{C})
, the parallel transport of
V=\mathcal{V}_{\gamma(0)}
along
\gamma
is the multiplication from the right on
V=\mathbb{C}^{3}=\oplus_{i}\mathbb{C}e_{i}
by

T(\gamma)=1+\int_{\gamma}\omega+\int_{\gamma}\omega\omega+\cdots\quad\in\mathrm{GL}_{3}(\mathbb{C})

where the integrals are the Chen’s iterated integral of
\omega
over
\gamma
(cf.
[
Hai87
, Lem.2.5]
,
[
HZ87b
]
).
As
\omega^{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&2dv(x)du(x)\\
&0&0\\
&&0\\
\end{array}\right)
and
\omega^{3}=0
, we have

(7.3.1.1)

T(\gamma)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&\int_{\gamma}dv(x)&2\int_{\gamma}dv(x)du(x)\\
&1&2\int_{\gamma}du(x)\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)

(again the integrals in the entries are iterated integrals).

Let
P\in X(\mathbb{C})
and
\gamma
a simple loop in
X(\mathbb{C})
which goes around
P
once counterclockwise, starting and ending at a point close enough to
P
such that it does not turn around any zero or pole of
l(x)
and
m(x)
, possibly except for
P
.
Then, as
u(x)=\log m(x)
,
v(x)=\log l(x)
, we have

(7.3.1.2)

\int_{\gamma}\frac{dl(x)}{l(x)}\frac{dm(x)}{m(x)}=\frac{(2\pi i)^{2}}{2}b_{1}a_{1},

for

(7.3.1.3)

b_{1}=v_{P}(l(x)),\ a_{1}=v_{P}(m(x))\ \in\mathbb{Z},

where
v_{P}=\mathrm{val}_{P}
is the valuation on
\mathbb{C}(X)
attached to
P
(
[
Hor14a
, 3.2]
).

Now, we compute the limit mixed Hodge structure of the Chern-Simons variation of mixed Hodge structure
(\mathcal{V},\nabla,\mathbb{V}_{\bullet},\mathscr{F}^{\bullet})
at a tangent vector at some ideal point
P
of
X(\mathbb{C})
.
Let
\overline{X}
be a smooth projective completion of
X
. Choose a local coordinate
z
on
\overline{X}
around
P
and a branch of
\log z
. For a simple closed curve
\gamma
around
P
(as above), let
T:=T(\gamma)
denote the parallel transport(or local monodromy) of
\omega
along
\gamma
: by (
7.3.1.1
) and (
7.3.1.2
), we have

T=I+\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&2\pi i\,b_{1}&(2\pi i)^{2}b_{1}a_{1}\\
&0&4\pi i\,a_{1}\\
&&0\end{array}\right)

and

(7.3.1.4)

\displaystyle N:=

\displaystyle-\log T=-(T-I)+\frac{(T-1)^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&-2\pi i\,b_{1}&0\\
&0&-4\pi i\,a_{1}\\
&&0\end{array}\right)

Then, the canonical extension
\overline{\mathcal{V}}
is endowed with a new connection
\nabla^{c}=\nabla+\frac{N}{2\pi i}\frac{dz}{z}
whose horizontal sections are
\lambda(z)\exp(tN)
, where
\lambda(z)
are horizontal sections of
\mathcal{V}
. The underlying
\mathbb{Q}
-space of the limit mixed Hodge structure on the fiber at the tangent vector
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
is spanned by the row vectors of the limit
\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\Lambda(z)\exp(tN)
(
t:=\frac{\log z}{2\pi i}
) (cf.
[
Hai94
,
\lx@sectionsign
7]
). We have

(7.3.1.8)

\displaystyle\Lambda(z)\exp(tN)=

\displaystyle\Lambda(z)(1+tN)\quad(N^{2}=0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&v(z)&(2\pi i)^{2}cs(z)+u(z)v(z)\\
&2\pi i&4\pi i\,u(z)\\
&&(2\pi i)^{2}\\
\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&-b_{1}\log z&0\\
&1&-2a_{1}\log z\\
&&1\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&v(z)-b_{1}\log z&(2\pi i)^{2}cs(z)+(u(z)-2a_{1}\log z)v(z)\\
&2\pi i&4\pi i\,(u(z)-a_{1}\log z)\\
&&(2\pi i)^{2}\\
\end{array}\right)

(Here, as variable we use
z
instead of
x
).

The
(1,2)
and
(2,3)
entries of this matrix have well-defined limits (as
z\rightarrow 0
) which depend only on
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
(mod
2\pi i
or
(2\pi i)^{2}
), rather than on the particular choice of a local uniformizer
z
. That is, in terms of the factorizations
l(z)=z^{b_{1}}\tilde{l}(z)
,
m(z)=z^{a_{1}}\tilde{m}(z)
, these entries are given by

(7.3.1.18)

b_{2}:=\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}(v(z)-b_{1}\log z)=\log\tilde{l}(0),\ a_{2}:=\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}(u(z)-a_{1}\log z)=\log\tilde{m}(0);

there constants are taken modulo
\mathbb{Z}(1)

(
a_{2}
and
b_{2}
are also the logarithms of the leading coefficients of
m(z)
and
l(z)
, respectively.)

The
(1,3)
-entry is equal to the sum
s_{1}(z)+s_{2}(z)
of two terms, where

(7.3.1.19)

\ s_{2}(z)=(u(z)-a_{1}\log z)(v(z)-b_{1}\log z)

and

\displaystyle s_{1}(z)=

\displaystyle(2\pi i)^{2}cs(z)+b_{1}\log z(u(z)-a_{1}\log z)-a_{1}v(z)\log z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(2\pi i)^{2}cs(z)+b_{1}\log z\log\tilde{m}(z)-a_{1}(b_{1}\log z+\log\tilde{l}(z))\log z

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle(2\pi i)^{2}cs(z)+(a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2})\log z-a_{1}b_{1}(\log z)^{2}\,+O(z\log z)

Hence, if we put

(7.3.1.20)

s(z):=(2\pi i)^{2}cs(z)+(a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2})\log z-a_{1}b_{1}(\log z)^{2}

since
\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}s_{2}(z)=a_{2}b_{2}
, the limt
\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}s(z)
must exist by the existence of limit mixed Hodge structure.
In other words, we have

(7.3.1.21)

\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\Lambda(z)\exp(tN)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&b_{2}&a_{2}b_{2}+\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}s(z)\\
&2\pi i&4\pi i\cdot a_{2}\\
&&(2\pi i)^{2}\end{array}\right).

7.4  Results from three-dimensional topology: Culler-Shalen theory, A-polynomial

Recall the constants
a_{1},b_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
(
7.3.1.3
),
a_{2},b_{2}\in\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}(1)
(
7.3.1.18
).

Theorem 7.4.1
.

If we choose a holomorphic coordinate
z
around
P
such that
\tilde{m}(0)=1
, we have
a_{2},b_{2}\in\mathbb{Q}(1)
.

The proof of this theorem uses results in three-dimensional topology, mainly from
[
CS83
]
,
[
CS83
]
and
[
CCGLS94
]
, which give us non-trivial properties of the constants
a_{i}
,
b_{i}

(i=1,2)
through their topological interpretations. For basic definitions in three-manifold theory appearing in the proof which are not covered in the above sources, we refer to
[
Kap01
, Ch.1]
.

An
essential
surface in an oriented three-manifold
M
with (possibly empty) boundary is an incompressible,
\partial
-incompressible, non-
\partial
-parallel, orientable surface which are neither a sphere nor a disk. Suppose that
\partial M
is a torus. A
slope
is an isotopy class of unoriented simple closed curves on
\partial M
; thus, each slope corresponds to a pair
\pm\gamma
of primitive elements of
H_{1}(\partial M,\mathbb{Z})
(i.e. an element that cannot be represented as a non-trivial multiple of some other element).
For an incompressible surface
S
in
M
, if
S\cap\partial M\neq\emptyset
, this intersection
\partial S
is a family of parallel simple closed curves. If an ordered basis
\{\mu,\nu\}
of
H_{1}(\partial M,\mathbb{Z})
has been chosen and the slope of
\partial S
is
\pm(r\mu-s\nu)
, we call the ratio
-\frac{r}{s}\in\mathbb{Q}\cup\{\infty\}
, the
boundary slope
of the surface.

The first topological meaning of the two constants
a_{1}
,
b_{1}
is that
-\frac{b_{1}}{a_{1}}
is the boundary slope of some essential surface inside
M
. More precisely, let
X_{0}
be any irreducible curve inside the character variety
X(M)

and
\tilde{X}_{0}
the smooth projective birational model of
X_{0}
. Then for any point
P
of
\tilde{X}_{0}
where the birational map to
X_{0}
is not regular,
17
17

17

Such point is often called
ideal point
in the Culler-Shalen theory. But in this work, we reserve this terminology for our definition
7.3.1
which has more strict meaning and is consistent with the definition used in
[
CCGLS94
, 2.2]
.

Culler and Shalen
[
CS83
, 2.3.1]
associate an essential surface
S
with non-empty boundary. Their method is by constructing a
\pi_{1}(M)
-action on the
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(F)
-tree, where
F
is the completion at
w
of a finite extension of the function field
\mathbb{C}(X_{0})
over which a universal(or tautological) representation
\tilde{\rho}
(
6.2.0.2
) exists, and
w
is a valuation lying above the valuation
v
of
\mathbb{C}(X_{0})=\mathbb{C}(\tilde{X}_{0})
corresponding to the ideal point
P
.
Now, let us assume that
X
is the smooth part of the canonical curve component (
7.2.0.1
). Then it is easy to see that the theory of Culler-Shalen works for
X
.
In this case, if
P
is an ideal point of
X
in the sense of Definition
7.3.1
, one has the additional information about the boundary slope of the essential surface
S
obtained by the Culler-Shalen method, namely that its boundary slope is
-\frac{v(l)}{v(m)}
, where
m
,
l\in F^{\times}
are the eigenvalues of
\mu
and
\nu
(
[
CCGLS94
, 3.1]
). Indeed, it follows from the construction that if the image of
\partial S
in
H_{1}(\partial M,\mathbb{Z})
is
\pm k\gamma
in for a primitive
\gamma\in H_{1}(\partial M,\mathbb{Z})\cong\pi_{1}(\partial M)
, the eigenvalues of
\tilde{\rho}(\gamma)
are finite valued at
P
(
[
CS83
, 2.2.1]
). But, since one of
v(m)
,
v(l)
is non-zero (
v
is the valuation at an ideal point), there exists a
unique
, up to sign, primitive
\gamma=a\mu+b\nu\in\pi_{1}(\partial M)
such that the eigenvalues
(m^{a}l^{b})^{\pm 1}
of
\tilde{\rho}(\gamma)
are finite valued at
P
, i.e. such that
v(m^{a}l^{b})=0
. As we always have
v(m^{-v(l)}l^{v(m)})=0
, by uniqueness and primitiveness, the pair
(-v(l),v(m))
of integers must be proportional to
(a,b)
, i.e. we have

(7.4.1.1)

-v(l)\mu+v(m)\nu=\pm d\gamma.

where
d:=\mathrm{gcd}(v(l),v(m))
.

Another important property of
S
is that the image
\mathrm{Im}(\pi_{1}(S)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(M))
lies in an “edge group”, i.e. the stabilizer group of a directed edge in the
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(F)
-tree (
[
CS83
, 2.3.1]
).
We recall the notion of ‘‘tree-action eigenvalue’’
18
18

18

This is our terminology coined to distinguish it from another use of the terminology of eigenvalue, i.e. that of
\tilde{\rho}
.

of an element of an edge group. Let
v:F^{\times}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathcal{O}
,
\pi
,
k:=\mathcal{O}/(\pi)
be respectively the discrete valuation, the valuation ring, a uniformizer, and the residue field. A point of the
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(F)
-tree
\mathbb{T}
is a similarity class of lattices in
F^{2}
and a (directed) edge is defined by lattices
L,L^{\prime}
of
F^{2}
with
\pi L\subsetneq L^{\prime}\subsetneq L
, in which case
[L]
,
[L^{\prime}]
are respectively the initial and terminal vertices of the directed edge
e
. When an element
A\in\mathrm{SL}_{2}(F)
fixes a directed edge
e
of
\mathbb{T}
corresponding to lattices
L,L^{\prime}
of
F^{2}
with
\pi L\subsetneq L^{\prime}\subsetneq L
,
A
leaves the line
L^{\prime}/\pi L\subset L/\pi L(\simeq k^{2})
stable, so
A\ (\text{mod}\pi)
acts on
L^{\prime}/\pi L(\simeq k)
by some
\lambda\in k^{\times}
. We call
\lambda
the
tree-action eigenvalue
of
A
attached to the fixed directed edge
e
.
It follows from the definition of the
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(F)
-tree action and the identity
\tilde{\rho}(\gamma^{d})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}l^{v(m)}m^{-v(l)}&\ast\\
0&l^{-v(m)}m^{v(l)}\end{array}\right)^{\pm 1}
(
7.4.1.1
) that the tree-action eigenvalue
\lambda
of
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\partial M)
satisfies
\lambda^{d}=(\frac{l^{v(m)}}{m^{v(l)}}|_{z=0})^{\pm 1}
. Then, up to taking inverse,

\lambda^{d}=\frac{l^{v(m)}}{m^{v(l)}}|_{z=0}=\frac{l(z)^{v_{z}(m(z))}}{m(z)^{v_{z}(l(z))}}|_{z=0}=\exp(a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2}).

Now, we use the non-trivial fact
[
CCGLS94
,
\lx@sectionsign
5.7, Corollary]
that the tree-action eigenvalue
\lambda
is a root of unity whose order divides
n(S)
, the greatest common divisor of the numbers of the boundary components of the various connected components of
S
.
19
19

19

We also remark that by definition, the exponential
\exp(a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2})
equals the tame symbol at
P
of the element of
\{l,m\}\in K_{2}(\mathbb{C}(M))
, which is also equal to the local monodromy around
P
of the pull-back line bundle
(l,m)^{\ast}\mathcal{L}
of the Heisenberg line bundle, cf.
[
Hai94
, 6.3]
,
[
Ram89
,
\lx@sectionsign
4]
.

Therefore, we deduce that when we choose a holomorphic coordinate
z
around
P
such that
\tilde{m}(0)=1
(i.e.
a_{2}\in(2\pi i)\mathbb{Z}
(
7.3.1.18
)), we have

b_{2}\equiv(a_{1}b_{2}-a_{2}b_{1})/a_{1}\equiv 0\text{ mod }\mathbb{Q}(1)

which completes the proof of Theorem
7.4.1
.

When
a_{1}=1
, such choice of the parameter
z
amounts to a choice of a tangent vector
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
; in general, there are several (i.e. as many as
|a_{1}|
) choices for a tangent vector
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
giving
a_{2}\in(2\pi i)\mathbb{Z}
. We also note in passing that when
a_{1}=1
,
a_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}(1)
(the second can be always achieved by a suitable choice of
z
),
one has
b_{2}\equiv a_{1}b_{2}-a_{2}b_{1}\equiv 0\text{ mod }\mathbb{Z}(1)
if
n(S)=1
, e.g. the essential surface
S
is connected and has a single boundary component.

To proceed, we now propose a conjecture.

Conjecture 7.4.2
.

There exists an ideal point
P
on
X=\tilde{X}(M)_{0}^{\mathrm{sm}}
such that for some local uniformizer
z
with
\tilde{m}(0)=1
, the limit of the
(1,3)
-entry in (
7.3.1.8
) belongs to
\mathbb{Q}\cdot(2\pi i)^{2}
:

\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\,(2\pi i)^{2}cs(z)+(u(z)-2a_{1}\log z)v(z)\,\in\,\mathbb{Q}\cdot(2\pi i)^{2},

equivalently (
7.3.1.20
)

\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}s(z)=\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\,(2\pi i)^{2}cs(z)+(a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2})\log z-a_{1}b_{1}(\log z)^{2}\,\in\,\mathbb{Q}\cdot(2\pi i)^{2}.

In other words, there exists an ideal point such that for a suitable choice of parameter
z
, the limit
\mathbb{Q}
-mixed Hodge structure of the CS-VMHS at the tangent vector
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
is split:
\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}(1)\oplus\mathbb{Q}(2)
.

Compare this conjecture with the corresponding statement for the polylogarithm variation of mixed Hodge structure
[
Hai94
, Thm.7.2]
.

In Appendix A, we confirm this conjecture for the “figure-eight knot” complement (labelled by
4_{1}
), in which case it turns out that all four ideal points
P
satisfy the conjecture.
20
20

20

We do not know and have any expectation about whether this will be always the case. In the polylogarithm case, this is not true (the limit MHS at the tangent vecotr
-\partial/\partial z
at
1
is not split,
[
Hai94
, Thm.7.2]
). In that respect, we remark that the figure-eight knot is rather special, i.e. is an arithmetic hyperbolic manifold.

We remark that we also verified the conjecture for the knot complement
5_{1}
(for at least one ideal point), and believe it to be always true, in view of some evident patterns appearing in these computations.

For the discussion in the next subsection of the global monodromy of the Chern-Simons VMHS, we say that a slope (class in
H_{1}(\partial M,\mathbb{Z})
of an unoriented simple closed curve) is
strongly detected
if it is the boundary slope of an essential surface which is obtained by the method of Culler-Shalen
[
CS83
]
(using the action of
\pi_{1}(M)
on the
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
-tree constructed from an ideal point of the character variety).
When the ideal point lies on an irreducible component
X^{\prime}
of
X(M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
(or
\tilde{X}(M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
), we will say that the essential surface is
strongly detected by
X^{\prime}
.

Culler and Shalen
[
CS84
, Thm.1]
showed that for a large class of three-manifolds including all knot-complements in
S^{3}
,
M
contains a separating connected essential surface with non-empty boundary; being separated, this is not a Seifert surface and thus its slope is not the longitude. In fact, it follows from their method of proof of
loc. cit.
that this essential surface is strongly detected by the
canonical component

X(M)_{0}
of
X(M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
. We prove a stronger fact:
21
21

21

Often, in literatures people assert a weaker statement where one does not require the slopes to be detected by the geometric component
X_{0}(M)
(not just
X(M)
), referring to
[
CS84
, Thm.3]
. We believe that its proof has a gap.

Proposition 7.4.3
.

Suppose that
M
is a (complete) hyperbolic three-manifold (of finite volume) with a single cusp.
Then, there exist at least two distinct boundary slopes
strongly detected by
X=\tilde{X}(M)_{0}^{\mathrm{sm}}
.

Our proof uses the A-polynomial theory developed in
[
CCGLS94
]
, which we review here.
The
A
-polynomial
A_{M}
of a compact three-manifold
M
with single torus boundary is a polynomial with
\mathbb{Q}
-coefficients in two variables
m
,
l
, the eigenvalue functions of a chosen ordered basis
\mu
,
\nu
of
H_{1}(\partial M,\mathbb{Z})
, constructed as follows:
Let
r:X(M)\rightarrow X(\partial M)
be the natural map between the
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
-character varieties (over
\mathbb{Q}
) induced by the restriction
\pi_{1}(M)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(\partial M)
, and recall the subvariety of
D(\partial M)
consisting of diagonal representations; let
t_{\partial M}:D(\partial M)\rightarrow X(\partial M)
denote the embedding. Let
X^{\prime}(M)
be the union of the irreducible components
Z^{\prime}
of
X(M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
such that the Zariski closure
\overline{r(Z^{\prime})}
of the image
r(Z^{\prime})
is
1
-dimensional:

\textstyle{\tilde{X}(M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{M}}
\textstyle{X(M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{\supset}
\textstyle{Z^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subset}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}(M)}
\textstyle{D(\partial M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t_{\partial M}}
\textstyle{X(\partial M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\supset}
\textstyle{r(Z^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subset}
\textstyle{\overline{r(Z^{\prime})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\subset}

Then, we define a
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
-subvariety (reduced closed subscheme) of
D(\partial M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
by

D_{M}:=\bigcup_{Z^{\prime}\subset X^{\prime}(M)}Z\quad\subset\ D(\partial M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}

where the union is over the irreducible components
Z^{\prime}
of
X^{\prime}(M)
, and for such
Z^{\prime}
,
Z:=t_{\partial M}^{-1}(\overline{r(Z^{\prime})})
, the inverse image of the Zariski closure of
r(Z^{\prime})
. The closure of
D_{M}
in
\mathbb{A}^{2}
is a plane algebraic curve, thus is defined by a single polynomial, which is by definition the
A
-polynomial
A_{M}=A_{M,\mathcal{B}}
of
M
. Up to a constant multiple,
A_{M}
is defined over
\mathbb{Z}
(ibid. Prop.2.3). We consider
A_{M}\in\mathbb{Q}[l,m]
as being defined up to multiplication by an element in
l^{\mathbb{Z}}m^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

If
Y
is an irreducible component of
D_{M}
, we write
\bar{Y}

for the completion of
Y
in
\mathbb{P}^{2}_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
and
\tilde{Y}
for the smooth projective model of
\bar{Y}
.
In
ibid.
, an ideal point is defined to be any point in
\tilde{Y}
for some irreducible component of
Y
of
D_{M}
which corresponds to
\bar{Y}\backslash Y
. As the ratonal map
\tilde{Y}\rightarrow Y\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}
is
(l,m)
, an ideal point of
Y
is a point on
\tilde{Y}
where one of the rational functions
l(z)
,
m(z)
on
\tilde{Y}
has zero or pole. For any irreducible component
Y^{\prime}
of
X^{\prime}(M)
,
Y:=t_{\partial M}^{-1}(\overline{r(Y^{\prime})})
is also the Zariski closure of the image in
D(\partial M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
of
t_{M}^{-1}(Y^{\prime})\subset\tilde{X}(M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
, hence for each ideal point
P
on
Y
in this sense of
ibid.
, there exists an ideal point of
t_{M}^{-1}(Y^{\prime})
in our sense of Definition
7.3.1
whose associated valuation extends that attached to
P
. It follows that the essential surfaces associated with them by Culler-Shalen theory are equal.

Proof.

(of Proposition
7.4.3
)
For
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
, let
I_{\gamma}:X(M)\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
be the regular function defined by the character at
\gamma
:
\chi\mapsto\chi(\gamma)
. If
Y^{\prime}
is an irreducible component of
X(M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
which contains the character of a discrete faithful representation
\rho_{0}
, then
Y^{\prime}
is 1-dimensional and for any non-trivial
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(\partial M)
, the function
I_{\gamma}
is non-constant on
Y^{\prime}
. Indeed, according to
[
CS84
, Prop.2:2nd assertion]
, if
V\subset X(M)_{0}
is the subvariety defined by
I_{\gamma}^{2}=4
, any irreducible component of
V
passing through
\chi(\rho_{0})
is a single point.

We first show that there exists at least one boundary slope strongly detected by
X:=\tilde{X}(M)_{0}^{\mathrm{sm}}
. If
Y_{0}
is the smooth projective birational model of the canonical component
X(M)_{0}
, the rational function
I_{\mu}
on
Y_{0}
must have a pole, say
P_{0}
(then, the birational map from
Y_{0}
to
X(M)_{0}
is not regular at
P_{0}
, either). Hence, we can find an ideal point (in the sense of Definition
7.3.1
) for the augmented canonical component
\tilde{X}(M)_{0}=X(M)_{0}\times_{X(\partial M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}}D(\partial M)_{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
, and by Culler-Shalen method
[
CS83
]
, there exists an essential surface with non-empty boundary.

Let
Y_{0}:=t_{\partial M}^{-1}(\overline{r(Y_{0}^{\prime})})
for the canonical component
Y_{0}^{\prime}=X(M)_{0}
of
X^{\prime}(M)
and
A_{M}^{0}\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}[l,m]
the defining polynomial of the closure of
Y_{0}
in
\mathbb{A}^{2}
. According to
[
CCGLS94
, Thm. 3.4]
, the set of the slopes of the sides of the Newton polytope of
A_{M}
equals the set of the boundary slopes of the essential surfaces strongly detected by some ideal point of
A_{M}
. In fact, the proof of this theorem works just fine for
A_{M}^{0}
, since Proposition 3.3 of
ibid.
is true for any polynomial in
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}[l,m]
.
Hence, we have to show that the Newton polygon of
A_{M}^{0}
has at least two distinct sides. If the Newton slope is a line segment, then there exist
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
and
p(t)\in\mathbb{Q}[t]
such that
A_{M}^{0}(l,m)=p(l^{a}m^{b})
up to
l^{\mathbb{Z}}m^{\mathbb{Z}}
. It follows that the canonical component
Y_{0}^{\prime}
is the curve defined by the equation
I_{\gamma}=\alpha
, where
\gamma=b\mu+a\nu
and
\alpha\in\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
is a zero of
p(t)
. But this contradicts the already mentioned fact that
the trace function
I_{\gamma}
is non-constant on
Y_{0}^{\prime}
for any non-trivial peripheral element
\gamma
.
∎

7.5  Local and global monodromy of CS VMHS

Let
\sigma
be a simple loop around a point
P
on
X(\mathbb{C})
with a base point
Q
. Suppose
\sigma=\gamma\sigma_{\epsilon}\gamma^{-1}
, where
\sigma_{\epsilon}
is a small loop around
P
with a base point
R
as used in the computation of local monodromy (
7.3.1.2
), and
\gamma
is a path from
R
to
Q
.
Then, we have the following formula for the iterated integral
\int_{\gamma}dv(x)du(x)
,
[
Hor14b
, formula before Lem.1.5]
(cf.
[
MT08
, 2.5]
):

(7.5.0.1)

\displaystyle\int_{\sigma}\frac{dl(x)}{l(x)}\frac{dm(x)}{m(x)}=

\displaystyle 2\pi i\left[t(P)-u(P;Q)+\pi ib_{1}a_{1}\right]

where

t(P):=a_{1}b_{2}-a_{2}b_{1},\quad u(P;Q):=\log(\frac{l^{a_{1}}}{m^{b_{1}}}(Q))

(
a_{2}=a_{2}(P)=\log\tilde{m}(P)
,
b_{2}=b_{2}(P)=\log\tilde{l}(P)
, (
7.3.1.18
)).
Thus, we see that

Lemma 7.5.1
.

For the loop
\sigma
as above, around
P
and based at
Q
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the monodromy (
7.3.1.1
) around
\sigma^{n}
equals

T(\sigma^{n})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&n\cdot 2\pi i\,b_{1}&v(P,Q;n)\\
&1&n\cdot 4\pi i\,a_{1}\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right),

where
v(P,Q;n):=n\cdot 4\pi i[t(P)-u(P;Q)]+n^{2}(2\pi i)^{2}a_{1}b_{1}
.

(2) For two points
P
,
P^{\prime}
, let
\sigma
(resp.
\sigma^{\prime}
) be the loop around
P
(resp.
P^{\prime}
) and based at
Q
defined as above. Then, we have

T(\sigma^{b_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma^{\prime-b_{1}}\sigma^{-b_{1}^{\prime}}\sigma^{\prime b_{1}})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&(2\pi i)^{2}2b_{1}b_{1}^{\prime}(a_{1}b_{1}^{\prime}-a_{1}^{\prime}b_{1})\\
&1&0\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right).

Proof.

The first statement is immediate from (
7.3.1.1
), (
7.5.0.1
).
From (1), for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\displaystyle\,T(\sigma^{k}\sigma^{\prime l})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&k\cdot 2\pi i\,b_{1}&v(P,Q;k)\\
&1&k\cdot 4\pi i\,a_{1}\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&l\cdot 2\pi i\,b_{1}^{\prime}&v(P^{\prime},Q;l)\\
&1&l\cdot 4\pi i\,a_{1}^{\prime}\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&\ast\\
&1&4\pi i\cdot(ka_{1}+la_{1}^{\prime})\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right),

where
\ast=4\pi i\cdot\left[k(t(P)-u(P;Q))+l(t(P^{\prime})-u(P^{\prime};Q))\right]+(2\pi i)^{2}\cdot(k^{2}a_{1}b_{1}+l^{2}a_{1}^{\prime}b_{1}^{\prime}+2kla_{1}^{\prime}b_{1})
. This implies (2).
∎

For the next proposition, we use the notion of “base point at infinity’ or “tangential base point” for fundamental groups of smooth complex algebraic curves and monodromy of their representations, cf.
[
Del89
,
\lx@sectionsign
15]
.

Proposition 7.5.2
.

For any single cusped hyperbolic three-manifold
M
for which Conjecture
7.4.2
holds, there exist an ideal point
P
and a tangential base point
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
such that the monodromy at
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
of the Chern-Simons variation of mixed Hodge structure (Subsection
7.2
) contains (unipotent) elements
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
of the following form:

T_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&2\pi i\cdot b&(2\pi i)^{2}\cdot c\\
&1&\ast\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right),\quad T_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&(2\pi i)^{2}\cdot c^{\prime}\\
&1&\ast\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right),

where
b,c,c^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
bc^{\prime}\neq 0
.

Proof.

Choose an ideal point
P
on
X
satisfying the assumption of Conjecture
7.4.2
. We may further choose a rational function
z
on
X
which becomes a local uniformizer at
P
whose tangent vector
\frac{d}{dz}
satisfies the assumption of Conjecture
7.4.2
.
Let
\sigma_{P}
be a small simple closed loop around
P
which leaves and returns to
P
in the direction
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
:
\sigma_{P}=\gamma\sigma_{\epsilon}\gamma^{-1}
as introduced above with
R
converging to
Q=P
.
In the notation from Subsection
7.3
, we have
a_{1}(P)b_{1}(P)\neq 0
(by the very definition of ideal point), hence
T(\sigma_{P})
is of the form
T_{1}
by Lemma
7.5.1
(take the limit as
Q\rightarrow P
).

By Proposition
7.4.3
, there exists another ideal point
P^{\prime}
with different slope
-b_{1}^{\prime}\mu+a_{1}^{\prime}\nu
(with the obvious notation). Suppose first that
b_{1}^{\prime}=0
; then,
a_{1}^{\prime}\neq 0
and

T(\sigma_{P^{\prime}})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&4\pi i[t(P^{\prime})-u(P^{\prime};P)]\\
&1&4\pi ia_{1}^{\prime}\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right).

If
t(P^{\prime})-u(P^{\prime};P)\neq 0
, we are done. Otherwise, we have

\displaystyle T(\sigma_{P}\sigma_{P^{\prime}}^{k}\sigma_{P}^{-1})=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&2\pi i\cdot b_{1}&(2\pi i)^{2}\cdot 2(a_{1}+2ka_{1}^{\prime})b_{1}\\
&1&\ast\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&-2\pi i\cdot b_{1}&(2\pi i)^{2}\cdot a_{1}b_{1}\\
&1&-4\pi i\cdot a_{1}\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&(2\pi i)^{2}\cdot 2ka_{1}^{\prime}b_{1}\\
&1&\ast\\
&&1\\
\end{array}\right)

which proves the claim.
If
b_{1}^{\prime}\neq 0
, the claim follows immediately from Lemma
7.5.1
, (2).
∎

For a survey of the theory of mixed Hodge structures on the pro-unipotent fundamental groups of complex algebraic varieties, we refer to
[
Hai87
]
,
[
Hai94
]
and the references therein.

Theorem 7.5.3
.

Keep previous notations and assumptions, including Conjecture
7.4.2
.

The Chern-Simons variation of mixed Hodge structure is a quotient of the variation of mixed Hodge structure on the completed (w.r.t. the augmentation ideal) path torsor at the tangential base point
\overrightarrow{v}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial z}

\{\mathbb{Q}[P_{\overrightarrow{v},x}X]^{\wedge}\}_{x\in X}\rightarrow X.

Compare this theorem with the corresponding statement for the polylogarithm variation of mixed Hodge structure
[
Hai94
, Thm.11.3]
whose strategy of proof we follow.

Proof.

We have to show that the fiber over
\overrightarrow{v}
of the (completed) path torsor is, as a MHS, a quotient of
\mathbb{Q}\pi_{1}(X,\overrightarrow{v})^{\wedge}
.
By Conjecture
7.4.2
, we have
\mathbb{V}_{\overrightarrow{v}}=\oplus_{i=0}^{2}\mathbb{Q}(i)e_{i}
, which thus contains a copy of
\mathbb{Q}(0)
(spanned by the vector
e_{0}
).
Since the parallel transport
\mathbb{V}_{\overrightarrow{v}}\otimes\mathbb{Q}[P_{\overrightarrow{v},z}X]^{\wedge}\rightarrow\mathbb{V}_{x}
is a morphism of mixed Hodge structure
[
Hai94
, 11.2]
,
we need to show that the first rows of the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space of upper-triangular matrices which is generated by the monodromy at
\overrightarrow{v}
span
\mathbb{Q}\oplus\mathbb{Q}(1)\oplus\mathbb{Q}(2)
.
This follows from Proposition
7.5.2
.
∎

Appendix A
Confirmation of Conj.
7.4.2
for knot complements
4_{1}

We confirm Conjecture
7.4.2
for the (four) ideal points of
4_{1}
.
Along the way, we also verify that the limt
\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}s(z)
exists.

Here, in the case of the figure-eight knot complement
4_{1}
, we confirm Conjecture
7.4.2
, by direct computation based on an explicit description of the Neumann-Zagier potential on the deformation curve as provided in
[
Hik07
]
.

The equation for the deformation curve of the complete hyperbolic structure obtained by Hikami (using the so-called saddle point method) is

(A.0.0.1)

1=y^{2}x\cdot x^{-1}(1-x^{-1})(1-y^{2}x)\quad(ibid.\,(4.5));

here, we used the letter
y
instead of the original letter
m
, which is the eigenvalue of the holonomy (
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
-representation) of the meridian, since we reserved
m
and
l
for (the derivatives of) the holonomies of the meridian and the longitude (so
m=y^{2}
).
The eigenvalue of the holonomy of the longitude (which we denote by
\eta
so that
l=\eta^{2}
) is given by (ibid. (4.6)):

(A.0.0.2)

\eta=\frac{1}{y^{2}x(y^{2}x-1)}

The potential function is given by (ibid. (4.4)):

\displaystyle V’(x,y)=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(1/xy^{2})-4\log y\log(xy)

(A.0.0.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)+\mathrm{Li}(xy^{2})+\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}+\frac{1}{2}\log^{2}(-xy^{2})-4\log y\log(xy)

(A.0.0.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x^{-1})-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(1/xy^{2})-\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}-\frac{1}{2}\log^{2}(-x)-4\log y\log(xy),

where
\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{z^{2}}{k^{2}}=-\int_{0}^{z}\frac{\log(1-t)}{t}dt
is the dilogarithm function (we take the principal branch of it with the branch cut being the real axis
[1,\infty)
; this is defined by the principal branch of the logarithm whose branch cut is the negative real axis). Here, for the (second) equality we used the transformation properties of dilogarithm function

(A.0.0.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z^{-1})=

\displaystyle-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)-\frac{\pi^{2}}{6}-\frac{1}{2}\log^{2}(-z)\quad(z\notin[0,\infty)).

Because of the choice of variables
[
Hik07
, (3.14)]
, the potential function
V(x,y)
used by Hikami differs from the Neumann-Zagier potential
\Phi
by

\Phi=4V.

The usual gluing equation of
4_{1}
is expressed as:
zw(1-z)(1-w)=1
, in terms of which we have
[
NZ85
, (63),(64)]

(A.0.0.6)

m=w(1-z),\quad l=[z(1-z)]^{-2}

(we took the inverse of the Neumann-Zagier’s
l
to match it with (
A.0.0.2
)). The relation to the equation (
A.0.0.1
) is given by:

(A.0.0.7)

z=1-y^{2}x,\quad w=x^{-1}.

Namely, the variety defined by (
A.0.0.1
) (which is the
\mathrm{SL}_{2}
-character variety) is a double covering of the deformation variety:
zw(1-z)(1-w)=1
(which can be regarded as a
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}
-character variety). From now, we work with the latter deformation variety.
There are four ideal points:
(z,w)=(0,\infty)
,
(1,\infty)
,
(\infty,0)
,
(\infty,1)
.

Case 1
. The ideal point
P
is
(z,w)=(1,\infty)
(which corresponds to
(x,y)=(0,0)
)
We make change of variables
(z,w)=(1-s,-t^{-1})
: so
P
becomes
(s,t)=(0,0)
, and the new equation is

(A.0.0.8)

st^{-1}(1-s)(1+t^{-1})=-1,

(i.e.
s[(1-s)(1+t)]=-t^{2}
), in terms of which we have

l=[s(1-s)]^{-2},\quad m=-st^{-1}.

The relation to the equation (
A.0.0.1
) is
s=y^{2}x=mx
,
t=-x
(
A.0.0.7
). At
P
,
t
is a uniformizer and we have
s=s(t)=-t^{2}f(t)
with
f(t)\in 1+\mathbb{C}\{\!\!\{t\}\!\!\}
. So, we see that

\displaystyle m=tf(t),\qquad

\displaystyle\quad l=t^{-4}f(t)^{-2}(1-t^{2}f(t))^{-2},

\displaystyle\boxed{a_{1}=1,\,a_{2}=\log(1)=0},

\displaystyle\quad\boxed{b_{1}=-4,\,b_{2}=\log(1)=0}.

and
(a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2})\log t-a_{1}b_{1}(\log t)^{2}=4\log^{2}t
.

Now, since
x=x(t)=-t
,
y^{2}=st^{-1}=tf(t)
(so that
x,xy^{2}\in O(t)
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\log^{2}(-xy^{2})-4\log y\log(xy)=\frac{1}{2}\log^{2}(-xy^{2})-\log(y^{2})\log(x^{2}y^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(2\log t+\log f(t))^{2}-(\log t+\log(f(t)))(3\log t+\log(f(t)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\log^{2}t-2\log t\log f(t)-\frac{1}{2}\log^{2}f(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\log^{2}t+r(t)

where
r(t)\in t\mathbb{C}\{\!\!\{t\}\!\!\}
(i.e.
\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}r(t)=0
) since
f(0)=1
, and
we have

\displaystyle(2\pi i)^{2}cs(t)+(a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2})\log t-a_{1}b_{1}(\log t)^{2}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\Phi(s(t),t)+4\log^{2}t

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle 4V(x(t),y(t))+4\log^{2}t+O(t)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\pi^{2}+O(t)

which confirms the claim.

Case 2
.
P=(z,w)=(0,\infty)
. We change the variables
(z,w)
in
[
NZ85
, (63)]
into
(s,t^{-1})
:
(s,t)=(0,0)
and the new equation becomes

(A.0.0.9)

st^{-1}(1-s)(1-t^{-1})=1,

and

l=[s(1-s)]^{-2},\quad m=(1-s)t^{-1}.

and
s=1-y^{2}x=1-mx
,
t=w^{-1}=x
, by (
A.0.0.7
).
Again, the uniformizer is
t
and we have
s=s(t)=-t^{2}f(t)
with
f(t)\in 1+\mathbb{C}\{\!\!\{t\}\!\!\}
. So,

\displaystyle m=t^{-1}(1+t^{2}f(t)),\qquad

\displaystyle\quad l=t^{-4}f(t)^{-2}(1+t^{2}f(t))^{-2},

\displaystyle a_{1}=-1,\,a_{2}=\log(1)=0,

\displaystyle\quad b_{1}=-4,\,b_{2}=\log(1)=0.

so that
(a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2})\log t-a_{1}b_{1}(\log t)^{2}=-4\log^{2}t
.

Since
x=x(t)=t
,
y^{2}=(1-s)t^{-1}=t^{-1}g(t)
with
g(t)=1+t^{2}f(t)
, we have
x(t)\in O(t)
but
xy^{2}=g(t)
. From

\displaystyle-4\log y\log(xy)=-\log(y^{2})\log(x^{2}y^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(-\log t+\log g(t))(\log t+\log g(t))=\log^{2}t-\log^{2}g(t),

we see that

\displaystyle(2\pi i)^{2}cs(t)+(a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2})\log t-a_{1}b_{1}(\log t)^{2}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\Phi(s(t),t)-4\log^{2}t

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle 4V(x(t),y(t))-4\log^{2}t

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle 4\left(\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(1/xy^{2})-4\log y\log(xy)\right)-4\log^{2}t

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle-4\mathrm{Li}_{2}(g(t)^{-1})+O(t),

confirming the claim.
In particular, we see that the
(1,3)
-entry of (
7.3.1.8
) is

-4\lim_{z\rightarrow 1}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)=-4\zeta(2)=-\frac{2\pi^{2}}{3}.

Case 3
. The ideal point
P
is
(z,w)=(\infty,0)
.
We make change of variables
(z,w)=(-s^{-1},t)
: so
P
is
(s,t)=(0,0)
, and the new equation is

(A.0.0.10)

ts^{-1}(1-t)(1+s^{-1})=1,

and

l=[z(1-z)]^{-2}=s^{4}(1+s)^{-2},\quad m=(1-z)w=s^{-1}(1+s)t.

The relation to the equation (
A.0.0.1
) is
1+s^{-1}=y^{2}x
,
t=x^{-1}
, by (
A.0.0.7
). At
P=(0,0)
,
s
is a uniformizer and we have
t=t(s)=s^{2}f(s)
with
f(s)\in 1+\mathbb{C}\{\!\!\{s\}\!\!\}
. So, we see that

\displaystyle m=s(1+s)f(s),\qquad

\displaystyle\quad l=s^{4}(1+s)^{-2},

\displaystyle a_{1}=1,\,a_{2}=\log(1)=0,

\displaystyle\quad b_{1}=4,\,b_{2}=\log(1)=0.

and
(a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2})\log s-a_{1}b_{1}(\log s)^{2}=-4\log^{2}s
.

Since
x=x(s)=s^{-2}f(s)^{-1}
,
y^{2}=(1+s)s^{-1}t=sg(s)
with
g(t)=(1+s)f(s)
, we have

x^{-1},(xy^{2})^{-1}\in O(s)
. If
h(s)=g(s)f(s)^{-2}
(
x^{2}y^{2}=s^{-3}h(s)
),

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\log^{2}(-x)-4\log y\log(xy)=-\frac{1}{2}\log^{2}(-x)-\log(y^{2})\log(x^{2}y^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}(2\log s+\log(-f(s)))^{2}-(\log s+\log g(s))(-3\log s+\log h(s))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log^{2}s-\log(f(s)^{2}h(s)/g(s)^{3})\log s-\frac{1}{2}\log^{2}(-f(s))-\log g(s)\log h(s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log^{2}s-\frac{1}{2}\log^{2}(-1)+r(s)

where
\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}r(s)=0
. Thus, by (
A.0.0.4
), we have

\displaystyle(2\pi i)^{2}cs(s)+(a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2})\log t-a_{1}b_{1}(\log s)^{2}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\Phi(s,t(s))-4\log^{2}s

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle 4V(x(t),y(t))-4\log^{2}s+O(s)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{3}\pi^{2}-2\log^{2}(-1)+O(s)

which confirms the claim.
Note that
-\frac{2}{3}\pi^{2}-2\log^{2}(-1)\equiv-\frac{8}{3}\pi^{2}\mod 4(2\pi i)^{2}
.

Case 4
. The ideal point
P
is
(z,w)=(\infty,1)
.
We make change of variables
(z,w)=(-s^{-1},1-t)
: so
P
is
(s,t)=(0,0)
, and the new equation is

(A.0.0.11)

ts^{-1}(1-t)(1+s^{-1})=1,

and

l=[z(1-z)]^{-2}=s^{4}(1+s)^{-2},\quad m=(1-z)w=s^{-1}(1+s)(1-t).

The relation to the equation (
A.0.0.1
) is
1+s^{-1}=y^{2}x
,
1-t=x^{-1}
, by (
A.0.0.7
). At
P=(0,0)
,
s
is a uniformizer and we have
t=t(s)=s^{2}f(s)
with
f(s)\in 1+\mathbb{C}\{\!\!\{s\}\!\!\}
. So, we see that

\displaystyle m=s^{-1}(1+s)(1-s^{2}f(s)),

\displaystyle\qquad l=s^{4}(1+s)^{-2},

\displaystyle a_{1}=-1,\,a_{2}=\log(1)=0,

\displaystyle\quad b_{1}=4,\,b_{2}=\log(1)=0.

and
(a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2})\log s-a_{1}b_{1}(\log s)^{2}=4\log^{2}s
.

Since
x=x(s)=(1-s^{2}f(s))^{-1}
,
y^{2}=(1+s)s^{-1}(1-t)=s^{-1}g(s)
with
g(s)=(1+s)(1-s^{2}f(s))
, we have

(xy^{2})^{-1}\in O(s)
, but
x(0)=1
. If
h(s)=(1-s^{2}f(s))^{-2}g(s)=(1+s)(1-s^{2}f(s))^{-1}
, from

\displaystyle-4\log y\log(xy)=-\log(y^{2})\log(x^{2}y^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(-\log s+\log g(s))(-\log s+\log h(s))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\log^{2}s+\log g(s)h(s)\log s-\log g(s)\log h(s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\log^{2}s+r(s)

where
\lim_{s\rightarrow 0}r(s)=0
,
we see that

\displaystyle(2\pi i)^{2}cs(s)+(a_{2}b_{1}-a_{1}b_{2})\log s-a_{1}b_{1}(\log s)^{2}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\Phi(s,t(s))+4\log^{2}s

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle 4V(x(t),y(t))+4\log^{2}s

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle 4\left(\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x)-\mathrm{Li}_{2}(1/xy^{2})-4\log y\log(xy)\right)+4\log^{2}s

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle 4\mathrm{Li}_{2}(x(s))+O(s),

confirming the claim.
In particular, we see that the
(1,3)
-entry of (
7.3.1.8
) is

4\lim_{z\rightarrow 1}\mathrm{Li}_{2}(z)=4\zeta(2)=\frac{2\pi^{2}}{3}.
